Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Root out the retailer-side subliminals you are already sending
Use when: Store environment, packaging, site ambient design, or reviewing what shoppers actually perceive versus what you hope they perceive.
- Audit the low-salience sensory inputs your customer already receives: music and its volume, ambient scent, lighting, color fields, micro-copy, small print on packaging. Lindstrom treats these as effectively subliminal because shoppers are too overstimulated to register them.
- Decide intent for each input. Retailers have played jazz/Latino tapes with sub-audible spend and anti-shoplifting messages (a vendor quoted 15% higher sales and 58% lower theft, per Lindstrom) — but you should decide deliberately whether and how you use unconscious cues.
- Fix small-print failures: important information (returns, ingredients, shipping thresholds, allergens) hidden in clutter is a trust risk. Pull it into a consciously readable spot.
- Use ambient design to carry brand association rather than to manipulate: signature scent, sound, lighting, and color reinforce memory and store ownership, as with Abercrombie's pervasive fragrance.
- Re-audit after redesigns — overstimulation means each new element competes for scarce conscious attention.
Caveat: The 15%/58% figures come from one tape vendor's claim, not independent ecommerce evidence.
Caveat: Subliminal self-improvement tapes and stealth messaging raise consumer-protection and ethical issues; check applicable disclosure rules before deploying.
Caveat: Subliminal perception has a long history of fabricated studies (Vicary's Coke/popcorn experiment was admitted a con), so treat strong claims with caution.

Tags: brand, merchandising, packaging, retail, customer-research, ethics
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use the origin story on packaging as a price-positioning lever
Use when: Packaging copy, PDP provenance sections, or origin labeling for premium fragrance, food, apparel, or consumer goods.
- Audit the cities/countries currently printed on your packaging and product page. Lindstrom describes a struggling perfume brand that listed manufacturing cities like Düsseldorf and Oberkochen instead of Paris, London, New York, or Rome.
- Replace functional or unglamorous origin references with cities that match the product's aspirational positioning — the perfume brand already had offices in Paris, London, New York, and Rome, so listing those was truthful and sales reportedly rose almost immediately.
- Keep the change honest: only claim cities where you actually have a relevant presence, facility, or design/atelier relationship.
- Apply the same principle to other origin-adjacent labels: certified origins, design-atelier cities, ingredient sourcing regions. Match them to what the target buyer associates with quality in your category.
- Test the packaging copy change in a small market before rolling out, measuring price acceptance and not just click-through.
Caveat: One perfume case, reported by Lindstrom himself as his own consulting engagement, is an anecdote not a controlled test.
Caveat: Substituting decorative origins for actual manufacturing origin can breach labeling regulations in food, cosmetics, and textiles.
Caveat: Branding-based origin gains are fragile if the product itself does not live up to the implied quality.

Tags: brand, packaging, positioning, pricing, product-page, country-of-origin
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Diagnose the anti-ad effect: strong warnings can strengthen craving
Use when: PDP warnings, ad creative for restricted or vice categories, or assessing whether your compliance creative is backfiring.
- Recognize Von Restorff-style failure in tobacco research: showing gruesome antismoking ads that picture people smoking convivially activated smokers' craving regions in Lindstrom's fMRI work — the respondents barely registered the fat imagery and instead latched onto the social scene.
- Before launching creative that depicts the behavior you want to discourage, test whether the depiction itself is the stronger cue.
- Where you must disclose risks (alcohol, gambling, nicotine, supplements, high-caffeine products), separate the warning from the appetitive scene in time and layout so the warning does not sit inside a positive context image.
- Consider showing the behavior-free version of the scene — the ritual and social setting without the consumable in hand.
- Measure craving or urge-to-buy, not warning comprehension alone, when evaluating restricted-category creative.
Caveat: Craving response measured in nicotine-dependent smokers does not directly transfer to alcohol, gambling, or food categories.
Caveat: This is not a reason to omit legally required warnings; it is a reason to test layout and context.
Caveat: Operators in restricted categories should confirm current advertising and health-warning rules with counsel — they change.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, compliance, advertising, customer-research, restricted-categories
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use race and sport sponsorship as an association shortcut
Use when: Sponsorship evaluation, creator or event partnerships, or selecting affiliate partners for a performance or lifestyle brand.
- List the qualities your buyer wants and the qualities the property already carries (Lindstrom's example: NASCAR and F1 conveyed masculinity, sex, power, speed, innovation, coolness). Match on overlap, not on audience size alone.
- Choose properties where your brand can appear prominently for the whole duration — Ferrari-red cars and matching team jumpsuits ensured Marlboro's color was continuously on screen even though logos were banned.
- Extend the sponsorship into owned assets: on-site activation, co-branded apparel, event content, and post-race email, so the borrowed association reaches shoppers who never watched the event.
- For online stores, an equivalent is partnering with creators or communities that already embody the target identity and having them show the product in-use throughout their content series.
- Measure association transfer with brand-attribute surveys before and after, not just reach or sales in the sponsorship window.
Caveat: Lindstrom's claim that associations are transferred 'in only seconds' is his interpretation of his fMRI data, not a validated sponsorship KPI.
Caveat: Sponsorship effect is slow and expensive — the tobacco sponsorship portfolio built over decades.
Caveat: Check category restrictions and local sponsorship rules; tobacco and gambling sponsorships face different regulations than apparel or food.

Tags: brand, acquisition, creative-strategy, partnerships, measurement, association-transfer
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Recall works image-first — lead your listing and ad memory with imagery
Use when: Product image ordering, ad creative hierarchy, search result presentation, or packaging with dominant imagery.
- Lindstrom cites a 2005 fMRI study in which subjects could identify the category of an image (celebrity, place, object) before they could recall its name — suggesting visual memories form and surface before verbal ones.
- Put the strongest visual identity first: on collection pages, list cards, and search results, ensure the product image does the identifying work before the title.
- For ad creative, let the visual carry brand recognition; use the text line to add specificity or offer, not to introduce the brand from scratch.
- On packaging and PDP hero images, ensure the product silhouette, color, and scene are distinctive enough to be recognized at thumbnail scale, since shoppers often see the image before they read anything.
- Test recall with logo-free or name-free image variants to confirm the visual alone is memorable.
Caveat: The fMRI study used celebrity faces, famous places, and everyday objects with 90 images; it is not an ecommerce listing experiment.
Caveat: 'Image before name' recall does not mean images convert better than copy, especially for complex or considered purchases.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, product-page, merchandising, packaging, measurement
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use repetition and familiar context to build store memory
Use when: Ongoing creative production, retention marketing, brand guidelines, or planning annual campaign consistency.
- Pick a small set of stable visual, auditory, or textual cues — one color field, one scene type, one phrase — and repeat them across every impression instead of redesigning each campaign.
- Keep the context stable too: the same scene type or ritual moment attached to the product every time builds the association. Lindstrom describes how tobacco brands association-built across years of consistent imagery.
- Link the cue to a positive emotional state at the moment of use or purchase so the cue later retrieves that state, as with Abercrombie's scent recreating the in-store dopamine rush.
- When bringing cues into retention channels (email, subscriptions), use cue-only messages periodically so the association is refreshed rather than only product-and-price reminders.
- Watch for cue fixation creeping in — reassess after long stretches whether the cue still carries the intended meaning for newer customers.
Caveat: Building stable associations takes sustained investment; expect a slower return than offer-led campaigns.
Caveat: Scent-based cues carry the strongest anecdotal memory claims in this material but are hard to reproduce online — treat digital equivalents cautiously.

Tags: brand, retention, creative-strategy, email, merchandising, brand-guidelines
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Build product rituals to make a brand 'sticky'
Use when: Merchandising or PDP/landing-page work where you want to convert a one-off purchase into a repeat-buy habit, or when a commoditized product needs differentiation.
- Identify an existing, unowned ritual your customers already perform with the product (how they eat, serve, mix, apply, set up or share it). Lindstrom's example: Irish pubs poured Magners cider over ice to compensate for missing fridges, which cooled the drink, cut the sweetness, and improved taste; the brand became 'Magners on Ice' without changing the recipe.
- Codify that ritual into explicit serving or usage instructions: a specific glass, quantity of ice, order of steps, or timing. Make it visible on packaging and PDP — not hidden in a blog post.
- Reinforce it with the product itself where possible (Lindstrom cites Oreo's partnership with the 'Got Milk?' campaign to make dunking-in-milk the sanctioned way to eat the cookie).
- Give the ritual a name or shorthand customers can say to each other and to staff, so it spreads without paid media.
- Apply it in ecommerce specifically: put the ritual on the PDP as a short numbered 'how to use it right' block, repeat it in confirmation/email and packaging inserts, and use it as a repeat-purchase trigger in replenishment flows.
Caveat: Rituals can trap a brand: Lindstrom notes Bacardi wanted customers to mix its rum with other mixers but the entrenched Cuba Libre (rum + Coke + lime) ritual proved hard to shake.
Caveat: A ritual that depends on a channel or venue (bartender behaviour, a physical store clerk) needs a digital equivalent if you sell online.

Tags: brand, retention, product-page, differentiation, customer-habits, replenishment
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Match 'smashable' brand assets to strong emotional response
Use when: Auditing brand assets before investing in creative, packaging redesign, or acquisition ads, and deciding where to concentrate design spend.
- Run the smash test on your own assets: mentally cut the product or page into a hundred pieces. Can a single fragment — a colour, a shape, a bottle silhouette, a UI element — still be identified as yours? Lindstrom notes Guinness drops are recognisably Guinness, a Harley wheel as unmistakable as the bike, a scrap of Ferrari metal identifiable by its signature red.
- Contrast with a weak case: he argues a de-logoed Ralph Lauren shirt is indistinguishable from any other premium shirt, which is why the logo carries the price premium. In a Chinese factory he observed workers attaching the cotton logo as the final step, as the sole difference between branded and unbranded garments.
- Prioritise assets that survive fragmentation — colour, material, silhouette, sound, typography — over anything that only works as a complete composition.
- Note that a fragment can be recognisable without a visible logo: Lindstrom points out an iPod's front face carries no Apple logo yet is unmistakable.
- Apply to ecommerce: derive your colour, shape and sound tokens from these assets and use them consistently on PDP imagery, packaging, unboxing inserts and ad creative so a scrolling thumbnail is still identifiable.
Caveat: This is Lindstrom's own fMRI study and his interpretation; treat the brand-religion brain equivalence as his finding, not established consensus.
Caveat: Distinctive assets can harden into a ceiling — see the Bacardi and Mallomars cases, where a single owned trademark made extension harder.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, packaging, design, differentiation, advertising
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use scarcity schedules and countdown signalling
Use when: Merchandising or launch planning for a product where you control availability windows, drops, or seasonal stock.
- Establish a real constraint (heat-sensitive chocolate in Mallomars' case, production halted April to September) or an explicit seasonal window, and communicate it plainly.
- Build anticipation around the return rather than apologising for the absence: Lindstrom describes Mallomar buyers awaiting the reappearance on shelves like nature lovers awaiting the swallows of Capistrano.
- Support the return with a ritual accessory — for Mallomars he lists sanctioned eating methods (marshmallow first, graham cracker last, or reverse, or whole) that give customers a way to participate.
- Apply to ecommerce with a waitlist, a launch email, a dated drop calendar, and a PDP that shows a return date rather than a simple 'out of stock'.
- Judge whether the constraint is credible: if customers perceive it as pure manipulation the anticipation effect can reverse.
Caveat: Lindstrom explicitly cites an article suggesting Nabisco manufactured Mallomars' seasonal ritual artificially by limiting availability — this is presented as a claim, not a proven strategy.
Caveat: Do not fabricate a scarcity reason; a discovered fake constraint damages trust.

Tags: merchandising, scarcity, launch-planning, retention, product-page
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Engineer 'brand belonging' signals in commerce touchpoints
Use when: Retention and community-facing decisions: loyalty programmes, packaging, post-purchase email, and on-site merchandising for a brand with repeat buyers.
- Identify an observable marker your customers share — Lindstrom's examples are spotting the same running sneaker brand on the treadmill, or waving at another Toyota Scion driver.
- Turn that marker into something visible and shareable in the ecommerce flow: a packaging insert, a member badge, a user-generated content gallery, or a labelled 'our customers also…' shelf.
- Group customers around a shared mission the way Weight Watchers meetings, Super Bowl crowds and concert audiences do — Lindstrom notes these bring together people with a similar mission.
- Use belonging to justify repeat purchase: reinforce that the buyer is part of something rather than only receiving a product.
- Pair belonging with the other pillars — vision, symbols and ritual — rather than running community activity in isolation.
Caveat: Lindstrom's evidence is observational and analogical; the belonging–purchase link is his interpretation, not a measured ecommerce effect size.
Caveat: Belonging signals must be authentic to the base; inventing an in-group marker customers don't recognise reads as marketing theatre.

Tags: brand, retention, community, loyalty, packaging, customer-research
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Cultivate mystery rather than exhaustive explanation
Use when: Copywriting and PDP decisions about how much ingredient, formula, or technology detail to reveal.
- Give the key ingredient or method a proprietary, unexplained name rather than a full technical explanation. Lindstrom cites Chanel's Sublimage copy about 'Planifolia PFA' and 'Polyfactioning of Active Ingredients' — which he mocks as 'crazy talk', yet notes it functions as mystery.
- Test the pull: he describes a Unilever employee writing 'Contains the X9 Factor' on an Asian shampoo label by accident; after the phrase was later dropped, customer letters complained the shampoo no longer worked and hair had lost lustre. He concludes customers were indignant that the mysterious factor had been removed.
- Keep at least one question unanswered on the PDP — a named technology, a numbered process, a guarded formula — instead of explaining everything to completion.
- Pair mystery with a verifiable core benefit so the unexplained element supports rather than replaces the claim.
- Watch the line: mystery works when the customer believes something is there; it backfires if the copy reads as empty jargon to a sceptical buyer.
Caveat: The X9 Factor story is anecdotal and the customer letters are described as claimed complaints that hair lost lustre because of the removal — treat it as a striking illustration, not evidence.
Caveat: Chanel's copy is quoted by Lindstrom as deliberately nonsensical; do not copy impenetrable jargon as a recipe.
Caveat: He also cites cosmetics brands claiming DNA-matched scents, which he calls complete nonsense — mystery is not licence to make false claims.

Tags: brand, product-page, copywriting, positioning, creative-strategy
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Run an us-vs-them comparison frame
Use when: Acquisition creative and positioning work where the category has a dominant incumbent or a clearly defined alternative.
- Name the alternative explicitly and frame the contrast around a customer-recognisable difference, as Lindstrom cites Hertz's 'We're Hertz and they're not' and Apple's 'Mac vs PC' spots.
- Give the comparison a protagonist the customer wants to be — Lindstrom notes the Apple character was played as the cool urban professional and the PC as the bespectacled geek.
- Use the contrast to build in-group membership, then convert belonging into repeat purchase through the community and ritual mechanics.
- Keep the us-vs-them tension focused on a genuine product difference; Lindstrom's point is that the division itself unites buyers and gets them arguing, which does the marketing work.
- Apply on landing pages with a direct comparison table or a positioning line your customers can repeat.
Caveat: An us-vs-them frame invites controversy; Lindstrom presents this as a tradeoff that attracts fans and incites argument, not a cost-free tactic.
Caveat: Naming a competitor in paid creative may carry legal or platform restrictions depending on market — check locally.

Tags: brand, positioning, acquisition, creative-strategy, landing-page, differentiation
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Design for an invitation-only convert funnel
Use when: Launch strategy or VIP-tier design for a new product, membership or loyalty tier where you can control supply of access.
- Open the product to a controlled group first and let existing members invite others, as Gmail did before opening to the general public — Lindstrom notes it had secured an estimated 10 million users before dropping the invitation requirement.
- For a premium tier, make membership something customers must ask for rather than buy openly: he describes tens of thousands calling Amex asking to be placed on the short list for the Centurion Black Card.
- Reward the convert at the moment of joining so acceptance feels like an honour, which he compares to how religions treat converts.
- Translate to ecommerce: a referral-gated early-access list, a waitlist with a numbered position, or an invite-only tier with a clear joining ritual.
- Rehearse the exit: decide in advance the condition for opening access, so scarcity does not become permanent importability.
Caveat: Both cited cases are historic and specific to their category; do not treat Gmail's 10 million figure as a target or benchmark.
Caveat: Gating access trades short-term purchase volume for desirability — only appropriate where the brand can sustain perceived exclusivity.

Tags: brand, acquisition, launch-planning, loyalty, referral, positioning
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Build symbols that work without the logo
Use when: Asset and packaging design decisions, and structuring on-site iconography and navigation.
- Identify the associations you want to evoke and design a symbol that carries them directly. Lindstrom's example: Lance Armstrong's 'Live Strong' yellow wristband, given away free by Nike and later sold by the foundation for around $70 million, made a charitable symbol rather than a product logo.
- Note the scale he cites for owned icon sets — roughly three hundred for Apple and five hundred for Microsoft — as evidence that symbol systems, not a single mark, are the asset.
- Anchor symbols in a meaning cluster rather than a product: Jimmy Buffett's following is built on sunshine, ocean, relaxation and rum drinks with paper umbrellas, which he has extended into restaurants, books and a radio show even without recent hit records.
- Apply in ecommerce by testing whether your key symbols read correctly in isolation on a small mobile thumbnail, an order-confirmation email, and unboxing — without the wordmark.
- Check each symbol for unintended cultural meaning before rolling it out across markets.
Caveat: Lindstrom himself notes elsewhere in the book (referenced as Chapter 4) that the logo is no longer as powerful as companies once believed — symbols here are broader than logos.
Caveat: The Buffett example is an entertainment brand; treat the extension pattern as analogy for product-to-merchandising extension, not a template.

Tags: brand, design, packaging, creative-strategy, differentiation
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use repeated navigation patterns as switching costs
Use when: App, site or subscription decisions about redesigning core flows, navigation, or checkout, where established users have learned your interaction pattern.
- Identify the interactions your customers can perform 'in their sleep' — Lindstrom's iPod example is Music, then Artists, then the track — and treat them as an asset.
- Protect those flows in redesigns: changing a habitual path forces relearning and hands a switching-cost advantage to whoever the customer moves to next.
- Extend the principle to loyalty mechanics and reordering: if a repeat purchase path is muscle memory, do not relocate it behind a new layout for aesthetic reasons.
- When you must change a core flow, stage it — keep the old path visible during transition so early purchasers do not lose their acquired habit.
- Note the flip side: familiar rituals also let an incumbent keep charging for a pattern customers no longer enjoy, so pair habit protection with genuine product improvement.
Caveat: Lindstrom's evidence is anecdotal brand observation, not a measured churn study.
Caveat: Habitual interfaces can also hide friction that new users experience as a barrier — protect core-user paths and test with first-time buyers separately.

Tags: retention, checkout, product-page, customer-habits, conversion-optimization
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use cultural number and symbol luck in market selection
Use when: Pricing, SKU naming, phone numbers, store numbers, product codes and promotion timing for cross-border or culturally specific online stores.
- Map lucky and unlucky numbers per target culture: Lindstrom reports 8 as favourable because it sounds like the Chinese word for wealth, and 4 as the unluckiest because si is close in sound to shi, meaning death. He notes hotels in China (and Asian-owned hotels elsewhere) omit fourth and forty-fourth floors.
- Price with this lens: he cites a Guangzhou plate auction where one bidder paid 54,000 yuan (~$6,750) for APY888, later beaten by 80,000 yuan (~$10,568) for AC6688, and a regional Chinese airline reportedly paying about US$300,000 for an 888-8888 exchange.
- Time launches accordingly — the Beijing Olympics opened at 8:08:08 p.m. on 8/08/08.
- Apply to ecommerce as price endings, SKU and variant numbering, promo codes, and event timestamps in specific markets.
- Check the direction of the effect: Lindstrom cites David Phillips' finding of up to 13% more heart attacks among US residents of Chinese descent on the fourth day of the month, and 27% in California, which he attributes to stress from cultural fear, not luck itself.
Caveat: The heart-attack and car-accident figures (51% rise in London, 32% in Germany on a Friday the 13th) are cited from other researchers and attributed to anxiety, not to actual luck — do not present them as causal proof.
Caveat: Numeric preferences vary by culture and can shift; verify with local customers before making pricing decisions.

Tags: pricing, market-research, cross-border, merchandising, brand, promotion
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Adapt a culturally loaded ritual, don't transplant it
Use when: Localising promotions, seasonal campaigns or holiday merchandising for a market where your brand's home ritual is not native.
- Identify which seasonal or symbolic device your campaign relies on and whether it is culturally specific — Lindstrom describes the advent calendar as specific to Christianity, and Lego was perceived as promoting a religious agenda.
- Fix the mechanics separately from the meaning: the calendar also broke for New Zealand and Australian users who were twenty-four hours ahead and could not open doors on the correct day; that was solved with a per-nationality script, but it did not solve the cultural problem.
- For each market, substitute a ritual the audience already performs, rather than translating the Christian one.
- Test locally before a global rollout: Lindstrom notes the advent calendar was approved because it seemed inexpensive and risk-free, and it was not.
- Apply in ecommerce by localising promo calendars, send-times and seasonal tokens per market instead of shipping one global campaign.
Caveat: This is Lindstrom's account of a Lego project he worked on; no sales figures are given, so treat the outcome as a qualitative lesson.
Caveat: Cultural sensitivity also cuts the other way — a substitute ritual may feel inauthentic if it is borrowed from a culture the brand has no connection to.

Tags: brand, cross-border, promotion, merchandising, campaign-planning
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Recognise collecting and completism as a retention mechanic
Use when: Product-line, packaging-variant and loyalty design where you want customers to build a set, extend a range, or complete a collection.
- Design the range so that no single purchase completes it: variants, series, numbered editions or recurring drops create the ongoing collection ritual.
- Give the collector visibility of what they have and what remains — a checklist, a set progress display, or an account view of owned items.
- Use the completion mechanic in retention messaging to prompt the next purchase, not just the first.
- Note Lindstrom's claim that collecting behaviours often take root in adolescence and that children with social difficulties are more likely to become preoccupied with collecting — the emotional driver is mastery and control, not utility.
- Apply in ecommerce as set-building bundles, missing-piece prompts and a collection view in the account area.
Caveat: The '30 percent of Americans tend to hoard' figure comes from a 1981 New York Times article cited by Lindstrom — it is dated and defined loosely.
Caveat: Hello Kitty's 22,000+ products is presented as an extreme example; do not treat scale of SKU proliferation as a goal.

Tags: merchandising, retention, bundles, loyalty, product-line
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Treat collecting behaviour as a control-seeking response to stress
Use when: Retention and lifecycle messaging decisions, especially during uncertain periods (economic stress, supply disruption, category change).
- Assume repeat purchase is often ritualised behaviour rather than a reconsidered decision — Lindstrom states that buying a product is more often ritualised than a conscious decision, and cites women repeatedly buying antiwrinkle creams they themselves believe are pointless, on a roughly three-month cycle.
- In stressful periods, lead with familiarity and stable, consistent patterns rather than novelty: he argues we seek comfort in familiar products when life feels random.
- Keep the core repeat-purchase path, familiar packaging and consistent naming stable; do not redesign basics during a period when customers are seeking reassurance.
- Reserve novelty for the ritual itself — a new season, drop, or variant — rather than the core product.
- Apply in ecommerce by making reorder one click, keeping the PDP layout stable for loyal SKUs, and using familiar naming in replenishment emails.
Caveat: The antiwrinkle-cream pattern is Lindstrom's observation of consumers, and he describes the creams as ones the buyers themselves say are pointless — this is a commentary on ritual, not evidence that efficacy does not matter.
Caveat: Stability is not the same as never improving the product; it applies to how change is staged, not whether it happens.

Tags: retention, customer-habits, replenishment, lifecycle-marketing, product-page
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## State a clear brand vision and let rivals define the contrast
Use when: Brand strategy refresh, homepage and about-page copy, or aligning merchandising and creative with a single stated mission.
- Write the mission as an unambiguous statement of what the brand is for, not a description of what you sell. Lindstrom cites Apple's mid-1980s line about man being the creator of change and above systems and structures, which he says the company still pursued twenty years later.
- Give the vision a durable frame so it does not need rewording each campaign — his examples are Bang & Olufsen's 'Courage to constantly question the ordinary in search of surprising, long-lasting experiences' and IBM's 'Solutions for a Small Planet'.
- Use the vision to filter merchandising decisions and creative concepts rather than as decoration on an about page.
- Pair the vision with an identifiable contrast to the alternative, the way religions have an Other and brands have a comparison frame.
- Re-check the vision periodically against what the store actually sells and how it promotes; drift is the failure mode.
Caveat: These are Lindstrom's cited corporate statements, not outcomes; he does not link them to sales results.
Caveat: A vision statement does not substitute for product or pricing work — treat it as a filter for consistent decisions.

Tags: brand, positioning, mission, creative-strategy, merchandising
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Audit your brand against the ten religious pillars
Use when: Brand audit or annual planning across the store's positioning, PDP, packaging and retention programmes.
- Score the brand honestly against each of the ten pillars: belonging (do users recognise each other?), clear vision, power over enemies (is there a defined alternative?), sensory appeal (sound, scent, texture, visual identity), storytelling (product and founder stories customers retell), grandeur (flagship presentation, photographic standard), evangelism (how converts join and invite), symbols, mystery, and ritual.
- For each weak pillar, pick one concrete ecommerce change: a ritual block on the PDP for ritual, a referral or invite mechanism for evangelism, a proprietary named ingredient for mystery, a symbol test for symbols.
- Prioritise pillars your category's leading competitor already owns, since they are the ones driving the category's loyalty.
- Re-run the audit after a season and check whether the changes show up in repeat rate and in unprompted customer language, not only in internal alignment.
- Use the pillar framework when coaching a team on why brand and performance creative should tell the same story.
Caveat: The ten pillars are Lindstrom's synthesis of his own interviews plus his brand observations — it is an interpretive framework, not a validated measurement instrument.
Caveat: His fMRI comparison of strong brands to religious icons is his own study with 65 male subjects; he does not report ecommerce outcomes from it.
Caveat: Pillars interact: belonging without ritual or symbols tends to produce passive affinity rather than repeat purchase.

Tags: brand, positioning, audit, retention, creative-strategy, customer-research
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Build Somatic Markers, Not Feature Lists
Use when: Writing PDP copy, category-page merchandising or ad creative and choosing what claim or image should dominate.
- Accept the source's claim that many purchase decisions are fast, emotional shortcuts, not slow rational comparisons — Lindstrom cites Gruppe Nymphenberg finding over 50% of in-store purchasing decisions are made spontaneously at point of sale. Treat that as the presenter's cited study, not a universal law.
- For your product, write out the rational internal monologue a shopper could have (price suspicion, 'generic brand', past disappointment, childhood memories) — the Jif vs. organic peanut butter example shows buyers weighing trust, nostalgia, price and 'you get what you pay for' in seconds.
- Identify which single concept you want linked to your brand (e.g. 'German engineering' for Audi) and repeat that link consistently across packaging, name, price signal and imagery so the association compounds.
- Make associations concrete and sensory where possible: the Andrex puppy links to softness, growing young families and even toilet training without ever being a feature claim.
- Audit whether your current creative produces a distinct, memorable marker or is interchangeable with competitors — Michelin tires all look the same; the Michelin Man, baby and Guides are what create preference.
- Reinforce existing positive links instead of replacing them; the GaJol lozenge campaign worked only because it borrowed an existing nationwide emotion (Pavarotti's canceled concert) and attached the brand to it within hours.
Caveat: Somatic-marker theory is presented as neuroscience-backed but the specific percentages are cited studies, not benchmarks you should promise in a plan.
Caveat: Incongruous or negative associations can also stick permanently — associations are hard to erase once formed.
Caveat: This is a brand-association strategy; it explains preference, not a guaranteed lift in conversion.

Tags: brand, positioning, creative-strategy, customer-research, product-page, somatic-markers
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use Shock and Incongruity to Make Creative Memorable
Use when: Planning acquisition creative, social video, or a launch stunt where the goal is recall rather than immediate purchase.
- Design one unexpected pairing rather than a list of benefits: Blendtec's 'Will it blend?' puts a kitchen blender against iPhones, hockey pucks and garden hoses, and Sony put a urinal seven feet up in a cinema restroom to promote Spider-Man 3.
- Keep the shock tied to the product's core promise so the marker reinforces it — Blendtec's pulverizing residue links back to blending power; the Sony stunt links to an upcoming film.
- Consider humor-based anthropomorphizing where the category is unglamorous — the Lamisil gremlin living under a toenail links the brand to germ-fighting in a memorable way.
- For ecommerce, translate stunts into PDP hero video, UGC-style demonstration content, or a landing-page hook that shows the extreme use case, then link it directly to the buy button.
- Track whether the startling image is being recalled and attributed to your brand; a marker that is memorable but unattributed wastes the spend.
Caveat: Shock that is unrelated to the product is a risk; the source's examples all keep a product link.
Caveat: Stunts can misfire with audiences — the source records the San Francisco 'Got Milk?' cookie-scented bus shelters being scrapped after 36 hours when commuters reported allergic reactions.
Caveat: No conversion benchmark is established; these are illustrative cases, not promised results.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, acquisition, landing-page, advertising
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Fear-Based Positioning and Its Limits
Use when: Deciding whether to lead with a risk-avoidance message on a PDP, ad or email, e.g. gum disease, hair loss, device failure.
- Recognise the mechanism: No More Tears shampoo sells by evoking the fear it prevents; Colgate's ad linked gum disease to heart disease, diabetes and stroke; ADD medicine ads walk parents through a chain ending in jail.
- If you use fear, name the specific consequence and pair it with a clear, credible remedy in the same asset so the shopper isn't left with only anxiety.
- Prefer prevention framing around an existing, personally-felt pain over inventing unlikely catastrophes — Lindstrom notes practically every category plays on fear, directly or indirectly.
- Test fear-led creative against benefit-led or identity-led creative; the source predicts fear-based advertising will increase, which is an opinion, not a finding.
- In regulated categories (health, supplements, finance), verify current claims rules before running a disease-link message.
Caveat: The source explicitly notes that overt or manipulated fear can be counterproductive and ethically fraught.
Caveat: The disease-linkage example is a specific ad claim; do not repeat health claims without substantiation.
Caveat: Fear appeals vary by audience and category; no universal uplift is claimed.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, positioning, product-page, health-claims
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Avoid Overt Religion and Belief-Specific Campaigns
Use when: Planning seasonal campaigns, calendars, gift guides or packaging around holidays with religious roots.
- Before shipping a religiously-coded mechanic, ask which beliefs it encodes — the source's virtual advent calendar was Christian-specific and Lego was quickly perceived as promoting a religious agenda.
- Check timezone and locale mechanics: the Lego campaign needed a per-country script because Australian and New Zealand users couldn't open doors on the right day.
- Prefer implicit or suggestive seasonal cues (winter, cosiness, family, counting down) over explicit religious iconography when you sell to a global audience.
- If you must localise religious content, segment by market and permission rather than shipping one mechanic worldwide.
- Plan the support load for backlash — the source describes thousands of angry emails landing on one person's desk.
Caveat: The source's conclusion is that covert use of religion often doesn't work and can harm a brand; it also notes some companies may use religious elements quietly without admitting it.
Caveat: Regional norms differ — Vodafone's Pope-quote text service and saint-of-the-day messages were planned for Italy, a market with different expectations.

Tags: brand, positioning, creative-strategy, seasonal-campaigns, localization
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Choose Fragrance That Is Congruent With the Product
Use when: Designing unboxing, store or packaging scent experiences, or choosing whether to add a signature scent to a product or mailing.
- Decide the emotional target first, then pick a scent that plausibly belongs to that product — Dr Calvert's lab found image+fragrance ratings dropped sharply when a Dove soap image was paired with scorched canola oil, and rose when a fish-filet image was paired with a lemon note.
- Use the scent as a memory cue for the product not an unrelated mood: smelling doughnut is claimed to make people picture Dunkin' or Krispy Kreme branding.
- For packaging, engineer scent release at the moment of opening — the source cites Nescafé and peanut butter jars designed to release maximum fragrance when the lid comes off (freeze-dried coffee needed reformulating since it barely smells).
- For mailers, consider microencapsulated scent released on opening, as Royal Mail and BRAND sense developed for shampoo flyers.
- Consider the category's own conventions: vanilla is cited as the most popular US scent and appears in Coca-Cola Vanilla variants and other products because of early childhood associations.
Caveat: Scent preferences vary by culture and generation — sandalwood is cited as popular in India, and the source notes birth-cohort differences (fresh-mown grass and horses for pre-1930, synthetic Play-Doh and Sweet Tarts for later cohorts).
Caveat: Scent experiments can backfire on sensitive customers; the 'Got Milk?' bus shelter case had to be removed within 36 hours.
Caveat: The claimed purchase effects come from cited studies (e.g. floral-scented Nike rooms in Dr Hirsch's experiment) rather than verified ecommerce results.

Tags: brand, sensory-branding, packaging, product-page, unboxing, customer-research
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use Ambient Scent to Shift In-Store Behaviour
Use when: Planning a pop-up, showroom or event, or deciding whether to include scent in physical packaging and samples.
- Anchor scent near the entry or point of decision: supermarkets put bakeries by the entrance because baking-bread aroma is claimed to increase spending across bread, butter and jam and to make shoppers abandon lists.
- Match scent to the target behaviour — a barely detectable lemon cleaning scent raised the share of people who wrote cleaning activities on their to-do list from 11% to 36%, and those in the scented room made less mess eating a crumbly cookie.
- Test whether scent changes evaluation metrics, not just mood: in Hirsch's Nike experiment, 84% of volunteers preferred the shoes in the florally scented room and estimated them roughly $10 higher, and a freshly-cut-grass scent in a German home-improvement store led 49% of surveyed customers to say staff seemed more knowledgeable.
- Keep concentrations low: in these studies subjects were unaware of the scent and it appears the effect depends on non-conscious exposure.
- For ecommerce, treat scent as an offline/UGC asset — include scented inserts or samples where a smell can cue the product experience, and capture the moment in video for the PDP.
Caveat: All results are cited studies, not benchmarks; effect sizes vary by setting.
Caveat: Some customers are sensitive or allergic to fragrance, so scent should be optional or absent in shared spaces.
Caveat: The source does not show that ambient scent raises online conversion.

Tags: brand, sensory-branding, customer-research, retail, pop-up, packaging
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Treat Sound as a Branded Asset, Not Decoration
Use when: Choosing background music for a store, pop-up or video, or designing notification, unboxing and jingle sounds.
- Decide the association you want and pick music that carries it: on French accordion days 77% of shoppers bought French wine, while on Bierkeller brass days most bought German wine, and only one of 44 interviewed customers mentioned the music as a reason.
- Build sounds into product moments where they will be repeated — the GE three-chime, the iPod wheel tick, the Kellogg's engineered crunch and Ford's vault-like door latch.
- Engineer sounds at the point of opening or use to signal freshness or quality — the source describes the engineered sound of a coffee jar or Pringles can opening, and the beep of a McDonald's fry machine.
- Use music with a behavioural purpose where relevant: classical music in London Underground locations was reported to be associated with a 33% drop in robberies, 25% fewer assaults on staff and 37% less vandalism (cited figures, not a proven causal ecommerce result).
- For online acquisition, design audio into video ads and ensure the sound carries the brand cue even when heard alone.
Caveat: The wine study is a cited field experiment; treat the multiplier as an example.
Caveat: Signature sounds can become irritating with overexposure — Nokia's default ring tone is the source's central case of a beloved brand damaged by its own ubiquity.
Caveat: Music affects different audiences differently; no universal genre works.

Tags: brand, sensory-branding, creative-strategy, audio, retail, subscription-replenishment
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Weight, Touch and Packaging Shape Quality Perception
Use when: Choosing packaging materials, device weight, garment presentation or sample format so the physical feel matches the intended price tier.
- Test the weight of your packaging against your price point — the source's Bang & Olufsen test gave 100 consumers identical-function remotes with and without a useless aluminium insert; without it, users said 'it's broken' even when told it worked.
- Do not assume lighter is always better: electronics buyers often read small and light as advanced (per Underhill's observation that shoppers touch clothing before buying, and the source's note on slim gadgets), but premium categories can rely on heft.
- Match form to buyer body image where relevant — in the French diet-mayonnaise test, all diet-conscious women chose the bottle with a narrow waist over the genie-bottle shape before tasting either.
- Design packaging to invite touch: open tables, swatch samples, unboxing steps that require handling the product.
- For Duracell-style weight cueing, note the source's caution that adding weight can reduce actual performance while increasing perceived power — decide which signal you're buying.
Caveat: These are experiments with small samples, described by the author; they are illustrative, not universal rules.
Caveat: Weight expectations differ by category — a heavy phone may read as dated to some buyers.
Caveat: The Duracell bullet-battery concept never reached the market, so treat it as an anecdote.

Tags: brand, sensory-branding, packaging, product-page, pricing, customer-research
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use Colour Deliberately to Signal Brand Meaning
Use when: Choosing a brand palette, packaging colour, product variant colours or a category-disrupting visual identity — including for banks, apps or supplements.
- Pick a colour that carries the association you want: the source describes women's heart rates rising 20% on receiving a blue Tiffany box with no logo visible, and pink carrying luxury, sensuality and femininity associations.
- Consider deliberate category incongruity to earn attention: the source advised a bank CEO to paint branches, cars, uniforms and his tie pink; after roughly three months, he reported the highest brand awareness of any bank in the country and marketing costs halved. Treat this as one anecdote, not a formula.
- Remember colour also shapes packaging perception and product variants — Heinz EZ Squirt green ketchup sold over 10 million bottles in its first seven months, the brand's biggest sales spike, and Apple's candy-coloured iMacs were preordered heavily.
- Test attention and recognition effects: cited studies include coloured phone-directory ads holding attention two seconds or more against under one second for black-and-white, Seoul International Color Expo's claim that colour can raise brand recognition up to 80%, and 84.7% of respondents saying colour was more than half their brand-choice criterion.
- In fast-scrolling commerce, treat colour as your thumbnail's primary attention device — the source cites that products have roughly one-twentieth of a second to grab attention.
Caveat: These figures are from cited studies and one anecdote; they are not guarantees and should be verified for your category and market.
Caveat: The pink-bank example includes a period where customers hated the change before it became accepted — expect an adjustment period if you take a disruptive colour route.
Caveat: Colour meanings differ by culture and demography.

Tags: brand, sensory-branding, creative-strategy, packaging, product-page, measurement
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Design Yolk, Surface and Substrate Cues for Food Products
Use when: Sourcing, photographing or commissioning food products where appearance signals quality: eggs, produce, baked goods, sauces.
- Identify the instinctive quality cue in your category: the source notes the egg industry's rule of thumb that more yellow yolks appeal more, likely an evolved signal that kept ancestors from eating bad eggs.
- Decide whether to shift the cue through diet or inputs — egg farmers add colouring to grain to deepen yolk colour, and the source identified a vitamin mix as a less artificial route, producing a spectrum from light to deep yellow.
- Apply the same logic to your imagery and specification: how the product looks on the plate or in the jar should match the cue shoppers already use to judge quality.
- Note that appearance preferences shift over time with no clear cause — white eggs were once associated with cleanliness and hygiene before brown eggs came to be read as more organic and natural.
- Treat this as expectation-setting: egg colour itself is an unreliable quality indicator, so pair the visual cue with honest product information.
Caveat: Preferences such as white vs brown eggs and yolk depth vary by market and change over time.
Caveat: Adding colouring to feed raises ethical questions; the source declined artificial colouring for that reason.
Caveat: Do not claim the colour cue proves nutritional quality.

Tags: brand, product-development, sourcing, packaging, customer-research
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Reinforce Brand Cues Without Redesigning Them
Use when: Planning a reformulation, rebrand, packaging refresh or outsourcing of any sensory element (scent, crunch, sound, colour).
- Document the exact formulation or asset before any change — the source reports Johnson & Johnson and Play-Doh altered their fragrances so much that competitors' scents now smell closer to the original than their own.
- Consider that a reformulated sensory asset may never be recovered: the source says Play-Doh has never fully replicated its original smell, estimating it is only about 80% there.
- Guard against accidental erosion from patchwork changes — small tweaks compound into a different signature.
- When you must reformulate (regulation, supply, cost), test whether existing customers still recognise and prefer it before switching, and treat recognisability as a KPI.
- Apply the same discipline to signature sounds, colours and packaging shapes so a refresh does not silently retire the marker that drives repeat purchase.
Caveat: The Johnson & Johnson and Play-Doh examples are the author's firsthand accounts; exact percentages of scent fidelity are the company's own estimate.
Caveat: Some reformulation is unavoidable for legal or supply reasons — the aim is deliberate management, not freezing the asset forever.

Tags: brand, packaging, subscription-replenishment, product-development, measurement
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Recognise That Overexposure Can Turn a Signature Asset Against You
Use when: Deciding how often to repeat a sonic logo, notification sound, ad jingle or packaging cue across the customer journey.
- Monitor whether your signature cue is becoming irritating rather than reassuring — the source notes Nokia's default ring tone was kept by 20% of subscribers, 41% of UK subscribers could recall or hum it, and yet it drove many users mad through constant overexposure.
- Where a cue is unavoidable, offer control: ring tones, notification sounds and autoplay audio can be muted or varied by the user.
- Track sentiment separately from recognition — high recall is not the same as positive association.
- For ecommerce, apply this to email notification sounds, autoplay video and repeated audio in ads: recognition without goodwill can trigger unsubscribes.
- If a signature asset is tainted, create a new one rather than trying to talk customers out of the irritation.
Caveat: This is a qualitative argument illustrated with Nokia data; the source does not establish a threshold at which a sound becomes net-negative.
Caveat: A loud minority complaining does not prove brand damage — pair sentiment tracking with behavioural data.

Tags: brand, audio, retention, creative-strategy, measurement
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Stack Senses Rather Than Relying on Visuals Alone
Use when: Briefing video ads, PDP media, in-game or streaming placements, or designing sensory touchpoints for a store or pop-up.
- Assume visual saturation reduces the attention any single logo or banner can win — Neuroco's study for 20th Century Fox placed ads in a video game but found visual saturation produced glazed eyes rather than attention.
- Pair images with matching sound or scent rather than adding either alone: Dr Calvert's experiment showed image-plus-fragrance combinations were rated more pleasant than either element on its own when they matched.
- Where possible add a third channel: the source cites a Diet Pepsi magazine ad with sound, taste and pop-up features achieving 100% reader awareness in People, the magazine's first such result.
- Choose only cues that make sense for the product — incongruence drives aversion and poorer recall.
- Measure combined-asset recall and preference against visual-only controls instead of assuming a single logo will carry the message.
Caveat: The 100% awareness figure is a cited, single-issue result and should not be promised.
Caveat: Added sensory elements raise production cost and can be blocked by platforms (autoplay, audio) — check current ad policies before committing.
Caveat: The Fox study's conclusion is that generic visual saturation fails, not that all visual advertising is useless.

Tags: brand, sensory-branding, creative-strategy, acquisition, advertising, measurement
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Pair Signature Sound With Logo to Boost Liking and Recall
Use when: Designing brand assets for a PDP, video ad, unboxing page or app: deciding whether to add a sonic signature, jingle or branded audio cue alongside your logo.
- Have one distinctive branded sound (jingle, sting, sonic logo) and one highly recognizable visual mark, then always present them together rather than separately.
- In video and animated PDP media, time the sound to land exactly on the logo reveal so eyes and ears fire simultaneously; simultaneous pairing outperformed sound-only or image-only in Lindstrom's fMRI study.
- Check congruence: the sound's mood must match the product category and imagery, since the positive results held when sound and vision were combined in a congruent way.
- Test the pairing, not just the assets: measure whether the combined unit is liked and remembered, because a sound that is liked in isolation can still suppress feeling for the logo (the Nokia result).
- For audio-only channels where a logo cannot appear, expect weaker liking and recall than the paired version and compensate with repetition or an additional visual cue elsewhere.
Caveat: Findings come from a small lab fMRI study of four brands; three of four benefited from pairing, so treat it as a directional principle, not a guaranteed lift.
Caveat: This is preference, emotional engagement and memory encoding data, not measured purchase lift.

Tags: brand, sonic-branding, brand-assets, creative-strategy, product-page, video-advertising, memory-encoding
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Audit Your Signature Sound for Negative Somatic Associations
Use when: Reviewing an existing sonic identity, notification sound, hold music or app alert that has been in market for years and may now be associated with interruption.
- Recognize the Nokia pattern: the ring was among the most successfully branded tunes ever, yet when paired with Nokia imagery it produced across-the-board negative emotional response and dragged down preference for the phones themselves.
- Investigate the context of exposure, not the melody. Negative associations came from where the sound was heard: a phone interrupting dinner, a movie, a yoga class, a call from the boss.
- Watch for a tell in your own team: Lindstrom noticed an 'uncomfortable yikes feeling' when his own phone rang. Ask internal staff and customer-facing teams what feeling your sound triggers.
- If the cue is a functional alert (order shipped, someone joined a call, doorbell, message), separate the functional alert from the branded marketing sound so the brand is not attached to interruption.
- Test preference for your imagery with and without your signature sound. If the sound suppresses feeling for the logo, replace or retire it even if recall of the sound is high.
Caveat: Source opinion: Lindstrom judged the Nokia tone was 'killing the brand' based on fMRI and rating data from his own study, not a sales regression.
Caveat: Familiarity alone is not evidence of brand equity; high recall can coexist with negative affect.

Tags: brand, sonic-branding, brand-audit, customer-research, creative-strategy, notification-design
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Multi-Sensory Congruence: Design Beyond Sight
Use when: Planning retail interiors, packaging, unboxing experience, brand film or event activation where you can add a non-visual cue on top of the visual identity.
- Pick the sense that carries the meaning you want, not just the loudest one. Lindstrom's vision of future retail links a lemon scent to sneakers, fresh orange to sporting goods, perfume to a hotel entrance.
- Choose a congruent sensory note rather than a random pleasant one, since the benefit came from the right pairing of sensory inputs.
- Build the cue into the physical product where it can carry association: GSK's Alli used vivid red, blue, yellow and green on white plus a uniquely shaped, portable 'shuttle' pill carrier with a gentle bubbled texture to evoke partnership and a journey together.
- Repeat the cue across channels (packaging, store, site video, ad) so the sensory signal becomes a recognizable asset rather than a one-off stunt.
- Track emotional engagement and recall in tests, not just stated liking, because stated preference diverges from measured response.
Caveat: The Times Square smell-and-sound scenario is Lindstrom's prediction, not a documented result.
Caveat: Scent and sound in ecommerce are largely limited to video, packaging and physical retail; browser-based scent is not a practical channel today.

Tags: brand, sensory-branding, packaging, retail-experience, creative-strategy, brand-assets
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Test Congruent Cue Combinations, Not Single Cues
Use when: Setting up creative testing for ad or PDP assets where you plan to combine a logo, a jingle, a face, a color or a spokesperson.
- Structure the test as three arms minimum: cue A alone, cue B alone, and the combination, so you can see whether the pairing is additive, neutral or destructive.
- Follow Lindstrom's run design: present the sound alone, then the image alone, then both simultaneously, and repeat the sequence to capture stable responses.
- Include a neutral benchmark in each category (a generic ring tone, an unrelated image) because Nokia's ring made subjects prefer unrelated benchmark images over Nokia phones.
- Interpret combination results in context, not in isolation: Microsoft's logo tested less positive than its sound, yet the joint presentation of logo and melody raised preference slightly, so combining can rescue a weaker element.
- Decide the go/no-go on the combined asset's emotional engagement and long-term memory encoding if you have access to those measures, otherwise on recall and preference for the pair.
Caveat: This is fMRI lab methodology; most ecommerce teams can only approximate it with survey-based recall and preference tests.
Caveat: Results varied by brand, so a positive category-level pattern does not guarantee your pairing will be additive.

Tags: brand, experiment-design, creative-strategy, brand-assets, measurement, customer-research
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Treat Stated Buy Intent as Unreliable for Launch Decisions
Use when: Deciding whether to launch a new product, SKU, bundle, landing page concept or ad campaign based on survey, focus group or concept-test feedback.
- Compare stated preference to a harder measure before committing budget. In Lindstrom's TV study, How Clean Is Your House? was a proven hit and The Swan a proven flop, yet the two scored neck and neck on viewers' stated likelihood to watch.
- Collection note: the source text contains a corrupted character (a single letter) where one of the two scores would appear, so only the neck-and-neck conclusion is reliable.
- Look for the divergence pattern: Quizmania was rated the show viewers would least likely watch, yet brain engagement landed between the flop and the hit, and it subsequently performed as the scans predicted in the U.K.
- Use non-verbal or behavioral proxies available to an ecommerce team: actual click-through and add-to-cart for creative variants, or the eye-tracking-style attention data below, rather than stated intent alone.
- Keep the benchmark logic: predict relative rank against known successes and known failures rather than an absolute pass/fail.
Caveat: The brain-engagement method is not available to most merchants; the transferable lesson is skepticism toward stated intent, not the specific prediction method.
Caveat: The 99 percent statistical certainty in the source refers to that study's confidence, not to any product launch prediction you make.

Tags: brand, customer-research, measurement, product-selection, experiment-design, launch-planning
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Vampire Effect: Titillating Creative Steals Attention From the Brand
Use when: Reviewing acquisition creative, hero images, model photography or landing page visuals that are suggestive but may not be carrying your logo, product name or offer.
- Check what the image is actually competing with: MediaAnalyzer's eye-movement test found men spent inordinate time on breasts and largely bypassed brand name, logo and body text.
- Compare recall numbers to decide whether the creative earns its place: brand recall was about 9.8 percent for men viewing sexual ads versus almost 20 percent for nonsexual ads, and about 10.85 percent versus 22.3 percent for women.
- Reduce the problem by physically separating the provocative element from the information: keep logo, product name and price in a zone the eye must land on, or overlay the brand mark on or near the focal point.
- Avoid stacking sexual creative on top of already sexual context, since viewers of Sex and the City had worse ad recall than viewers of a non-erotic sitcom, as the show's own content eclipsed the ads.
- Measure brand recall, not just CTR, when testing this category of creative: a suggestive image may win clicks and still fail to lodge the brand.
Caveat: The Eye-movement and recall studies used print ads and young adult samples, so figures are study-specific and not benchmarks for your store.
Caveat: The conclusion attributed to Parker and Furnham is that sex 'does not sell anything other than itself', which is a strong generalization from their recall study.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, acquisition, advertising-recall, landing-page, measurement
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Choose Controversy Over Titillation When You Want Attention
Use when: Deciding whether a provocative campaign, limited drop or naming choice is worth the risk to brand perception.
- Separate the two levers in your planning: sexual content draws the eyes to itself (Vampire Effect), while controversy generates publicity and news coverage around the brand.
- Consider the Calvin Klein pattern as a case study, not a playbook: the 1980 Brooke Shields campaign was reported to drive jeans sales up to roughly two million pairs a month, and by 1984 the business was worth nearly a billion dollars a year — but the source also notes sales have declined, the brand was sold to Phillips Van Heusen in 2002, and the same provocative ads these shoppers saw also drew boycotts, TV network refusals and a Justice Department investigation.
- Weigh the reversibility of brand damage: pull ads generated fresh publicity (the pattern Lindstrom describes as a deliberate PR maneuver), but some brands suffered — the source says Dolce & Gabbana was hurt after a gang-rape-look ad was pulled under protest.
- Decide the message in advance: an ad needs attention, the name in memory, and consistency. Nike's ad worked because the image pointed to product performance and brand, not to an unrelated sight gag.
- Watch the boundary: controversy may drive short-term customers away while making the ad more memorable, so it is a trade, not a free win.
Caveat: Controversy-driven growth claims are historical case observations, not controlled tests, and the source explicitly says the mechanism is unproven.
Caveat: Boycotts and retailer refusals are real costs the source documents alongside the sales gains.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, acquisition, brand-risk, publicity, positioning
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Keep Sex and Attention-Getters Pointing at the Product
Use when: Judging hero imagery, spokesperson choice, humour or shock value in an ad or PDP before committing spend.
- Ask what the viewer will look at and remember: if it is the joke, the model or the controversy rather than the product, the creative fails the Vampire Effect test.
- Contrast pairs: the source presents a sexualized image of a dominatrix holding a vacuum cleaner, a suggestive toilet-paper image and a suggestive car ad as examples where the relevant connection is weak or absurd, while Nike's product-performance-toned campaign is presented as a case where the provocative image pointed straight at the product.
- For ecommerce applications, adapt this to PDP video and social creative: the eye-catching element should be the product in use, or a demonstration of the claim, rather than a model or gag unrelated to it.
- Check the exception: for fashion categories with a natural link between body and product (lingerie, fragrance, swimwear), the productive path is to keep the presentation on the product and its wearer, as the source argues Calvin Klein does.
- If your only aim is clicks and the product link is weak, expect a short-lived spike and measure post-click quality (add-to-cart, return rate) rather than click volume.
Caveat: The comparison set is Lindstrom's editorial selection of ads he saw, not a controlled experiment.
Caveat: The source's own viewpoint is that most sexual advertising is 'harder to swallow', so treat the sex-versus-controversy distinction as his interpretation.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, acquisition, advertising-recall, product-page, positioning
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Measure Before Believing: Human Responses Don't Match Stated Intent
Use when: Designing post-purchase surveys, NPS follow-ups, ad recall questions or any research meant to predict which product, offer or campaign will actually sell.
- Treat stated intent as a hypothesis to verify, not a finding: how we say we feel about a product cannot truly predict how we behave.
- Design questions around past behaviour (what did you buy, when) — memory of actual past behaviour is more reliable than prediction of future behaviour.
- Where possible, measure emotional response alongside rational response; in Lindstrom's TV study the brain data matched the show's real performance while the questionnaire did not match either show's real performance.
- Keep a benchmark: include a product or campaign with known outcomes when testing new concepts so you can see whether the test can separate winners from losers at all.
- Watch for the novelty effect: Lindstrom's team gave viewers the DVDs to watch and 'sleep on it' before scanning, to reduce the inflated response first-time viewing produces.
Caveat: The source's own confidence is high, but the technique under test in his book is not considered settled science.
Caveat: Some of the source's claims about fMRI methodology exceeding research capability are carried over from the research team, not independently proved.

Tags: brand, measurement, customer-research, experiment-design, positioning, launch-planning
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Run Quarterly NPS and Segment by Historical Value
Use when: Setting up recurring customer feedback for a store and deciding where to spend service, range, price and convenience effort.
- Ask the recommend question on a 0-10 scale repeatedly and consistently so you can track trend rather than one-off scores.
- Do not collapse feedback into the score: read the comments for category-specific issues that word clouds hide, such as store cleanliness, staff attitude, price and range in the source's grocery example.
- Link scores to historical transaction data: the source reports that for one business the most valuable customer segments were measurably more likely to be promoters, and that senior store managers whose locations scored well on service were more likely to exceed profit plans.
- Use the feedback for budget decisions: the source describes how the NPS result redirected investment away from long-underfunded service areas toward store-level improvements.
- Set an internal threshold for action and follow the score over time so you can tell whether a change moved the needle, rather than chasing a single survey cycle.
Caveat: The source reports some of these findings as its own consulting analysis rather than as independently published results.
Caveat: NPS is a public-domain measure with contested academic support, so treat it as one input alongside behavioural metrics.

Tags: brand, measurement, customer-research, retention, service-quality, commercial-strategy
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Use Recurring Research With Complaining and Non-Complaining Customers
Use when: Building a customer research cadence for an ecommerce brand and deciding which responses should trigger a follow-up or win-back action.
- Ask a short, repeatable recommendation question with an open comment box.
- Do not rely on complaints alone: the source's view is that unhappy customers who never complain are a hidden cost because the company believes they are satisfied.
- Read comments at category level and watch for the gap between what leadership assumes and what the free text actually says, as the source describes for store cleanliness and staff attitude.
- Use the closed-loop follow-up as the mechanism: Lindstrom describes a chief executive who called customers personally because 'that is how I learn', which is the spirit of following up rather than only scoring.
- Tie results to spend behaviour where you can, since the source connects promoter likelihood to higher historical customer value and to store-level performance.
Caveat: The 85 percent threshold for successful promoters and the segment-level comparisons in the source are reported as one consultancy's proprietary results, not independently verified.
Caveat: A single survey cannot tell you why a customer behaves as they do; pair it with transaction data.

Tags: brand, customer-research, retention, measurement, voice-of-customer, service-quality
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Move Research Out of the Building to See Real Behaviour
Use when: Planning a product, pricing or merchandising decision and deciding how much effort to spend on direct customer contact versus internal deck reviews.
- Build direct observation into the calendar the way Lindstrom describes: personally working the front line and calling users is the version of 'getting out of the building' the source recommends.
- Attend the touchpoints where your customers actually make decisions — search and product pages for a store, aisles and tills for physical locations — rather than reviewing reports about them.
- Capture questions customers ask and objections they raise on the call, at the till, or in chats, and use them verbatim as inputs to copy and FAQ.
- Push the observation habit down to senior managers so store-level or channel-level decisions are informed by their own observations, not just from reporting.
- Reserve time for this every quarter; the source's criticism is that leaders drift toward internal meetings as the default.
Caveat: This is executive practice guidance from the source, not a controlled study.
Caveat: Direct observation is qualitative and should not replace measurement of conversion, retention and margin.

Tags: brand, customer-research, retention, service-quality, product-page, merchandising
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Design Products That Fit the Way the Brain Works
Use when: Deciding on navigation, checkout flow, packaging format or subscription mechanics where users must learn a new pattern.
- Prefer the pattern that feels natural: the source's example is Amazon's Simple Storage Service, which won because it was simple, and TiVo, which lost because it left the user in no-man's-land between recording and watching television.
- When you invent a new model, test whether users can predict what happens next: the failure mode in the TiVo example is that the software divided the mind and required the user to deal with a newly present and equal-demanding dual entity.
- Apply this to subscription and replenishment design: keep control and expectations explicit so customers can see what will happen, when and how to change it.
- Apply it to the site or app: use familiar, logical navigation and visible indicators (progress, stock, delivery estimates) so the user's mental model matches the interface.
- Treat category-leading functionality as insufficient: the source argues that superior products can still fail on usability and that better understanding of the brain improves both design and service.
Caveat: TiVo and Amazon are Lindstrom's chosen examples of design principles, not tested experiments in the book.
Caveat: Underestimating the next big idea is a risk the source also acknowledges; simplicity should not be read as 'never innovate'.

Tags: brand, product-design, checkout, retention, subscriptions, user-experience
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Assume ~90% of Buying Is Unconscious — Fix Self-Report Research
Use when: Designing customer research before a product launch, repositioning, or pricing change, and deciding how much weight to put on surveys, reviews and interview answers.
- Accept the source's framing that roughly 90% of consumer buying behaviour is unconscious, meaning 'why did you buy this?' answers capture only a thin slice of the real drivers.
- Keep surveys, NPS and exit polls for what they are good at: stated preference, feature comprehension, price expectation ranges and open complaints.
- Add observational evidence — session recordings, heatmaps, click/scroll data, search-term mining, cart-abandon reason coding, unboxing and shelf footage — for anything about attention and impulse.
- Test at the behaviour level instead of asking: run A/B tests on headline, hero image, price framing, bundle composition and page order, and let measured add-to-cart and purchase conversion decide.
- Treat stated preference as directional only for expensive qualitative decisions such as new brand names or category entry, and confirm with small live experiments on traffic you already own.
- Record which findings came from stated versus observed data so later analysis does not treat opinions as measurements.
Caveat: The 90% figure is the author's summary of the neuromarketing field, not a store-measurable benchmark; do not quote it as an internal KPI.
Caveat: Small-n lab studies (some cited experiments used ten to twenty volunteers) are illustrative, not predictive of your traffic.

Tags: brand, customer-research, experiment-design, measurement, conversion-optimization, consumer-psychology
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Price and Product Story Shape Perceived Enjoyment
Use when: Setting price positioning, discount depth and PDP copy for premium or gift-oriented products where perceived quality drives purchase.
- Decide deliberately whether the product is sold as a premium or a value item before writing copy, because the source's wine and luxury studies suggest price itself feeds perceived pleasantness.
- For premium positioning, avoid habitual discounting on the same PDP: the same item shown heavily discounted changes the reward/conflict balance and can flatten the quality story you paid to build.
- Support the price with concrete provenance, materials, origin, certification or craft detail (see the Seki saba brand case, where certification and handling rules justified a large price rise).
- For value positioning, make the saving explicit and immediate so reward rises while purchase conflict falls — bundle value, quantity breaks, or clear comparison framing.
- Test price-anchoring variants (bundle vs single, gift vs refill) as experiments rather than assuming your audience matches the study populations.
- Do not run both stories on the same page: pick the premium narrative or the discount narrative per SKU and per landing page.
Caveat: The luxury and wine findings come from small fMRI studies with students and volunteers; treat them as hypotheses about framing, not proof your customers will pay more.
Caveat: Discounts may be necessary for liquidation, seasonality or competitive reasons — the point is not to discount reflexively alongside premium positioning.

Tags: brand, pricing, positioning, product-page, offer-design, consumer-psychology
Source: BUYOLOGY MARTIN LINDSTROM (1).docx

---

## Branding Turns Commodities into Premium SKUs
Use when: Differentiating commodity or undifferentiated products — own-label food, staples, unbranded accessories — to escape price comparison.
- Name the product with a specific origin, method or maker rather than a generic descriptor, so buyers can reference it and repeat it.
