Tags: brand, brand-strategy, positioning, differentiation, customer-research
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Use the three brand functions as a decision filter
Use when: When auditing your store's brand, name, imagery or messaging and asking whether it actually helps shoppers or just looks attractive.
- Navigation: check that the brand helps shoppers choose from a bewildering array of options — a distinctive, recognizable identity makes your store findable and memorable in a crowded category.
- Reassurance: verify the brand communicates intrinsic product quality and reassures the customer they made the right choice — this connects directly to PDP content, reviews, guarantees and post-purchase messaging.
- Engagement: check you use distinctive imagery, language and associations that let customers identify with the brand, rather than generic category conventions.
- Treat every touchpoint as an opportunity to increase awareness and build loyalty — the source frames each customer interface as a chance to strengthen the brand, not just complete a transaction.
- Anchor your identity in an immediately recognizable, distinctive professional image that positions you for success regardless of company size (source's wording: any company, any size, anywhere).
Caveat: These functions are a strategic framework from the source, not a measured conversion model; treat them as evaluation criteria, not performance guarantees.
Caveat: Recognition and reassurance depend on consistent repetition over time, so expect the payoff to be long-term rather than immediate.

Tags: brand, brand-strategy, positioning, customer-research, merchandising
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Make it easy to buy, sell, and build brand equity
Use when: When justifying brand/identity investment internally or prioritizing which store improvements to fund first.
- Make it easy for the customer to buy: use a distinctive, recognizable professional identity; help customers manage their perception of the company; and make features and benefits easy to understand. The source also notes a better product design or environment can create delight and loyalty.
- Make it easy for the sales force to sell: strategic identity works across diverse audiences and cultures to build awareness and understanding, and communicates a unique value proposition. Consistent communication across media signals the company's focus.
- Make it easy to build brand equity: treat reputation as one of the most valuable company assets. For small companies and nonprofits the source says future success depends on awareness, preserving reputation, and upholding value. Equity grows through recognition, awareness and loyalty.
- Act on the branding imperatives the source lists: acknowledge the branded world; seize every opportunity to position the company in customers' minds; repeat the strong brand idea; demonstrate (not just declare) your competitive advantage; understand customers' perceptions, preferences, dreams, values and lifestyles; and identify touchpoints where customers meet the product.
- Use identity as a 'sensory magnet' to attract and retain customers rather than treating design as decoration.
Caveat: The source claims intangible brand value often exceeds tangible assets but does not supply a method or figures; do not present this as a measured ratio.
Caveat: Equity building is framed as a long-term investment ('readiness to invest in the future'), so don't expect short-horizon returns.

Tags: brand, brand-equity, positioning, customer-research, merchandising, measurement
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Perception-behavior-performance chain for brand impact
Use when: When setting brand objectives or explaining to leadership why identity work should be funded and measured against intermediate perception and behavior outcomes rather than only sales.
- Work the chain in order: perception → behavior → performance. Improvements in identity first change how the brand is perceived, then whether people act, then the business result.
- Verify each link rather than assuming it: does the identity actually support the desired perception? Does the perception match how customers describe you?
- Note the source's caution (via IBM's Jon Iwata) that there can be no image management without behavior management — brands live transparently, so the operational experience must match the promise, not just the marketing.
- Use the chain to design measurement: pick indicators at the perception and behavior stages instead of jumping straight to revenue conclusions.
- Treat identity as something that should become intrinsic to company culture and act as a constant symbol of core values and heritage.
Caveat: The chain is a conceptual model in the source, not a validated causal equation with effect sizes; don't promise a specific performance lift.
Caveat: Brand value appears on balance sheets in many companies per the source, but valuation methods vary and are outside this material.

Tags: brand, measurement, brand-equity, perception, customer-research
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Cultural layering method before entering a new market
Use when: When localizing a store, marketplace listing, ad creative or packaging for a new country or cultural segment, or when choosing colors, symbols, humor or tone for a non-domestic audience.
- Map objective variables first: naming, language, writing, symbols, color and sound. These are the concrete elements most likely to misfire. The source's example: Deloitte's Green Dot campaign uses black backgrounds in most countries but white in China, because the culture associates black with death.
- Then examine subjective variables: aspirations, emotions, humor, expectations and feelings. These determine whether the brand connects, not just whether it offends.
- Finally look at fundamental principles: societal, economic, spiritual, religious, intellectual and ethical dimensions of the culture.
- Avoid treating a group as one market. The source quotes Joe Ray: the Latino market is not monolithic, monochromatic or one-dimensional, and warns against assumption and stereotype.
- Include trusted native experts on the team — the source says subtle cultural differences are often invisible to outsiders — and research and test widely across diverse cultures within a country or region.
- Plan to keep a team in or in close touch with the region, and test often to stay relevant. Localization is continuous, not a one-time translation pass.
Caveat: The Deloitte background-color example is the source's illustration, not an ecommerce result.
Caveat: The source gives no specific testing thresholds or sample sizes; it stays at the level of 'test widely' and 'test often'.
Caveat: Cultural variables can change; re-validate rather than treating an old localization as permanent.

Tags: brand, cross-culture, positioning, localization, customer-research, packaging
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Write a big idea that acts as organizational totem pole
Use when: When crafting a brand platform, tagline direction, homepage hero promise or recurring campaign theme for a store, and when the team needs one organizing idea across merchandise and marketing.
- Expect the hard part to be the process, not the wording: the source says simplicity of language is deceptive and demands extensive dialogue, patience and the courage to say less.
- Use a skilled facilitator experienced in building consensus when the idea must be agreed across functions; the source states this is usually needed to ask the right questions and reach closure.
- Narrow the focus — citing Al and Laura Ries, the source argues a brand becomes stronger when you narrow it. A big idea should not try to cover everything the store sells.
- Use the finished statement in two directions: internally as a beacon of a distinctive culture, and externally as a competitive advantage that helps consumers choose.
- Benchmark candidates against examples the source cites for scale and tone: Apple 'Think different', Coca-Cola 'Happiness in a bottle', FedEx 'The world on time', GE 'Imagination at work', Target 'Expect more. Pay less.', Method 'People against dirty', MINI Cooper 'Let's motor'.
- Test success by whether the idea can springboard creative work and act as a litmus test for whether a given campaign or page belongs.
Caveat: The listed brands are illustrations of the form, not evidence that your store will perform similarly.
Caveat: A big idea requires long-term internal buy-in; changing it frequently undermines the alignment it is meant to create.

Tags: brand, brand-strategy, positioning, creative-strategy, landing-page
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Treat each customer experience as a designed, chargeable offering
Use when: When designing a store's unboxing, onboarding, customer service or post-purchase flow, or when deciding whether a premium experience can justify higher prices.
- Recognize that audiences are relatively immune to targeted messages; the source's position is that the way to reach customers is to create an experience within them.
- Treat experiences as distinct economic offerings that generate memory — and, per the source, new revenue and profit in commoditized categories.
- Apply the source's test of seriousness: 'It's only when you charge admission that you will be forced to design an experience that's worth an admission fee.' Use it to pressure-test whether a paid bundle, membership or premium service is genuinely experience-worthy.
- Remember even mundane transactions can be turned into memorable experiences; the goal isn't only spectacle.
- Treat 'the experience is the marketing' as a budgeting principle: invest in the experience itself rather than assuming ad spend alone will compensate for a weak one.
Caveat: This is a conceptual argument from The Experience Economy, not a measured ecommerce study; do not present profit claims as guaranteed.
Caveat: Premiums for differentiated experiences are conditional ('if they are truly differentiated') — the source does not promise that any added experience commands a higher price.

Tags: brand, customer-experience, offer-design, retention, bundles, pricing
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Five principles for designing a brand experience
Use when: When designing or auditing store navigation, account features, community elements, personalization or support flows.
- Ubiquitous: be available every day, through the right channels, at the right time. Apply to channel mix and availability rather than assuming one storefront channel is enough.
- Social: help customers build connections with others — the source's claim is that this enhances their experience. Apply to reviews, community, sharing and referral mechanics.
- Semantic: decide what information should be made available to customers, given that more data exists than ever. Apply to PDP specs, sizing, provenance, comparison content.
- Sentient: sense the customer's real-world context so the experience connects to it. Apply to personalization and localization of content and offers.
- Human: ask what the most natural way for customers to interact with your brand is, and how to simplify complexity. Apply to checkout flow, forms and support interactions.
- Use all five together as a review lens; the source presents them as principles for a strong brand experience, not a sequence.
Caveat: These are design principles attributed to Nathan Williams of Wolff Olins in the source, without measured uplift.
Caveat: Semantic and sentient depend on data you actually hold; don't design personalization you cannot support with real data and privacy compliance.

Tags: brand, customer-experience, personalization, retention, product-page, checkout
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Map moments of truth across the customer journey
Use when: When deciding where to invest in store improvements — mapping the path from first ad impression to post-purchase follow-up and prioritizing weak links.
- List the sequence of encounters in order, as the source's hotel example does: sighting the hotel from the highway, walking into the lobby, glimpsing the room. Translate the same progression to your store: ad or search result, category page, PDP, cart, checkout, delivery, unboxing, support contact.
- For each touchpoint, judge whether it currently creates a memorable and positive experience or a forgettable/negative one.
- Note the source's warning: a memorable experience generates positive buzz and is fun to share, but a bad experience becomes a lost opportunity that can sabotage the brand.
- Recognize that sharing the experience can matter as much as having it — the source notes families sharing a Disney experience via Facebook and Instagram is as important as the visit itself, so build in shareable moments.
- Look for ways to preserve the core while enhancing the experience (source quotes Starbucks' Howard Schultz), rather than changing everything at once.
- Treat differentiation as the goal: the source's framing is that compelling, truly differentiated experiences attract customers, extend loyalty and can command a premium.
Caveat: The hotel moments-of-truth diagram is an illustrative example, not an ecommerce result.
Caveat: The source says global competition is fierce and urges thinking 'far beyond the point of sale', but gives no prioritization metric for which touchpoints pay off most.

Tags: brand, customer-experience, retention, journey-mapping, packaging, acquisition
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Choose a brand architecture pattern before adding lines or acquisitions
Use when: When a store adds new categories, private-label lines, acquired brands, or sub-brands and you must decide whether to use the parent name, endorse it, or keep names separate.
- Clarify the strategic questions first: do the benefits of leveraging the parent name outweigh the cost? does the new entity's positioning require distance from the parent? will co-branding confuse customers? do you build on existing equity or rename? should the parent always appear in a secondary position?
- Match the answers to a pattern. Monolithic: a strong single master brand where customers choose on brand loyalty and features matter less than the brand promise and persona (source examples: Google and Google Maps, FedEx and FedEx Express).
- Endorsed: clear market presence for the product or division plus marketing synergy and visibility from the parent (source examples: iPod and Apple, Polo and Ralph Lauren, Oreo and Nabisco).
- Pluralistic: a series of well-known consumer brands where the parent may be invisible or inconsequential to consumers and known mainly to investors (source examples: Tang and Kraft Foods, Godiva and Campbell Soup, The Ritz-Carlton and Marriott, KFC and Yum Brands, Kleenex and Kimberly Clark).
- Expect a mixture: the source states most large companies selling products and services use a mix of strategies. Bring visual and verbal order to the hierarchy so the company can grow and market more effectively.
Caveat: The source explicitly says there is no universal agreement on brand architecture terms across strategists, so don't treat the three labels as an industry standard.
Caveat: Brand architecture decisions carry marketing, cost, time and legal implications; the source says decision makers examine all four.

Tags: brand, brand-architecture, product-selection, merchandising, private-label, brand-equity
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Evaluate a name against seven qualities and named types
Use when: When naming a store, product, collection or private-label line, or when reviewing an existing name that no longer fits the catalogue or markets you sell into.
- Score candidates on the source's seven qualities: meaningful (communicates the essence and supports the desired image); distinctive (unique, easy to remember, pronounce and spell, differentiated from competitors, easy to share on social networks); future-oriented (positions for growth and has 'long legs'); modular (enables brand extensions); protectable (can be owned and trademarked, with a domain available); positive (positive connotations in served markets, no strong negatives); visual (works in a logo, in text and in brand architecture).
- Identify the type, because each carries different trade-offs: founder names (Tory Burch, Ben & Jerry's) may be easier to protect but are tied to a real person; descriptive names (E*TRADE, Toys 'R' Us) communicate intent but can become limiting as the business diversifies; fabricated names (Pinterest, Kodak, TiVo) are distinctive but require capital to educate the market; metaphor names (Nike, Patagonia, Amazon.com) allude to a quality; acronyms are hard to remember and copyright and require substantial advertising (the source notes IBM and GE only became well-known after establishing their full names); magic-spell names alter spelling for distinctiveness (Flickr, Tumblr, Netflix); and combinations of types (Citibank, Hope's Cookies) can be strongest.
- Expect extensive search and legal clearance work — the source stresses that the process is rigorous and exhaustive, with hundreds of names reviewed, and that connotation analysis in served markets is required.
Caveat: The source warns there is no perfect name and that compromise is likely; it also lists 'I will know it when I hear it' and 'we cannot afford to test the name' as naming myths rather than valid methods.
Caveat: Trademark/domain availability changes, so verify current status with counsel rather than relying on the type as protection.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, private-label, product-selection
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Decide whether a tagline earns a place in the store's messaging
Use when: When writing or reviewing a store's tagline, homepage hero line, packaging promise or recurring campaign line.
- Check the essentials: short, differentiated from competitors, unique, captures brand essence and positioning, easy to say and remember, no negative connotations, displayed in a small font, protectable and trademarkable, and evokes an emotional response.
- Classify the tagline type to ensure fit: imperative (commands action, usually starts with a verb — Nike 'Just do it', TOMS 'One for One'); descriptive (states the service, product or promise — BMW 'The ultimate driving machine'); superlative (positions as best in class, e.g. Budweiser 'King of beers'); provocative (thought-provoking, often a question — eBay 'Where else?'); or specific (reveals the business category).
- Confirm the tagline is a brand promise you can actually deliver — the source cites Target's 'Expect More, Pay Less' as a promise to consumers.
- Plan for long use and frequent, consistent repetition; the source says the best taglines have a long life and transcend changes in the marketplace and lifestyle.
- Expect taglines to have shorter life spans than logos historically, but do not use that as license to change them casually — repetition is what builds meaning.
Caveat: Tagline examples are illustrative, not evidence that a similar line will lift sales.
Caveat: Protectability matters; verify trademark status if the line is central to your packaging or campaigns.

Tags: brand, tagline, positioning, landing-page, packaging, creative-strategy
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Keep the brand speaking with one voice across channels
Use when: When assigning copy across store pages, emails, packaging inserts and social posts, or fixing inconsistency after multiple teams and freelancers have written content.
- Inventory the language assets the source lists, so nothing is left to improvisation: nomenclature (formal and informal company name, taglines, descriptors, product/process/service/division names), brand essence, mission and vision statements, value propositions, key messages, guiding principles, customer pledges, vocabulary, history, boilerplate and elevator speech.
- Standardize voice and tone rules: headline style, punctuation, capitalization, emphasis, and the standard for accuracy, clarity and consistency.
- Cover information mechanics too: calls to action, phone numbers, URLs, email signatures, voicemail, abbreviations, titles, addresses and directions.
- Apply the same discipline across touchpoints the source enumerates: websites and blogs, news releases, FAQs, press kits, brochures, shareholder communications, call-center and sales scripts, presentations, announcements, blast emails, ad campaigns, direct mail, product directions, signage and apps.
- Write so the message is vital, straightforward, eloquent and substantive, and accessible to all customers; cut through hype and clutter.
- Accept that unified messaging requires buy-in at all levels and long-term commitment — repetition is what makes a message memorable.
Caveat: Consistency requires ongoing governance; one style guide alone does not enforce it.
Caveat: The source notes a busy reader may spend only minutes, so prioritize clarity and brevity over completeness in any single message.

Tags: brand, tone-of-voice, messaging, merchandising, packaging, email
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Use message mapping: one big idea, three key messages
Use when: When structuring a homepage, product page, pitch deck or press material and you need to decide how many core claims to feature.
- Identify the one unified big idea first, then derive three supporting key messages rather than a long list of features.
- Apply the three-item limit deliberately; the source's rationale for the constraint comes from risk communications, where people at risk can comprehend only three messages. It notes the approach is also helpful in brand communications and press relations.
- Use message mapping as a discipline for holding-company communications too — the source's example is establishing key messages at the holding level to protect assets and signal to operating companies that clarity is valued (source: Jessica Berwind, Berwind Corporation).
- Write each of the three with clarity, brevity and precision, editing out modifying phrases, adverbs and extraneous conversational text so what remains is the distilled essence.
- Exploit repetition: the source says consistency is built on repetition and soundbites acquire a vibrant identity when heard again and again.
Caveat: The three-message limit is a communications heuristic from risk communications, adapted by the source for branding; it is not a tested ecommerce rule.
Caveat: The source attributes the three-message threshold to Dr. Vincent Covello; treat the exact number as source-specific.

Tags: brand, messaging, landing-page, product-page, creative-strategy, copywriting
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Write brand copy by polishing and cutting
Use when: When writing or editing product descriptions, category copy, emails or ads where length and clarity directly affect comprehension.
- Use language that resonates with meaning and expect readers to complete the message with layers of their own experience — this argues for copy that leaves room for the customer rather than over-explaining.
- Aim for clarity, brevity and precision on the assumption that a busy reader may have only minutes to spare.
- Polish and cut as if you were a jeweler; the source's image is that every sentence should reveal new facets to the customer.
- Cut through clutter to produce soundbites that gain identity through repetition — but only repeat lines you are comfortable having associated with the brand long term.
- Edit out modifying phrases, adverbs and extraneous conversational text; the source's claim is that what remains is the distillation, the essence, and that eliminating distracting references gives the text impact.
- Accept the underlying writing standard the source quotes from Strunk and White: a sentence should contain no unnecessary words, a paragraph no unnecessary sentences.
Caveat: These are style principles, not measured conversion tactics; the source does not claim brevity itself increases conversion.
Caveat: Cutting must not remove necessary product information that buyers need for the purchase decision.

Tags: brand, copywriting, messaging, product-page, tone-of-voice
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Choose between redesign and identity change using stated triggers
Use when: When diagnosing your own store's situation — whether a growth problem, a naming problem, a visual problem, or a consistency problem — and scoping the right kind of brand project.
- New company/product: triggers include starting a business, launching a product that needs a name and logo, raising capital, giving a campaign its own identity, or going public.
- Name change: triggers include a name that no longer fits the business, a trademark conflict, negative connotations in new markets, a misleading name, a merger, or needing a new name for the Chinese market.
- Revitalize a brand: triggers include repositioning, unclear communication of who you are, going global, low awareness, devalued stock, appealing to a more affluent market, or a site that fails on mobile.
- Revitalize a brand identity: triggers include looking dated next to cutting-edge products, uncertainty about whether the identity works on the web, not standing shoulder to shoulder with competitors, inconsistent nomenclature across divisions, embarrassment when handing out a card, or an iconic symbol that has become unreadable as a logotype.
- Build an integrated system: triggers include inconsistent presentation to customers, lack of visual consistency, needing brand architecture for acquisitions, undistinctive packaging, competitors looking better with rising sales, marketing that looks like different companies, or divisions each doing their own marketing inefficiently.
- Merger situations: triggers include signaling a merger of equals, communicating synergy, building on both companies' equity, signaling a new industry leader, needing a new name, and deciding how to evaluate an acquisition's brand and fold it into the architecture.
Caveat: This list is descriptive of common situations, not a scoring system; several triggers can apply at once.
Caveat: The source stresses that a strong brand identity signals coherence, but no evidence is given that any specific trigger predicts commercial outcomes.

Tags: brand, redesign, rebranding, brand-architecture, positioning, naming
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Protect the brand through behavior, not image management
Use when: When responding to negative reviews, a service failure, a product issue or public criticism, and when deciding whether to hide or engage with customer complaints.
- Accept the source's framing via IBM's Jon Iwata: character is like a tree and reputation like its shadow. The warning is that many treat their job as manipulating the shadow rather than tending the health of the tree.
- Treat the structural constraint as decisive: in a world of transparency and democratized media it is increasingly difficult to lead a double life, so there can be no image management without behavior management.
- Audit operational behavior — fulfillment, returns, support, product quality — because that is what the brand ultimately reflects, and communication alone cannot cover a mismatch.
- Prefer transparent, direct engagement over suppression of criticism; the source's crisis checklist (referenced in the same section) includes a 'do not delete negative comments' protocol.
- Recognize consumers as co-creators and competitors as potential collaborators (source: Karl Heiselman, Wolff Olins): brand is not what you say it is, it is what they say it is (source: Marty Neumeier).
Caveat: This is a strategic principle from the source, not a measured crisis-response method; the detailed checklist referenced in the source's outline is not reproduced in the supplied text.
Caveat: 'Do not delete negative comments' is the source's protocol, not a legal or platform-policy rule; moderation choices must also respect platform and legal requirements.

Tags: brand, reputation-management, crisis-communications, retention, customer-experience
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Use an outside perspective to articulate strategy
Use when: When a store's brand/positioning is stalled by internal disagreement, competing priorities, or a founder's attachment to legacy decisions.
- Recognize the pattern the source describes: no one develops brand strategy alone; it comes from extended dialogue among the CEO, marketing, sales, advertising, public relations, operations and distribution, and global companies frequently bring in brand strategists, strategic marketing firms or brand consultants.
- Assess whether an internal consensus is actually reachable. The source's claim is that it often takes someone from outside — an experienced strategic and creative thinker — to help a company articulate what is already there.
- Run the process as dialogue rather than a decision handed down; the source links strong strategy to extended dialogue and, for big ideas, to a skilled facilitator experienced in building consensus who asks the right questions and achieves closure.
- Consider the alternative route the source notes: strategy sometimes originates with a visionary at company inception, or is redefined by a visionary team.
- Watch for the failure mode the source names — companies falter because they have no clear strategy — and treat the absence of an articulable strategy as the trigger for this work.
Caveat: This card is about internal strategy articulation; the source's discussion does not specify fees, timelines or guarantees.
Caveat: Bringing in outsiders is one situational route per the source, not a requirement for every store.

Tags: brand, brand-strategy, positioning, decision-making
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Survey stakeholders beyond buyers before setting positioning
Use when: When conducting customer and stakeholder research before a repositioning, launch or packaging overhaul, and when deciding who to interview beyond existing buyers.
- Identify the full set of constituencies the source calls key stakeholders: reputation and goodwill extend far beyond target customers, and employees are treated as 'internal customers' whose power is far-reaching.
- Gather insight into stakeholder characteristics, behavior, needs and perceptions — the source claims this yields a high return.
- Use stakeholder bias and opinion data specifically to inform positioning and achieve meaningful differentiation (source: Ann Willoughby, Willoughby Design).
- Apply the source's workshop technique to surface assumptions: Willoughby Design uses persona cards; participants draw a picture representing a key stakeholder and explain what matters most to them, then must fully understand the role they are playing.
- Apply research across the breadth of decisions the source names: positioning, the tilt of brand messages, launch strategy and plan.
- Account for generation differences cautiously; the source includes a generational table but notes researchers use the same terms without agreeing on the dates, and cites that Millennials are the first generation to grow up in a digital culture and aspire to values like freedom, knowledge and creative self-expression more than conspicuous consumption.
Caveat: The generational birth-year bands are described in the source as non-standardized; do not treat them as fixed segmentation rules.
Caveat: The persona-card exercise is an illustrative workshop method, not a validated research instrument.

Tags: brand, customer-research, positioning, differentiation, retention
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Define brand ideals before choosing visual solutions
Use when: Approving or rejecting a logo/identity concept, refreshing packaging, or briefing any designer for a new product line or store rebrand.
- Write down how the business scores on the ten ideals named in the source: vision, meaning, authenticity, differentiation, sustainability, coherence, flexibility, commitment, value, and design excellence.
- Rate where you are strong and weak on each. The source treats brand identity as a strategic asset, so gaps here become constraints on any visual work.
- Treat design excellence as a given rather than a differentiator; the source argues the best brands marry intelligence and insight with imagination and craft, not just a nice look.
- Use this list as the brief you hand to designers and the scorecard you use when evaluating concepts, so you judge proposals against brand logic instead of personal taste.
- Revisit the ideals when you launch, reposition, merge or add a retail presence; the source states they hold across all these engagements.
Caveat: These are the source's ideals, not a validated scoring model with numeric benchmarks.
Caveat: Small brands can adopt them, but the source gives no evidence about cost or timing for tiny teams.

Tags: brand, identity-design, brand-strategy, product-page, packaging, positioning
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Use functional criteria as the minimum bar for any mark
Use when: Selecting a logo for a new store, product label, packaging or app icon, or auditing an existing mark that may be failing on small screens.
- Test the proposed mark against the source's functional criteria: bold, memorable and appropriate; immediately recognizable; a consistent image of the company; clearly communicating the company persona; legally protectable; enduring value; works across media and scale; and works in both black and white and color.
- Physically preview the mark at the sizes your store uses: favicon, packaging panel, label, mobile icon and signage. The source notes identity must work at every scale.
- Check trademark availability with an IP professional before committing, since the source treats legal protection as a functional requirement rather than an afterthought.
- Run the mark in single color and full color before launch; the source lists this as a criterion, so a mark that only works in color is not finished.
- Forget nothing: ask whether the mark still reads as your brand when seen out of context.
Caveat: The source does not rank criteria or explain which trade-offs to make when criteria conflict.
Caveat: Legal protectability generally requires advice from a trademark attorney, not the designer alone.

Tags: brand, identity-design, logo-design, packaging, app-icon, legal
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Write meaning before visuals so approval stops being a taste fight
Use when: Running a rebrand, product-line identity, or packaging redesign where stakeholders keep disagreeing on subjective grounds.
- Document what the brand stands for—the big idea, strategic position, values and voice—before showing logo options, naming options or key messages.
- Use that written framework as the rallying point for decision-makers; the source compares meaning to a campfire that builds consensus and precedes any presentation of visual solutions.
- Require every element in the identity system to have a rationale within that framework, so each choice can be explained, understood, communicated and approved.
- Expect meaning to evolve as the business changes; plan to revisit the written framework when the assortment, business model or market shifts.
- When a concept is rejected, go back to the framework rather than iterating blindly on visuals.
Caveat: The source is a general brand-identity framework, not empirical evidence that written frameworks improve outcomes.
Caveat: Meaning that evolves over time requires periodic review, which adds process overhead.

Tags: brand, identity-design, brand-strategy, stakeholder-alignment, rebrand, process
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Distill the brand name into its meanings
Use when: Naming a private label, a new product line or a sub-brand, or choosing taglines and messaging for a landing page.
- Write the proposed name or a long scientific or descriptive name and split it into each word or component.
- Put each component into different parts of speech—verb, adjective, adverb, noun—and note what new meanings and associations emerge.
- Use the exercise as a group activity with the client or team; the source's stated purpose is discovery, exploring meaning and nuance and coming together around key messages.
- Shortlist the resulting language for taglines, product names, key messages and site copy, then test each on real customer language.
- Keep the strongest two or three meanings as your key messages; the source notes that people at risk can comprehend only three messages, and applies the same discipline to brand communications and press relations.
Caveat: This is a discovery and alignment exercise from a brand consultancy; it does not predict purchase behavior by itself.
Caveat: Culturally loaded names need dialect and market checks before adoption.

Tags: brand, naming, messaging, positioning, copywriting, product-page
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Build coherence across every customer touchpoint
Use when: Auditing the customer experience across product pages, emails, packaging, service replies and checkout, or onboarding new team members who write and design for the brand.
- State the positioning clearly and write down how you want to be perceived; coherence begins with internal clarity, not with visual rules.
- Adopt a single consistent voice that evolves from one central dynamic idea, and use it in every communication channel.
- Treat each touchpoint as a brand experience, including packaging, invoices, emails, replies and the checkout flow; the source says every touchpoint should be considered a brand experience.
- Choose a cohesive visual system: designed color, typeface families and formats that advance immediate recognition.
- Hold every product and service to the same high quality level; the source argues anything less than superior quality reduces brand value both consciously and unconsciously.
- Keep language clear and naming logical inside your architecture so customers can navigate choices on the site without confusion.
Caveat: Coherence in the source is explicitly not rigid or limiting; over-enforcing standards can suppress needed marketing creativity.
Caveat: The source asserts quality effects but cites no measured results.

Tags: brand, retention, packaging, checkout, customer-experience, copywriting
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Start from a clear-eyed self-assessment for authenticity
Use when: Writing the About page, brand story, sustainability claims or any positioning copy for the store, and reviewing whether claims are defensible.
- Answer in writing: who we are, what market we serve, how we are positioned, what value we deliver, and how we differ from named competitors.
- Audit all claims on packaging, listings and pages against what you actually deliver. One contributor in the source defines authenticity as doing what you promise, not being who you are.
- Correct claims you cannot substantiate, because social networks broadcast brands that do not stand true to their promise, per the source's sustainability discussion.
- Let expression stay appropriate to your mission, history, culture, values and personality instead of copying a trend aesthetic.
- Repeat the self-assessment when the assortment or business model changes; the source treats self-knowledge as the starting position of strength for identity work.
Caveat: The source presents two overlapping definitions of authenticity; reconcile them per your context rather than treating either as law.
Caveat: No method is given for proving consumer perception of authenticity.

Tags: brand, positioning, customer-research, landing-page, claims, storytelling
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Design for differentiation that customers can grasp quickly
Use when: Choosing how to present your products against a crowded category on the homepage, PDP or paid creative, or planning a zag when every competitor does the same thing.
- Name the category norm explicitly: what does every competitor say and show? Then decide where you will deliberately do something else, per the 'when everybody zigs, zag' guidance in the source.
- Translate the difference into a claim a customer can grasp in seconds on a listing, ad or page headline, because the source warns difference alone is not enough.
- Reduce choice overload. The source cites the view that best companies curate offerings so consumers are not overwhelmed sorting through every possibility; use this to cut filters, SKUs on a page or bundle options.
- Show the difference in product imagery and copy, not just in an adjective; the source says the brand must demonstrate the difference.
- Re-test presentation when competitors copy your differentiation, since attention and loyalty are contested by all brands in the category and beyond.
Caveat: Curating assortments to reduce overwhelm is a quoted executive opinion in the source, not a tested merchandising rule.
Caveat: Do not treat differentiation as permission to be confusing; the same passage requires easy understanding.

Tags: brand, positioning, differentiation, merchandising, product-page, creative-strategy
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Use flexibility so identity survives new products and interfaces
Use when: Planning a logo and identity system for a store that expects new product lines, marketplaces, apps or packaging formats.
- Design the identity as a system with parameters rather than a single fixed artwork, so the toolbox allows creativity inside recognizable limits.
- Set up a durable brand architecture with an overarching logic for sub-brands, collections and product names, so marketing a new product is facilitated rather than blocked.
- Account for unknown future touchpoints. The source notes no one can say with certainty which products or devices will exist in five years, so the system must seize new opportunities quickly.
- Test the system on the hardest format you use today, such as a tiny app icon or a mobile page, not only on the largest.
- Expect to refresh expression while keeping the central idea; the source describes such design activity as programming and craft dependent, not a one-off set of files.
Caveat: Flexibility is not license for inconsistency; the source pairs it with coherence and strict standards.
Caveat: The source provides no cost or maintenance guidance for flexible systems.

Tags: brand, identity-design, brand-architecture, product-selection, app-icon, packaging
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Classify your mark to choose the right problem fit
Use when: Briefing a designer or deciding the form of a logo for a store, private label or app icon.
- Review the categories against your needs: abstract or symbolic marks carry strategic ambiguity and suit companies with many unrelated divisions; pictorial marks use a literal simplified image; emblems attach the name inextricably to the picture; letterforms use one or more letters as a mnemonic; wordmarks are freestanding names or acronyms.
- Note the emblem's specific weakness in the source: as mobile icons and tiny one-sixth-inch logos shrink, emblems present the biggest legibility challenge when miniaturized.
- Define the signature, meaning the structured relationship between logotype, brandmark and tagline, and decide whether you need split or horizontal and vertical variations per application.
- Choose based on both aspirational and functional criteria, then require a written rationale for the chosen approach.
- Combine elements where useful; the source states categories are pliant and many marks combine more than one.
Caveat: The source explicitly says there are no hard-and-fast rules for matching a mark type to a company type.
Caveat: Category boundaries blur, so treat them as a briefing aid rather than a taxonomy.

Tags: brand, identity-design, logo-design, app-icon, design-brief, packaging
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Exploit the shape-then-color-then-form sequence of cognition
Use when: Choosing a favicon, app icon, packaging panel, ad thumbnail or any visual that must register instantly at small size.
- Prioritize a distinctive silhouette, because the source says the brain acknowledges and remembers shapes first and reading is not necessary to identify shapes.
- Choose a distinctive color signature second; color can trigger emotion and evoke a brand association, which is why the source notes companies such as Kodak and Tiffany trademarked core colors.
- Treat language and wordmarks as third in the sequence, since the brain takes longer to process language; through repeated exposure some companies drop the logotype from advertising entirely.
- Apply this to small placements: make the shape work with the color at favicon, icon and thumbnail sizes before adding the name.
- Use repeated exposure to build recognition; the source describes color and symbol becoming mnemonic devices when seen frequently.
Caveat: The source is a design text describing perception sequence, not a controlled experiment with conversion metrics.
Caveat: Dropping the logotype depends on very high prior recognition; most young DTC brands cannot assume it.

Tags: brand, identity-design, app-icon, packaging, creative-strategy, conversion-optimization
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Decide whether a character belongs in your brand
Use when: Considering a mascot for packaging, kids' products, social content or ad creative, or reviewing whether an existing mascot still fits.
- Confirm the character expresses real brand attributes or values rather than being decorative; the source says a character trademark embodies brand attributes or values.
- Plan for voice and motion, not just a drawing: the source notes the best characters have recognizable voices and jingles, enabling them to move from shelf to screen.
- Budget for periodic redrawing. The source observes that characters rarely age well and usually must be redrawn to stay culturally current, citing Michelin and Betty Crocker as examples.
- Test the character for cultural fit in every market you sell in, especially if voices and accents are involved.
- Reconsider the mascot when the assortment pivots; a character tied to one product line can become a constraint, as the source illustrates with a former corporate icon tied to a single category.
Caveat: Character effectiveness claims in the source are narrative examples, not measured ecommerce results.
Caveat: Historic characters listed are examples from the source's era and should not be read as recommendations.

Tags: brand, mascot, packaging, creative-strategy, product-selection, retention
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Build a consistent identity on responsiveness, not separate sites
Use when: Planning the mobile experience of a store, deciding on site architecture, or fixing inconsistent experiences between desktop and mobile shoppers.
- Design for the most constrained device first to define parameters, then roll out to larger resolutions, per the fundamentals of responsive design credited in the source to Vijay Mathews.
- Establish a clear hierarchy of information that supports an intuitive experience, and structure content access so it responds to the user's environment and behavior, such as on-the-go versus at home.
- Keep a clear relationship between resolution families so the site remains visually recognizable across formats.
- Account for each device's physical input and build a more native experience, since the source notes not everything is point-and-click.
- Use webfonts to preserve legibility across resolutions and formats.
- Prefer a singular system with one content management system and a flexible grid over multiple variant sites.
Caveat: Responsive design fundamentals are stated as principles; the source gives no measured conversion evidence.
Caveat: Native input conventions and device families change quickly, so verify current platform guidance.

Tags: brand, responsive-design, mobile, user-experience, site-architecture, content-strategy
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Design icons and apps around one task
Use when: Specifying a store app, a mobile checkout, or an app icon that must compete on a crowded phone home screen.
- Pick one task for the app and do it very well; the source states the single easiest way to screw up an app is making it try to do too many things.
- Verify the qualities listed: reliable and consistent performance, careful testing, compatibility with the platforms you target, fast loading, and continuous uninterrupted performance.
- Design the app icon to express an attribute or brand idea, choosing among iconic, illustrative, photographic, wordmark, letterform or abstract approaches described in the source.
- Use color to differentiate a family of apps, as the source describes for one museum's multiple apps, and use letterforms when a single letter already carries your brand equity.
- Confirm the icon reads at actual small size, since icons are seen at phone scale rather than presentation scale.
Caveat: The qualities list is attributed to a third-party app developer in the source, not to controlled testing.
Caveat: Performance thresholds and platform interface conventions change; verify current requirements before building.

Tags: brand, app-icon, mobile, checkout, user-experience, design-brief
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Turn customer experience into video storytelling
Use when: Briefing a product video, brand film, founder story or channel content plan for a store or marketplace listing.
- Optimize the first 15 seconds of the video, per the checklist the source reproduces from a platform creator playbook.
- Decide in advance which use the video serves: launching or relaunching a brand with one voice, articulating a common message, simplifying complex subjects by storytelling or animation, or showing personality without reciting a list of attributes.
- Include a specific call to action in the video or through annotations and cards.
- Set a recurring publishing schedule and build a programming calendar around tentpole events relevant to your audience.
- Identify channels with similar content and audiences and create cross-promotion or collaboration videos.
- Use analytics on the channel to refine content, programming and production rather than guessing.
Caveat: The YouTube checklist is reproduced from a platform document that may have changed; verify current requirements and formats.
Caveat: Video effectiveness claims in the source are anecdotal, including an internal law-firm example, not ecommerce conversion results.

Tags: brand, video, creative-strategy, acquisition, content-strategy, measurement
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Treat social media as a participative channel with disclosure rules
Use when: Setting up or auditing social content, influencer or affiliate programs, and community management for a store.
- Define the objective first: customer service, engagement, driving sales or media outreach are different jobs requiring different setups.
- Find where your audience is, listen to what they say and build or tap into that community before pushing content.
- Plan what you will say; the source warns that a good community with nothing to say cannot sustain conversation.
- Decide who manages the activity based on the objective and audience, and invest in training for internal or external people.
- Agree metrics in advance and review them regularly, covering outputs, outcomes and impact qualitatively and quantitatively.
- Use the disclosure checklist: err on the side of over-disclosure, disclose fully where space allows, hold agencies and contractors to your standards rather than lowering them, and ask whether family and friends or public discussion would make you uncomfortable with the campaign.
Caveat: Disclosure norms and platform rules change and vary by market; verify current requirements and legal obligations.
Caveat: The source debates social ROI and offers no formula.

Tags: brand, social-media, retention, acquisition, measurement, influencer
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Budget brand work as an asset with legal protection
Use when: Allocating budget to branding versus performance, choosing whether to register trademarks, or justifying identity spend to stakeholders.
- Treat identity as a strategic business tool that seizes opportunities to build awareness, increase recognition and express competitive difference, rather than a one-off design cost.
- Protect the asset: register trademarks and trade dress in the markets you serve, both local and global, and educate employees and vendors about compliance.
- Maintain uniform standards and pursue quality relentlessly; the source lists strict adherence as a business priority that preserves value.
- Expect value to build through consistent use over time rather than a single campaign, and align the identity with sustainability claims consumers can verify, since the source notes consumers shop their values and social networks expose brands that fail their promise.
- Review coverage when you enter new markets or launch new categories, because protection must follow where you sell.
Caveat: The source asserts a premium for strong brands but gives no pricing or valuation formula.
Caveat: Trademark strategy is jurisdiction-specific and needs professional advice.

Tags: brand, legal, brand-strategy, pricing, measurement, positioning
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Run the brand like a managed asset with internal tooling
Use when: Building brand guidelines, onboarding new employees or agencies, or auditing where your brand shows up across owned channels and packaging.
- Inventory your touchpoints, including packaging, emails, listings, invoices, retail or event materials and social profiles; the source's example organizes touchpoints by type, country, channel and stakeholder.
- Provide employees with tools that make it easy to be a brand champion, since the source says the best companies supply these rather than relying on policing.
- Pair a top-down mandate with bottom-up understanding of why the brand matters, as the source describes for a global brand program.
- House best practices in one accessible internal place, using case studies to inform and inspire local implementation rather than only to enforce rules.
- Review the touchpoint inventory regularly; the source treats managing the asset as requiring desire and a disciplined approach to keep it relevant.
Caveat: The 101-touchpoint figure comes from one large professional-services firm and may not fit a small or mid-sized store.
Caveat: Internal tooling and content-management systems require ongoing maintenance the source does not budget.

Tags: brand, governance, onboarding, packaging, retention, process
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Enter complex markets through local partners and naming research
Use when: Launching or expanding a store into a new country or region, or adapting product names and marketing copy for a new market.
- Begin by finding in-country native partners and advisors, and take the necessary time to build relationships based on trust, respect and understanding, per the source's account of successful multinationals in China.
- Treat naming as the hardest decision: decide whether to blend or balance East and West or emphasize one, and check what the name sounds like and means across dialects.
- Commission extensive research before launch, because the source attributes China's branding complexity to diverse regional, linguistic and cultural nuances and relative newness to branding.
- Adapt rather than export; the source quotes the view that succeeding in China is adapting to China, and that innovating there can open pathways for global innovation.
- Test the local identity work against the same brand ideals used at home, particularly coherence and differentiation.
Caveat: The source gives directional guidance about market entry, not a step-by-step launch process or cost estimate.
Caveat: Dialect and naming assessments require local expert and legal review; this card does not replace that.

Tags: brand, international, naming, positioning, customer-research, market-entry
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## China market entry for global brands: slow relationship-first approach
Use when: Planning to enter, launch, or expand an ecommerce brand into the Chinese market, or considering whether to localize naming, identity, and packaging first.
- Identify and retain in-country native partners and advisors before committing to go-to-market. Source view: the most successful multinationals in China are the ones that don't rush and take time to build relationships based on trust, respect and understanding.
- Treat cultural understanding as the input to naming, product design, identity design, taglines and color selection rather than a later localization step. Different dialects have different pronunciation and connotations, so test in the main Chinese dialects to avoid negative associations.
- For naming, decide whether to create a local- or foreign-sounding Chinese name based on target consumer, competitors, cities, industry and other marketplace dynamics. A Chinese name need not be a direct translation; it should reflect brand attributes, and is often chosen for associative, relevant meaning rather than sound similarity (though sound similarity is sometimes used).
- Weight auspiciousness, good fortune, happiness, power and status in Chinese naming and design, and keep cultural heritage in view since consumers value it.
- Build trademark and IP awareness into brand development from the start: China's trademark registration is competitive, so register early in appropriate categories and budget for monitoring and enforcement.
- Monitor cultural and economic change continuously because China's rate of development is rapid, and influences converge from local and international sources.
Caveat: Source-specific claim, not a guarantee: 'most successful' multinationals are described as those who don't rush, but the source gives no data on failure rates for faster entrants.
Caveat: China is described as the most complex BRIC market branding-wise; advice here is presented as applying to China specifically.

Tags: brand, china-market-entry, localization, naming, trademark, cultural-research, global-expansion
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Designing Chinese names that carry meaning and avoid dialect risk
Use when: Naming or renaming a product, line, or store for Chinese-speaking consumers, or evaluating a proposed name against regional pronunciation risk.
- Start from the brand attributes you want the name to convey rather than from a literal translation of the English name. The Kleenex Chinese name means 'clean and comfort' — a meaning-based name rather than a transliteration.
- Decide deliberately whether the name should sound local or foreign. This depends on target consumer, competitors, cities, industry and other marketplace dynamics; there is no universal answer.
- Choose the naming path: sometimes Chinese names are chosen for similarity of sound to the original name, but more often for an associative and relevant meaning.
- Test pronunciation and connotations across the main Chinese dialects (Mandarin and Cantonese are main but not the only dialects) to avoid negative associations.
- Build in auspiciousness: good fortune, happiness, power and status carry weight in Chinese culture.
- Worked example of a hybrid logic: the Everwines Chinese name uses a syllable that means to sing or praise and is pronounced the same as the word for 'forever', corresponding to 'Ever', plus a syllable meaning 'grape', the source of wine.
Caveat: No single naming rule applies across China's regional, linguistic and cultural diversity; testing, not assumption, determines suitability.
Caveat: The Everwines and Kleenex examples are case illustrations of naming logic, not evidence that the same construction will work for other categories.

Tags: brand, naming, china-market-entry, localization, multilingual-branding, cultural-research
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Chinese name and product-structure design: Gillette SCR-1 example
Use when: Deciding whether to adapt product format, packaging structure, or positioning when entering or expanding in a market with different usage habits.
- Research the actual usage behavior of the target consumer before assuming your product format fits. Gillette's SCR-1 was designed for Chinese men beginning wet shaving, many of whom previously used electric razors for dry shaving.
- Translate the usage insight into the physical design: the triangular packaging structure was based on Chinese men typically needing to shave only the chin and upper lip, referred to as 'the sensitive triangle'.
- Use the insight to define the product name and positioning — SCR-1 stands for Skin Care Razor — so the name communicates the market-specific benefit.
- Treat naming, product design, identity design, taglines and color selection as one connected localization problem, all affected by cultural understanding.
- Before committing tooling and shelf format, verify the local behavior claim with native advisors or local partners, since behavior assumptions drive physical design decisions that are expensive to reverse.
Caveat: This is a documented example of one company's market-specific design, not a template to copy for other products or categories.
Caveat: The source does not give sales or adoption results for SCR-1.

Tags: brand, product-design, china-market-entry, localization, packaging, consumer-behavior
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Private label strategy: choose single master brand or pluralistic architecture
Use when: Building or refreshing an online or omnichannel private label program and deciding how many owned brands to run and how to position them.
- Decide the architecture: a monolithic/master-brand approach puts one owned brand on all products (IKEA cited as an example, Waitrose also listed as master brand), while a pluralistic architecture creates multiple brands (Safeway, Aldi, Giant Eagle, Target, Loblaw examples).
- If you use a master brand, invest in great product stories and clear value positioning rather than trying to build separate name recognition for each product.
- If you use multiple brands, treat each as a differentiated proposition (e.g. Target's Market Pantry versus Archer Farms versus Sutton & Dodge; Loblaw's President's Choice spanning cookies to financial services at brand level; Costco's Kirkland Signature spanning fresh food, alcohol and tires).
- Position on value-add, not cheapness: the perceptual shift from low quality to value-added has been accelerated by more upscale and better-designed packaging and by insights into consumers' unmet needs.
- Use the private-label advantage: because the brand already exists in a branded retail environment, it can devote less energy to recognition and more to product story.
- Target ranges from household cleaning through personal care to over-the-counter drugs (Target's up & up spans fifteen categories), so check whether one architecture scales across the whole range you intend to cover.
Caveat: Source examples are large chain retailers; the licensing of a 'less energy on recognition' advantage is specific to private label being sold in the retailer's own environment.
Caveat: Costco quote about raising quality, raising price and selling more units is a case example, not a general rule that raising private-label price always increases units.

Tags: brand, private-label, brand-architecture, merchandising, packaging, positioning, retail
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Brand licensing benefits and role split between licensor, licensee and agent
Use when: Deciding whether to license your brand for products sold via other retailers or marketplaces, or recruiting/licensing a property to extend your product assortment.
- Set licensing goals and objectives first, then approve an annual strategic licensing plan — licensing strategy should follow the brand, not precede it. Harley-Davidson's former VP states the company is 'very judicious' and ties licensing to the history and heritage of the brand.
- As licensor, own the brand guardian duties: approve prospective licensees, approve licensed products, packaging, marketing and collateral materials, provide access to licensable assets or develop a style guide, register trademarks in appropriate categories, and pursue trademark infringers.
- As licensee or manufacturer, focus on development and distribution: develop, manufacture and market approved products, monitor the marketplace for infringers, and deliver quarterly royalty reports and payments.
- Use an agent to prospect qualified licensees, negotiate license terms, guide contract management, lead acquisition or development of licensable assets or a style guide, manage product, packaging and collateral approval, administer royalties, and police the marketplace.
- Expect the customer-facing benefit: consumers are more likely to purchase new products from familiar brands (source view), and licensed product gives consumers a legitimate alternative to unauthorized goods while letting them display brand affinity.
- Check the extended-asset universe before assuming licensing is only for consumer or entertainment brands: nonprofits, branded destinations and cultural venues are listed as expanding licensable properties — e.g. a museum licensing images to products for its store.
Caveat: Detailed program mechanics and the top licensor list (Disney, Iconix, Warner Brothers, Marvel, Nickelodeon) are as of the source's writing and will change — verify current market data before using them in planning.
Caveat: Licensing adds approval and policing workload; the source positions that overhead as the licensor's responsibility, not something to delegate entirely to the agent.

Tags: brand, brand-licensing, intellectual-property, distribution, trademark, product-assortment, partnerships
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Certification as a decision shortcut and value alignment for products
Use when: Choosing which certifications to pursue or display on product pages and packaging to reduce purchase friction or support a values-based positioning.
- Map the specific trust question your customer is asking: environmental and social responsibility, product safety, privacy protection, and responsible sourcing (categories listed include green building and products, sustainable business, efficiency, heart-healthy, social justice, no animal testing, data and privacy, product safety, food, responsible forestry, recycling, environmental responsibility).
- Select certifications that map to those questions rather than adding symbols for decoration; qualifying requires rigorous testing by government bodies or professional associations.
- Consider standards that measure a company, not just a product — B Corporation certification requires meeting higher standards of social and environmental performance, accountability and transparency, earning a minimum score on the B Impact Ratings System measuring impact on employees, suppliers, community, consumers and environment, and legally expanding corporate responsibilities to include stakeholder interests.
- Display certifications where purchase decisions happen (packaging, product page) to help consumers distinguish 'good companies' from good marketing.
- As cross-border and global selling grows, prioritize certifications whose symbols are clear and trustworthy across cultures.
Caveat: Certification requirements and the B Impact minimum score have specific current rules — verify the current standard for any certification you pursue rather than relying on this summary.
Caveat: Pursuing certification adds testing and compliance cost; the source frames it as a trust shortcut, not a guaranteed sales lift.

Tags: brand, certification, sales-trust, product-page, sustainability, compliance, positioning
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Crisis communications planning: build the plan, train against it, update it
Use when: Preparing an ecommerce brand's response plan before a product safety issue, recall, fulfillment failure, data breach, or viral complaint escalates into a public crisis.
- Conduct a proactive planning sequence: retain outside communications counsel; organize an internal crisis team with outside communications and legal counsel; audit threats to the organization's reputation; familiarize leadership and run regular training against the plan; participate in a simulated crisis; set up organizational and media monitoring; and track emerging issues.
- Confirm baseline readiness with critical questions: do you have a crisis team leader and team, a plan regularly reviewed, senior leaders familiar with and trained against it, in-house and outside legal counsel in plan development, a protocol for determining a crisis, an assessment of where crises may originate, prepared key messages and FAQs, an identified and trained spokesperson, and considered audiences with the vehicles to reach each.
- Operate the source's imperatives during a live issue: be prepared, be quick (get ahead of the story with your statement first rather than reacting to false or negative information), define the issue before the media or adversaries do, be forthright (acknowledge action steps), be helpful (don't speculate — if you know, say so; if you don't know, say you don't know), and be transparent (monitor, engage and update on a timely consistent basis across traditional and social media).
- Prepare social media specifically: create a social media policy before a crisis, establish a microsite for 24/7 updates, run round-the-clock monitoring, do not delete negative comments on your page or blogs, and train the crisis team on social media.
- Escalate internally: if it's not important to senior management it won't be important to middle or line management, so leadership ownership is required; treat crisis communications as a management issue, not just PR.
- Practice, assess and refine the plan annually.
Caveat: Source is a communications consultancy's framework, not an enforcement standard; it offers no measurable benchmark for response times or outcomes.
Caveat: Social platform interfaces and policies change — verify current tools and rules (microsites, monitoring, comment moderation) when implementing.

Tags: brand, crisis-communications, reputation-management, social-media, risk-management, customer-trust
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Before-and-after rebrand decision: evolutionary vs revolutionary change
Use when: Planning a brand refresh, logo redesign, or packaging redesign and deciding how much to change versus preserve.
- Start from the business imperative: as organizations grow, purpose becomes more lucid, positioning is refined, and stakes shift as new markets open — define what is actually changing in the business before touching visuals.
- Identify what elements must be maintained to preserve brand equity, then decide whether the change should be evolutionary or revolutionary.
- For a large-scale example, Target engaged Wolff Olins to reinvent its own consumables brand spanning fifteen categories from household cleaning to personal care to over-the-counter drugs, with the stated goal of creating new levels of guest loyalty, increasing purchase frequency, and differentiating from national players; the brand had exposure throughout the store.
- Expect the redesign to change shopper attention: Target's EVP of merchandising stated the belief that the new brand would stand out on the shelf and be distinctive enough to attract guests who may not have noticed the Target brand before.
- Note the range of redesign logics in the source examples: positioning shifts (Columbus Salame repositioned to upscale customers; Brooklyn Botanic Garden redesigned to embody a new tagline; ACLU designed to 'look like the guardians of freedom'), identity simplification (Bala, Hot Wheels logo evolved to fit on the cars), architecture change (Santos Brasil monolithic architecture), and freeing a symbol for personal connection (Starbucks).
- For naming changes, require the new name to be concise, memorable, easy to pronounce and globally positive while providing continuity from the previous name.
Caveat: Target's loyalty and frequency goals are stated intentions from the case, not measured outcomes reported in this source.
Caveat: Examples span very different budgets and brand types; treat the before/after rationales as situational, not as a decision rule.

Tags: brand, rebranding, brand-refresh, brand-equity, logo-design, packaging, brand-architecture
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Packaging redesign to shift quality perception and organize assortment
Use when: Redesigning packaging across an SKU range to raise perceived quality, organize variant families, or signal a positioning shift on shelf and in product imagery.
- Connect the design to a positioning story rather than decorating the pack: Lean Cuisine's new design was framed around expressing the brand's happy culinary spirit and helping women eat healthier; Sesmark's identity was rebuilt to tell an all-natural 'health from the heartland' story.
- Use one system to segment the full range. Source states that reimagining the coffee category allowed dramatically increased brand quality perceptions and instant segmentation of all forms, flavors and grinds — apply the same logic when your SKUs multiply across flavors, sizes or formats.
- Make the visual system signal the promise: Quick Chek's private label redesign was tied to a straightforward commitment to fresh convenience; Steaz's identity was designed to disrupt category norms with a contemporary, relevant presence.
- For heritage brands, borrow credible visual codes (19th century engravings and typography for Irving Farm Coffee; a wood engraving and botanical illustrations for a family business) rather than generic modern styling.
- Align packaging with shelf/thumbnail distinctiveness, since the practical ecommerce benefit of package design is recognition in compressed grids and on-shelf.
Caveat: Source examples are design-agency recaps with no measured sales outcomes; the 'dramatically increased quality perceptions' claim is stated without data.
Caveat: Heritage-visual advice is situational — it suits brands with a heritage story and may be wrong for new or technology-led brands.

Tags: brand, packaging, product-page, merchandising, positioning, visual-identity, sku-range
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Personal branding principles applied to founder and operator presence
Use when: Deciding how a founder, operator or store team should show up publicly on social and content channels as part of the brand.
- Treat every digital touchpoint as personal branding: source states that every time a person sends an email it's personal branding, and that blogs have become mainstream for anyone in business as the modern replacement for exchanging business cards.
- Lead with authenticity because the web never forgets — inconsistent or manufactured personas are hard to unwind later.
- Accept that personal branding is now standard business practice, not only for famous figures; source frames it as required for corporate execs, design gurus, aspiring entrepreneurs and sales associates alike.
- Account for structural context: jobs no longer last forever and a third of the workforce is self-employed (source figure), and connection is 24/7, which blurs business and life, work and leisure, public and private.
- Use the six career secrets cited from Daniel Pink as a frame for how to allocate effort: there is no plan; think strengths, not weaknesses; it's not about you; persistence trumps talent; make excellent mistakes; leave an imprint.
- Keep the brand's core definition clear while doing this: brands represent clarity, reassurance, consistency, status and membership, enabling people to define themselves (Wally Olins).
Caveat: The 'one third self-employed' figure is as of the source's writing and should be verified before use.
Caveat: Personal-brand advice here is largely opinion and aphorism, not measurement; use it as framing, not as a tested playbook.

Tags: brand, personal-branding, founder-brand, social-media, authenticity, content
Source: Designing Brand Identity.docx

---

## Brand Strategy as a Creative Filter Before Any Visual Work
Use when: You are starting a brand (or a major brand refresh/own-label launch) and are tempted to jump straight into logo and packaging design.
- Run documented strategy sessions before opening any design tool. Answer four questions explicitly: why should people care, what makes us different, who are we speaking to, and what are we trying to change.
- Write a purpose (e.g. the client's was boosting local economy by encouraging organic farming), a mission (introducing Himalayan flavors to the world) and a long-term vision (making the spices a staple in kitchens globally).
- Set 3-5 non-negotiable core values (in the example: ethical farming, sustainability, authenticity, regional pride). Treat them as decision filters — the source's point is that values constrain design choices rather than decorate a slide.
- Derive the visual direction from those values: the Himalayan example's 'organic and rooted in tradition' brief came directly from ethical farming and regional pride.
- Keep the strategy written down and refer back to it at each approval gate (mood board selection, sketch selection, final asset sign-off) so design debates are settled on alignment, not taste.
Caveat: These are illustrative stages from one chili oil project, not benchmarks.
Caveat: This process covers identity design; audience psychographics belong to a separate research step per the source's own module split.

Tags: brand, brand-strategy, positioning, creative-strategy, visual-identity, core-values
Source: How to Design a Brand Identity - From Start to Finish.docx

---

## Profile Target Consumers by Curiosity and Authenticity, Not Demographics
Use when: You need an audience definition to brief design, copy or ad creative for a specialty food or artisan product launch.
- Move past age/income demographics. Describe the audience by behaviour and worldview instead.
- For this project the audience was defined as 'modern conscious consumers': curious about food, keen to experiment, and caring about quality, authenticity and the story behind what they buy.
- Check that the store's core values mirror that worldview — the source frames alignment between brand values and audience values as the foundation for all later creative and strategic decisions.
