Tags: brand, brand-architecture, line-extension, product-page, brand-strategy, new-product-launch
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Let the Free-Ride Trap Guide How Far You Stretch a Brand
Use when: You're deciding whether to put an existing store or brand name on an adjacent product range, and want to anticipate where the name stops helping.
- Start by defining what your name is currently associated with; that association, not general brand awareness, is the only asset the new product can ride on.
- Estimate how far the association stretches: does the new product share the same customer, occasion, and quality expectation as the parent? If not, the name is unlikely to transfer.
- Budget for the possibility that the extension does not inherit the parent's effect. The book presents the LifeSavers gum case as evidence that a well-known name did not grant a second product a free ride.
- Where the association does not transfer, price and promote the extension on its own merits rather than assuming inherited trust.
- Track sales and repeat purchase of the extension separately from the parent; if repeat is weak, the assumed free ride is not happening.
Caveat: The book's position is a strong one, presented through a single case example; results vary by category and are not guaranteed in either direction.
Caveat: The book itself names exceptions where line extension can work (GE), so treat the free-ride trap as a warning, not a universal law.

Tags: brand, brand-architecture, line-extension, brand-strategy, retention, product-selection
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Use the Teeter-Totter Principle When One Name Risks Standing for Two Products
Use when: You're considering putting an existing brand name on a second, genuinely different product and need to assess the risk to both.
- Check whether the two products are distinctly different in the shopper's mind (category, usage, price tier, target customer). The teeter-totter principle applies to two distinctly different products under one name.
- Assume the pull will go one way: the book states one name cannot stand for two distinctly different products without one going down. Decide in advance which product you are willing to sacrifice.
- Test which product the name currently anchors by asking shoppers what they think of first when they hear the name.
- If both must survive with one name, look for a shared position that genuinely covers both, or split into a house-name/parent structure so the parent carries the brand and each product has its own identity.
- Watch the launch period closely: if sales of the original product dip after the extension launches, treat it as the predicted trade-off, not coincidental noise.
Caveat: The teeter-totter principle is described at the level of a marketing principle, not a tested model; no quantification of the trade-off is provided.
Caveat: 'Distinctly different' is undefined in the source; classify using your own category and customer research.

Tags: brand, brand-architecture, line-extension, positioning, brand-strategy, measurement
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Use Six Questions to Start a Positioning Program
Use when: You're kicking off a positioning or rebrand effort and need a starting checklist before spending on channels.
- Ask the basic scouting question: where is your category on the product ladder in the prospect's mind?
- Then answer the planning question: what position do you want to own, and can the brand, product, and channel support that intent?
- Ask whether your intended position is already owned by a competitor, and if so, how you could reposition around them.
- Ask whether you have the resources to establish and hold the position — the book notes holding a position is an ongoing commitment, not a one-time campaign.
- Ask whether you have the will and conviction to see the position through, including doing nothing when there is no viable position yet.
- Answer the final question honestly: is the intended position consistent with what you are willing to build in the product itself?
Caveat: The book names the six questions but the source excerpt describes Chapter 24 rather than listing them verbatim; treat the phrasing as a faithful reconstruction of the intent, not a quotation.
Caveat: Validate with real shoppers before investing; the checklist structures judgment rather than replacing research.

Tags: brand, positioning, brand-strategy, customer-research, new-product-launch, measurement
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Become an Outside-In Thinker to Hold a Position Over Time
Use when: You're setting up an ongoing positioning program and need the operating mindset that keeps it consistent across launches and campaigns.
- Adopt the outside-in view: ask what the shopper already believes and expects, rather than what the product team wants to communicate. The book's stated key is to work from the prospect's reality, not the product's.
- Accept the discipline requirements the book names: patience (positions take time to establish), courage (you must choose a position and live with it), and strength of character (you must resist temptations to claim everything).
- Adopt the source's external quote — 'never get a second chance to make a first impression' — as a reason to prepare before launching; the first campaign shapes the mental slot.
- Apply to yearly brand planning: choose a small set of consistent claims and reuse them rather than re-creating positioning each cycle.
- Review every new product or campaign against the chosen position before shipping it.
Caveat: These are prescriptions about stance and character, not measurable procedures; they complement but do not replace testing.
Caveat: The source frames this largely through advertising, so adapt the mindset to catalog, packaging, and site copy rather than only paid channels.

Tags: brand, positioning, brand-strategy, creative-strategy, category-entry, customer-research
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Find a Window in the Market and Concentrate on a Narrow Target
Use when: You're planning launch timing and audience selection for a new product or campaign.
- Accept a baseline: a brand is not a broadcast; it competes in a crowded channel where only a tiny fraction of messages get through, so concentration matters more than reach.
- Pick a narrow target segment rather than a wide one; the book frames this as the only hope to score big in the communication jungle.
- Time your launch or repositioning to a receptive moment — the book calls this a window in the mind, noting that customers must be in a receptive state for the message to land.
- Check the ladder before writing creative: if consumers already see you as a category leader or generic term, position yourself relative to that perception rather than contradicting it.
- Use this narrow-segment concentration to buy efficiently — a concentrated claim that reaches fewer, more relevant people can beat a diluted claim that reaches everyone.
Caveat: The book argues segmentation and narrow targeting are more effective than broad reach; this is a stated principle rather than a controlled finding.
Caveat: The phrase 'window in the mind' is the authors' metaphor, not a scheduling rule.

Tags: brand, positioning, acquisition, targeting, media-planning, product-selection
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Distinguish the Product Era, Image Era, and Positioning Era When Choosing Messaging
Use when: You're reviewing your acquisition creative and deciding whether to lead with features, brand imagery, or a positioning claim.
- Recognize the book's historical sequence: the 'product era' centered on a Unique Selling Proposition (Rosser Reeves); this faded as me-too products made a unique product claim hard to sustain.
- Then came the 'image era' (associated with David Ogilvy), where reputation mattered more than a feature; this faded as every company tried to build an image, raising the noise level so only a few succeeded.
- The current 'positioning era', per the authors, demands creating a position in the prospect's mind that accounts for both your own strengths and competitors' positions — strategy over creativity alone.
- For ecommerce: when writing PDP copy, features and image statements should serve a single position rather than stand alone; a feature list with no position is the product era repeated.
- Audit current creative against that structure: does it establish a distinct mental slot that accounts for competitors, or could any competitor run the same ad?
- Expect the position to be the frame around which campaigns are built; the book claims positioning has increasingly replaced slogans as the centerpiece of strategy.
Caveat: The eras are the authors' historical narrative, not an empirical study; treat them as framing devices.
Caveat: Copying a competitor's supposed era position is not the point — the shift is about strategy orientation, not a new ad template.

Tags: brand, positioning, creative-strategy, brand-strategy, marketing-history, acquisition
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Occupy the No. 2 position against the leader
Use when: Choosing positioning for a store or brand entering a category where a dominant competitor already owns the top rung in customer minds (e.g., a new gear shop vs. an established giant).
- Identify who owns the mental top spot in your category — the brand customers name first when thinking of the product.
- Do not position as a smaller version of the leader or claim you're simply 'better'; that invites a head-on comparison you will lose.
- Take the explicit alternative position: state your place relative to the leader (Avis framed itself as 'No. 2' to Hertz rather than trying to out-Hertz Hertz).
- Lean into the underdog sympathy that comes with the No. 2 or challenger stance, since customers root for an honest underdog.
- Keep the position consistent for years; the source's point is that Avis succeeded because it related itself to Hertz, not because it 'tried harder.'
- For an ecommerce store this can mean a PDP/landing-page headline and about-page narrative that names the category leader and defines your brand as the honest alternative.
Caveat: The No. 2 strategy requires an actual recognized leader; if the category has no clear leader, the relationship has no anchor.
Caveat: This is a positioning frame, not proof of sales results — case figures cited come from the source's historical examples, not your market.

Tags: brand, positioning, competitive-strategy, product-page, landing-page, differentiation
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Find an open 'creneau' inside the prospect's mind, not the factory
Use when: Deciding what new product, size, or category to launch or how to angle a product-page headline for a crowded category.
- List the positions already occupied in customers' minds — the established claims competitors own (cheap, premium, large, masculine, morning-use, etc.).
- Search for an empty slot customers would recognize, not a gap in your own product line: the source's Volkswagen 'Think small' worked because the small-car position was unclaimed in the mind, even though other small cars existed.
- Use reverse thinking: if every competitor is going one direction (bigger, fancier), test the opposite (smaller, simpler).
- Test positions along dimensions such as size, price tier, sex/gender coding, age, time of day, distribution channel, or heavy-user usage.
- Avoid the Edsel mistake: a hole in the factory lineup (between two models) is not automatically a hole in the customer's mind.
- Preempt the position with clear, single-minded messaging on the PDP/landing page — preemption matters more than being merely better.
Caveat: The source notes few truly empty positions remain today; many categories require repositioning a competitor instead.
Caveat: A technical achievement or genuinely new product feature can still fail if no mental slot exists for it (the Frost 8/80 'dry white whisky' example).

Tags: brand, positioning, product-selection, merchandising, differentiation, customer-research
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Use price as a positioning claim, stated in advance
Use when: Setting price tier strategy or writing pricing-related copy for a product line or category page.
- Decide whether your category rewards a high-price or low-price position: the source argues low-price positions work well for new or unproven products customers are taking a chance on, and high-price positions work for established products with quality or repair concerns.
- Claim the tier first: the source says success comes from being first to establish the high-price position with a valid product story in a receptive category (citing examples like Michelob at premium and Mobil 1 synthetic oil).
- Communicate the price tier in ads and page copy so there are no price surprises when the customer lands on the product page — price is a feature, stated in advance.
- Use the framing, not just a number: 'the costliest,' 'gourmet,' or premium-language headlines position the tier even when exact prices aren't quoted.
- Run a portfolio across tiers only if each brand gets its own distinct position and name, as with Budweiser/Michelob/Busch.
Caveat: The source explicitly warns that charging high prices is not itself the way to get rich — greed is not positioning.
Caveat: A high price without a position, product story, and receptive category will drive customers away.
Caveat: Retailer discount emphasis can erase low-price opportunities over time (the source's generic-brand point).

Tags: brand, positioning, pricing, offer-design, product-page, merchandising
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Reposition the competitor to create space in the customer's mind
Use when: Entering a saturated category where all obvious positions are taken and you need a wedge for your brand story or comparison content.
- Find a true, verifiable weakness in the competitor's product or story that customers aren't thinking about (Tylenol used aspirin's stomach irritation and allergy risks).
- State the competitor issue first and in detail; Tylenol's ad ran about 60 words on aspirin's downsides before naming its own product.
- Make the prospect change their mind about the competitor's product, not just hear praise for yours — Royal Doulton repositioned Lenox as American-made ('Pomona, New Jersey') against its imported-sounding name.
- Give the customer a simple, self-verifiable fact, not clever logic; Stolichnaya showed that rival 'Russian' vodkas were actually made in Connecticut, Pennsylvania, and Indiana.
- Distinguish repositioning from comparative advertising: 'we're better than X' benchmarks you against the leader and invites 'then why aren't you the leader?' — real repositioning changes the competitor's meaning.
- Apply this in comparison pages, ingredient/label content, or category education — e.g., reading the ingredient list aloud as Wise did against Pringle's.
Caveat: The source acknowledges repositioning draws ethical complaints, though it argues comparative advertising is legal and can be done honestly and fairly.
Caveat: Repositioning can backfire if your own product has an unclear name or weak follow-through — Scope's repositioning of Listerine was undercut by an unmouthwash-like name.
Caveat: Once a product is mentally tagged a loser, the source says recovery is very hard — repositioning works best as an offensive move, not a rescue.

Tags: brand, positioning, competitive-strategy, conversion-optimization, landing-page, comparison-content
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Leader strategy: reinforce the first position instead of repeating 'we're No. 1'
Use when: Writing brand messaging or category pages when your store genuinely owns the leading mental position in a niche.
- Skip 'We're No. 1' copy: either customers know and wonder why you're insecure, or they don't know — which means you defined leadership in your own terms, not theirs.
- Reinforce the original concept that got you into the mind first, as Coca-Cola did with 'the real thing' — positioning the brand as the standard others imitate.
- Sell the category, not just yourself; the source describes IBM's advertising selling the value of computers generally.
- Strengthen with invention claims when true ('we invented the product'), as the source cites for Xerox, Polaroid, and Zippo.
- Expand the uses of the product to widen the category — Arm & Hammer promoting baking soda in refrigerators, or orange juice promoted beyond breakfast.
- Consider broadening the brand or company name when scope changes, following the Haloid to Xerox pattern.
Caveat: Leaders should not read their own ads too admiringly; the source urges covering new developments by adopting promising innovations early or with a multibrand launch.
Caveat: Ignore-competition messaging assumes genuine category leadership in the customer's mind, not self-declared metrics like 'best-selling above $1,000 east of the Mississippi.'

Tags: brand, positioning, merchandising, landing-page, category-leadership, messaging
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Cover competitive moves with a separate brand, not a stretched one
Use when: Deciding whether to launch a new sub-brand, product line, or SKU versus attaching a new variant to your best-selling brand.
- Recognize that once a position is established in customers' minds, changing it is very hard; the source points to Procter & Gamble keeping Ivory as a soap and introducing Tide for detergent rather than 'Ivory Detergent.'
- Give each genuinely new concept a new name and identity — Cascade rather than 'Dishwasher Tide.'
- Follow the pattern of one brand, one position: no Plus, Ultra, or Super add-ons blurring the concept.
- Apply the covering principle to new formats or features before a rival claims them: the source cites General Motors buying a Wankel license as cheap insurance, and Kodak/3M declining the xerography rights as the costly opposite.
- Budget for the possibility that a new brand cannibalizes some of the old one — the source treats that as preferable to wrecking an established position.
Caveat: This is a strategy for brands that already own a strong position; the source warns a follower copying the leader is a 'me-too' move, not covering.
Caveat: Internal resistance is common — management often sees the new product as a competitor rather than protection, so the covering move is hard to sell internally.

Tags: brand, positioning, product-selection, portfolio-strategy, line-extension, retention
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Don't fall into the F.W.M.T.S. trap
Use when: Reviewing brand messaging, campaign themes, or homepage copy that has accumulated multiple taglines over time.
- Audit how many different themes your brand has run; the source notes that despite Avis's many campaigns, the one theme people remember is the original 'No. 2' idea.
- Identify the single concept you actually own in the customer's mind and make it the consistent spine of your messaging.
- Don't advertise aspirations that contradict your position (Avis's 'Avis is going to be No. 1' abandoned the underdog sympathy and No. 2 relationship that was working).
- Reuse the same position across campaigns, product pages, and ads rather than rotating fresh taglines for novelty.
- Accept that a strong position may cap some ambitions — if you can't exploit a real leader weakness, the source says you're not destined to be No. 1 just by wishing it.
Caveat: The source treats consistency as the core positioning discipline; it doesn't argue you can never launch new products, only that the owned position shouldn't be abandoned.
Caveat: Failing to hold your position risks being passed by a competitor, as the source warns about Avis and National.

Tags: brand, positioning, messaging, consistency, creative-strategy, campaigns
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Check whether you can even 'get there from here'
Use when: Deciding whether to enter a new product category, compete with a dominant platform, or fund an aggressive growth goal.
- Identify the entrenched leader and its share (the source cites IBM's 70% of the mainframe business): head-on attacks against a mental leader have no hope of progress.
- Do not mimic the leader's approach — acting like IBM in the source's example is the most common and fatal mistake of bigger challengers.
- Instead, relate your existing owned position to the new category: the source suggested RCA (a communications leader) should position computers around communications, and GE around its time-sharing strength; NCR progressed by focusing on retail data-entry (computerized cash registers).
- Where the situation is genuinely hopeless, redirect resources to areas of your business where you do hold a position; the source argues outright failure is often preferable to mediocre success.
- Distinguish strategic positions where extra effort early builds leadership from ones where no amount of money or can-do spirit closes the gap.
Caveat: The RCA and GE computer outcomes are historical case examples, not predictions about your market; treat them as illustrations of a rule about head-on competition.
Caveat: The source also says extra effort is valuable when applied early to establish leadership — timing matters, so 'can't get there' applies mainly to late, head-on moves.

Tags: brand, positioning, product-selection, competitive-strategy, resource-allocation, market-entry
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Avoid the everybody trap and commit to a specific niche
Use when: Writing category, homepage, or brand copy and choosing whether to keep broad, inclusive messaging or commit to a defined customer segment.
- Accept that being all things to all people can keep an established brand afloat but is deadly when building a position from nothing.
- Choose a specific position along a real dimension (use case, price tier, customer segment, ingredient stance) and state it unambiguously, as Volkswagen did with 'Think small.'
- Accept that taking a strong position makes some enemies or excludes some buyers — the source argues you can't win by stepping on no one's toes.
- Avoid multiple vague value claims competing for space on the same page; one clear position beats a list of universal benefits.
- Recognize the volume of competing messages customers face — the source compares sorting product names to a vocabulary problem, so a single claim is what can register.
Caveat: This is the source's advice for brands trying to build a position; it concedes a broad, already-dominant brand may survive without a sharp niche.
Caveat: The source's argument rests on the overcommunicated-market premise, not on measured conversion data.

Tags: brand, positioning, messaging, customer-research, creative-strategy, niche
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Make the product name do positioning work
Use when: Naming a new product, private label, or sub-brand, or diagnosing why a well-positioned offer underperforms.
- Choose a name that supports the intended position rather than contradicting it — the source argues Scope's repositioning of Listerine was partly undone because 'Scope' sounds like a board game, not a good-tasting mouthwash.
- Avoid house-name extensions for genuinely new concepts, since they trade a quick share of market for a diluted position.
- Prefer names that reinforce the positioning angle, such as Luke's masculine name or 'Old Milwaukee' for a low-priced beer versus the owner's own name on the cheapest brand.
- Remember the source's claim about sensory expectations: people 'taste what they expect to taste,' so a name and copy implying artificiality can make the product itself seem worse (the Pringle's ingredient-list backlash).
- Test the name against the mind's existing associations, not just internal brand logic.
Caveat: The naming failures cited (Scope, Max Factor's 'Just Call Me Maxi') are historical anecdotes presented as illustrations, not controlled evidence.
Caveat: The source treats the name as critical but does not claim a name alone fixes a product with no position.

Tags: brand, positioning, naming, product-selection, product-page, private-label
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Race on launch speed and budget, not late 'better'
Use when: Planning a product or category launch, deciding launch timing, naming, and marketing spend against an incumbent.
- Prioritize being first to establish the position over making a marginally better version; the source says accent should be on speed, not 'better.'
- Give the launch a substantial budget — the source warns the opposite happens: me-too brands waste time, then launch with a smaller budget than the leader's.
- Give the new product its own name rather than the house name, since house names are the easy way to get quick share but a positioning trap.
- If you're late and can't be first, use a follower strategy (alternative position or repositioning the leader) rather than a better-me-too claim.
- Weigh timing explicitly, as the source does when describing Lorillard's late masculine cigarette brand 'Luke' failing about 20 years after Marlboro claimed masculinity.
Caveat: The source's claim about 'first gets twice the long-term share' is presented as a general historical pattern, not a formula or a guaranteed benchmark for your category.
Caveat: This reflects the source's view that first-mover advantage dominates; a truly differentiated late entry can still win, but not by fighting the leader head-on.

Tags: brand, positioning, product-selection, launch-strategy, acquisition, timing
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Turn sensory expectation into a merchandising and copy lever
Use when: Writing product page copy, ingredient lists, or label descriptions for food, supplements, or any product judged by sensory quality.
- Recognize that consumer taste is partly mental: the source notes Pringle's common complaint was 'tastes like cardboard' after rivals read its industrial-sounding ingredient list on TV.
- Keep ingredient and process copy human and concrete; long chemical names invite negative perception even when the product is fine.
- Use the water-vs-'dihydrogen oxide' example as the principle: identical substances can be perceived differently based on the words used.
- When a competitor's formulation is technically fine but sounds unappealing, you can reposition it honestly by focusing attention on what it contains, as Borden's Wise did comparing ingredient lists.
- Pair sensory-expectation copy with the actual product experience so expectations don't outrun reality.
Caveat: The Pringle's outcome is a historical case, not a controlled test; use as illustration, not a guaranteed result.
Caveat: Once a product is widely perceived as a 'loser,' the source says recovering is very hard, so this lever works better ahead of negative perception than after.

Tags: brand, product-page, copywriting, merchandising, positioning, conversion-optimization
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Competitive ads vs. repositioning ads: know the difference
Use when: Planning comparison content, 'us vs. them' pages, or competitor-focused acquisition creative.
- Avoid the 'we're better than X, Y, Z' structure; the prospect reacts with 'then why aren't you the leader?', and you're saying what they already expect you to say.
- Instead, state something about the competitor's product that changes the customer's understanding: Tylenol on aspirin's risks, Royal Doulton on Lenox's American origin, Stolichnaya on rivals' US production.
- Run the competitor-framing portion first and in real detail before mentioning your own product — the source notes Tylenol's roughly 60 words of aspirin copy before naming itself.
- Be factually accurate to keep the tactic honest and defensible; the source cites these as verifiable claims, not invented ones.
- Keep in mind that such claims are legally permitted in many markets (the source traces US TV comparative-ad acceptance to FTC pressure in the 1970s), but verify current rules and platform policies for your market.
Caveat: Repositioning draws ethical objections, which the source acknowledges while defending it as more effective than brag-and-boast.
Caveat: Comparative claims carry legal and platform risk; the source's regulatory timeline is historical and specific rules should be verified with counsel.

Tags: brand, positioning, comparison-content, landing-page, creative-strategy, advertising
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Treat equality between top brands as unstable
Use when: Timing a promotional push or brand investment in a category where you're roughly tied with a rival for the top spot.
- Watch for a genuine dead heat at the top of a category — the source argues it's inherently unstable and typically resolves to a lasting lead (citing Hertz/Avis, Harvard/Yale, and the Ford/Chevrolet race resolved by Chevrolet in 1931).
- Concentrate extra marketing effort during that period of doubt rather than spreading it evenly, since the source says the time for extra effort is when neither side has clear superiority.
- Expect a near-term win to lock in a long-term advantage; the source says winning sales leadership in a single year often clinches victory for decades.
- Recognize leader advantages beyond sales — preferential retail stocking, better hiring, and customer default repurchase habits (buying the same brand as last time) — and account for them in your plan.
- Once ahead, shift from fight to defense: use short-term flexibility to protect the long-term position rather than continuing to spend as if tied.
Caveat: These patterns come from historical consumer-goods and auto examples; the source presents them as general positioning rules, not modern measured benchmarks.
Caveat: The 'twice the long-term share' figure is a source heuristics claim, not a calculation to plan against literally.

Tags: brand, positioning, competitive-strategy, acquisition, retention, timing
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Position by a category dimension before rivals do
Use when: Choosing an angle for a new product, line, or landing-page narrative in a mature category where broad claims are taken.
- Survey the category for unclaimed dimensions: gender coding (Marlboro's masculinity, Virginia Slims' femininity), age (Geritol for older customers, Aim toothpaste for children), time of day (Nyquil as the first night-time cold remedy), distribution (L'eggs in supermarkets), and heavy-user framing (Schaefer for the frequent beer drinker).
- Pick a dimension you can be first to own; the source emphasizes timing is critical and cites late me-toos like Eve and Luke as failures against earlier claimants.
- Consider the counterintuitive direction: the source notes the largest perfume became Charlie, the first brand to use a masculine name with pantsuit advertising, even though most brands were feminine.
- If a rival already owns the dimension, don't copy it — a me-too naming and ad campaign on the same axis tends to fail.
- Translate the chosen dimension into a specific, single-minded claim in the product page headline and imagery.
Caveat: Numeric share claims like Aim's '10 percent of the toothpaste market' are historical examples, not targets.
Caveat: The source's timing claims are anecdotal case comparisons, not controlled tests, so validate demand for the dimension with customers before building around it.

Tags: brand, positioning, product-selection, segmentation, creative-strategy, product-page
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Name as the Core Positioning Decision
Use when: Naming a new product, brand, SKU or storefront, or deciding whether to rename an existing product line.
- Treat the name as the first positioning act, not a cosmetic label. The source (Ries & Trout) argues that in an overcommunicated market the name is the single most important marketing decision, because the mind stores a product by its name.
- Choose a name that begins the positioning process: pick a name that telegraphs the product's major benefit, in the way Head & Shoulders signals dandruff care and DieHard signals long battery life.
- For ecommerce listings, aim for a name that makes the benefit legible in the search result, the cart line and the ad headline — where you have seconds, not a salesperson.
- Avoid names that are lazy, do-nothing or purely internal (project codes, initials, founder surnames) unless the brand is already first and famous in the category.
- Remember that Shakespeare was wrong according to the source: a rose by any other name would not smell as sweet, because people see and smell what they expect to.
- Run a sounding test before committing to a name you plan to say in video, podcast ads or voice search.
Caveat: The source is opinionated and dated; the core claim is a positioning argument, not a measured benchmark.
Caveat: Benefit-led names risk becoming generic if too close to the category (see separate card on that trap).

Tags: brand, positioning, naming, product-selection, creative-strategy
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Descriptive Names Beat Coined Names — Unless You're First
Use when: Deciding between a descriptive name (e.g. Soy Butter, Spray 'n Wash) and a coined or abstract brand name for a new product or store line.
- Prefer common descriptive words that say what the product does, in the tradition of Spray 'n Wash and DieHard; these block me-too competitors from muscling into your territory.
- Reserve coined, meaningless names (the source's examples: Kodak, Keds, Kleenex) for the case where you are first into the mind with an absolutely new product that millions will want.
- Understand that Coca-Cola, Kodak and Xerox succeeded because they were first in their categories, not because the names were invented; the source calls this the Charles Lindbergh syndrome — get in first and any name works.
- For an ecommerce store launching a familiar category (supplements, cookware, apparel basics), use a benefit-descriptive naming pattern rather than abstract invented words, since you are not likely to be first.
- Check letter-frequency as a rough guide: the most common English initial letters are S, C, P, A and T, and the least common are X, Z, Y, Q and K — a name starting with X is far harder to recall.
Caveat: The source's claim that only first-movers can afford coined names is a heuristic, not a tested rule.
Caveat: Descriptive names invite trademark trouble and category substitution (Lite problem).

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, trademark, product-selection
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Don't Let a Name Become Generic
Use when: Naming or trademarking a launch SKU, deciding whether to coin a new word for a new product format, or reviewing a product name that has become a category catch-all.
- Aim for a name that is almost-but-not-quite generic, the way the source describes choosing like driving a racing car — you must take chances and occasionally spin out.
- Watch the cautionary case of 'Lite beer from Miller': the press and public shortened it to 'Miller Lite,' and Miller lost exclusive rights to 'light' or its phonetic equivalent as a trademark for beer.
- If your name is already a category word (e.g. a store brand literally called 'Vitamin C'), the source's warning applies at scale: you have made it easy for competitors to sell under the same literal label.
- When a coined name is genuinely warranted, keep it distinguishable: a strong, generic-like but not-generic name blocks me-too competitors, according to the source.
- Consult trademark counsel before launch on any name that reads as a category description; the source notes trademark lawyers prefer coined marks like Kodak and Xerox.
Caveat: Choice of name is a trade-off: too abstract and the prospect learns nothing; too descriptive and you lose the mark.
Caveat: Legal standards vary by jurisdiction and category; get current local advice.

Tags: brand, naming, trademark, positioning, legal
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Audit Existing Names for Obsolescence and Confusion
Use when: Portfolio review: assessing whether a legacy brand or product name still communicates the current offer, or whether a rename is warranted.
- Check the ladder position, not the aesthetics. The source's Eastern Airlines case: a regional name placed Eastern in the same mental bracket as Piedmont and Ozark, not the national carriers.
- Look for names that no longer match what the product does: United Shoe Machinery, RCA and Esquire all outgrew or lost the thing their name meant.
- Watch for name confusion with a larger competitor. In the source's famous case, B. F. Goodrich was the first to market steel-belted radials in the U.S., yet 56% of buyers named Goodyear (which didn't make them domestically) versus 47% who named Goodrich.
- Check the Toledo triplet / Continental confusion problem: when multiple brands share a common word, prospect research cannot distinguish them; use a name test to measure misattribution.
- Recall that names can carry hidden devalued meaning (the source's example of 'margarine' hiding its soy origin), so test whether the current name withholds or misrepresents what the product actually is.
- Assess whether a rename will actually help. The source says most companies refuse to change because they believe 'our customers and employees would never accept a new name' — but cites Uniroyal, Eaton, Mobil and Exxon as successful renames.
Caveat: Rename case studies are retrospective examples, not guarantees of improved sales.
Caveat: Goodrich opted to keep the name and effectively told customers it was their problem to remember it — a defensible but expensive choice.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, competitive-research, measurement
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Test Names and Headlines for Sound, Not Just Look
Use when: Choosing a store or product name, writing ad headlines or slogans, or preparing a brand name for voice search and audio advertising.
- Read names and headlines aloud before committing; if a name sounds bad, it will feel bad even in print, because the brain translates letters into sound.
- Apply the phonetic shorthand test: RCA (3 syllables) works versus Radio Corporation of America (12); GE works versus General Electric (6); but Ford is rarely FM because Ford is already one syllable.
- For ecommerce, test the name in spoken contexts — audio ads, video UGC, voice search queries — not just in a logo lockup, since ads are increasingly heard.
- Design ad copy for 'radio first' as the source suggests, then adapt to print and display, because the primal medium is sound.
- Watch name collisions that only appear in written form: USM's 'US' doesn't stand for anything, so it sorts into the phone-book front matter where no one looks.
Caveat: Phonetic shorthand rules were developed in an era dominated by radio and print; streaming and app search change the retrieval rules but not the value of a pronounceable name.

Tags: brand, naming, creative-strategy, copywriting, search
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Initials and Acronyms Are a Shortcut Trap
Use when: Deciding whether to brand a new store line, private label or acquisition sub-brand with initials or an acronym, or reviewing a corporate rebrand.
- Apply the cause-and-effect test: the source argues that IBM got rich and famous first (the cause), which made its initials widely understood (the effect). Reversing the sequence does not work.
- Take the source's measured example with caution: a Business Week subscriber survey found average awareness of 'initial' companies at 49% versus 68% for a matched group of 'name' companies, a 19-point gap.
- If you inherit an initial name, verify there is a buried, well-known original name for the initials to trigger; otherwise the mind gets nothing back when it reads the letters.
- Check the telephone-directory (or search-results) behavior of an initial name: MCI sorts 48 pages away from where customers look, and competes with several other MCI-containing companies.
- Prefer a shortened name with phonetic gain over initials. The source contrasts Pan Am (2 syllables, better than PAA) with TWA, where 'Trans World' (2 syllables) is shorter and clearer than the T-Double-U-A it uses.
- Reserve initials for brands that are already household-level famous; even the source concedes ITT and NCR work only because they were well known before they went initial.
Caveat: The 49%/68% awareness survey is a single source study and should be read as illustrative, not a benchmark for your category.
Caveat: Some acronyms are deliberate and work as names (CARE, MADD, Fiat, Sabena), so the rule is 'do the initials add phonetic or mnemonic value?' not 'never abbreviate'.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, measurement, brand-architecture
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Line Extension Blurs a Hard-Won Position
Use when: Deciding whether to launch a new product or category under the existing hero brand name versus creating a new brand or sub-brand, e.g. an existing supplement brand moving into skincare.
- Apply the teeter-totter principle: one name cannot stand for two distinctly different products; when one goes up, the other goes down.
- Recall the source's Bayer case: 'Bayer nonaspirin' never took more than a tiny share of the acetaminophen market, and each extension — Bayer timed-release aspirin, Bayer decongestant cold tablets — undercut the core aspirin position.
- Recall Dial: a large share of the soap market and a very small share of the deodorant market after extending the Dial name.
- Recall Protein 21: from a 13% share of shampoo to 2% after launching hairspray, conditioner and Protein 29 for men.
- For ecommerce, decide pre-launch whether a new product requires a new mental ladder. If the source's logic holds, a genuinely new product needs a new ladder and therefore a new name.
- Weigh the inside-out argument ('our customers know us, they'll accept it, we'll save marketing dollars') against the outside-in view (the prospect thinks in products, not companies).
Caveat: The source argues that Procter & Gamble's no-house-name strategy beat Colgate-Palmolive (roughly twice the business and five times the profit) — a historical comparison, not a guaranteed operating rule.
Caveat: There are exceptions: some brands extend successfully when the new product is genuinely within the same ladder. Run a name-fit test before launching.

Tags: brand, positioning, line-extension, product-selection, merchandising
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Use a Distinctly Named Hero SKU to Hold a Feature
Use when: Naming a flagship SKU or private-label item where you want one benefit to be remembered, e.g. an extended-life battery, a durability claim, or a specific food benefit.
- Name the product for the benefit, not the retailer. In the source's example, the DieHard battery gave the buyer a mental hook for 'long-lasting', while the JCPenney battery gave a weak hook for the 'never needs water' feature.
- Accept the trade-off: the DieHard name makes it hard to remember it is only sold at Sears, but the source argues it is better to establish the position in the prospect's mind first and solve the retail connection later.
- For a store brand, consider whether your retail name is strong enough to hold a functional claim, or whether a differentiated product brand fits the ladder better.
- When naming a bundle or kit, choose a name that conveys the primary benefit rather than a house name that simply identifies who made it.
- Recognize that 'the obvious name is not always the best name'; the source warns that obvious, internal-logic names are a barrier to success.
Caveat: Sears' retail-connection problem is real; a great name cannot fix distribution customers cannot easily reach.

Tags: brand, naming, product-selection, merchandising, private-label
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## New Product, New Ladder, New Name
Use when: Launching a new product line in a new category under a company that already owns a position in another category.
- Ask what the existing brand name stands for in the prospect's mind. If the answer is a specific product (Xerox means copier), the name cannot also mean a new category.
- Recall the Xerox experiment: Xerox bought Scientific Data Systems and renamed it Xerox Data Systems; the ad line 'This Xerox machine can't make a copy' signals the trap, and the company folded the computer operation with an additional $84.4 million write-off.
- Also recall Heinz: it made Heinz mean ketchup, gaining the No. 1 ketchup position, while losing pickle leadership to Vlasic — the other end of the teeter-totter.
- Before launch, ask whether you are prepared to make the hero name mean the new thing. If the answer is no, launch under a new dedicated name.
- Be honest about the corporate-ego problem: the source observes that telling GE not to put GE on a new product is very difficult, which is exactly why the wrong decisions get made.
Caveat: This is the source's opinion, illustrated through historical cases; not every extension fails.
Caveat: Sub-brand naming can capture some, but not all, of the separation benefits described.

Tags: brand, positioning, line-extension, naming, product-selection
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Use Negative Names to Polarize Instead of Hide
Use when: Naming or renaming a product whose origin or formulation is unfamiliar to buyers — alternative ingredients, plant-based or lab-made goods, or a product seen as an imitation.
- Bring the product out of the closet by deliberately naming the origin, as in the source's prescription that margarine should have been called 'soy butter' rather than hiding its source.
- The source's logic: because the origin of margarine is hidden, the prospect assumes something is wrong with it; a name that names the source allows a long-term program selling the advantages of soy butter versus cow butter.
- Apply the same principle to corn sweeteners, which the source says were hampered by names like 'high-fructose corn syrup'; renaming to 'corn sugars' let corn stand on equal footing with cane and beet.
- For an ecommerce store selling a product category that suffers from an imitation perception, build the origin into the brand name and the PDP headline rather than burying it in the 'About' section.
- When fighting an entrenched competitor term (the source cites 'fair trade' laws), do not try to rename the competition — that only confuses the audience. Turn the name around: 'Fair to the trade, but unfair to the consumer.'
Caveat: Renaming an entrenched concept early — before the powerful name takes root — is much more effective than late; the source notes 'price maintenance' as a blocking strategy would probably have worked only early.
Caveat: Some origin words carry stigma the brand may not be able to neutralize; validate with real customer language before committing.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, product-page, copywriting
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Protect a Position You Already Own
Use when: Portfolio and pricing decisions when a hero brand or hero SKU is being considered as a vehicle for a new offer, bundle or adjacent category.
- Identify the position the name actually owns — the generic substitute customers use in speech ('hand me a Kleenex', 'get me a Xerox copy').
- Note that generic-strong brands can promote the category, not the brand ('Coffee keeps you awake? Drink Sanka'), which is an efficiency advantage worth protecting.
- Beware of teaching the prospect that your brand is 'nothing but a brand name'. Each line extension, according to the source, destroys the illusion that the brand is a superior form of the product.
- For subscription and replenishment stores, keep the hero position on the hero product and avoid using it as a banner for consumables of a different job-to-be-done.
- If you must grow into a new category, create a new brand or an explicitly distinct sub-brand rather than diluting the core name.
Caveat: The source is strongly anti-extension; some brands do extend profitably, so treat this as a risk framework rather than an absolute ban.

Tags: brand, positioning, line-extension, pricing, retention
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Spend Publicity Like a Budget, Not a Habit
Use when: Planning a PR push, press outreach or influencer campaign for a new store, brand or hero product, and deciding when to use your best story.
- Conserve anonymity until you are ready to spend it, then spend it big, keeping the objective on a position in the prospect's mind rather than on coverage for its own sake.
- Recognize that media hunger is for the new and fresh face; an unknown company with an unknown product has more to gain from publicity than a well-known company with an established product.
- Note the source's caution that nothing kills the publicity potential of a product quite as much as a cover story in a national magazine — repeated exposure without a purpose burns the asset.
- For an ecommerce launch, time the one big reveal around the moment a purchase decision is actually possible (site live, inventory ready), not months before.
- Use the momentum of a media moment to convert — landing page, offer and stock — rather than treating coverage as the goal.
Caveat: The source treats publicity as finite, which may understate the compounding effect of a well-run content channel.

Tags: brand, positioning, acquisition, pr, launch
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Use House Names and Separate Names According to Provenance
Use when: Deciding whether an acquired brand or a newly developed product should carry the parent store/brand name, after an acquisition or a category expansion.
- Know the pattern the source describes: internally developed products usually get the corporate name (General Electric computers), while acquired products usually keep their name (Hertz under RCA, Avis under ITT) — though Xerox broke the pattern by renaming Scientific Data Systems.
- Treat the pattern as a default born of corporate ego, not as a decision rule derived from the customer's mind.
- For an ecommerce operator buying a DTC brand, do not automatically rename the acquired brand to the parent; consider whether the acquired name holds a distinct position the parent name cannot serve.
- If the parent name clearly dominates in search, subscription recognition or ad efficiency, weigh a deliberate rename against the position the acquired name already owns.
- Account for conglomerate logic honestly: the source credits conglomerates with funding competition, but that business benefit does not by itself justify a naming decision.
Caveat: The source's naming analysis is drawn from large industrial and packaged-goods history; small ecommerce portfolios may have different trade-offs.

Tags: brand, brand-architecture, naming, acquisition, positioning
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Position Against the Future, Not the Past, When Renaming
Use when: Renaming a store, a legacy product line, or a brand after a pivot, product obsolescence or a merger.
- Diagnose the obsolescence honestly: U.S. Rubber sold many non-rubber products, Eaton Yale & Towne was a merger with a complicated name, and Socony-Mobil was saddled with a Standard Oil legacy name.
- Do not take the easy abbreviation route; the source calls USR Corporation, EY&T Company and SM Inc. three marketing monstrosities.
- Adopt a clean new name rather than initials. Uniroyal, Eaton and Mobil came from 'forgetting the past' and positioning against the future.
- Expect a transitional cost but note the source's claim that there is only negative equity in a bad name — when the name is bad, things tend to get worse.
- Ask the same question about a store that started in one category: is the store name now a USM-style liability, and would a name change produce a cleaner position?
Caveat: Renaming is rare in practice because companies believe they have too much equity in the current name; the source counter-cites Olin, Mobil, Uniroyal, Xerox and Exxon as companies that made the change work.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, rebrand, strategy
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Use the Shopping List Test to Diagnose Brand Dilution
Use when: Deciding whether to launch a new product category, sub-brand, or SKU under your existing store/brand name, or evaluating whether an existing catalog is already diluted.
- Write down your brand name alone on a list, the way a customer would. Ask: if someone read just this name, is there exactly one product they would return with?
- Contrast with strong names: 'Kleenex', 'Bayer', and 'Dial' pass because they map to one product each. 'Heinz', 'Scott', and 'Kraft' fail because the reader cannot tell whether they mean ketchup, pickles, or baby food.
- Apply this to your own catalog: list your brand plus each category you sell under that same name. If customers cannot predict the product from the name alone, the name has no independent position.
- Note the source's claim that the damage is slow and cumulative — the brand becomes a 'burned-out hulk' over years, not quarters, so a single successful quarter of extension sales does not disprove the problem.
- Treat 'what's a [brand]?' as the diagnostic question: if the answer is 'it's everything and nothing', you have a positioning problem to fix before extending further.
Caveat: The source is a 1980s packaged-goods argument; its share-loss examples are illustrative, not benchmarks for your store.
Caveat: A broad house name works fine for low-involvement commodity items where shoppers have no going-in preference — the test matters most where you want to own a category.

Tags: brand, positioning, line-extension, naming, category-strategy, merchandising, customer-research
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Line Extension's Short-Term Sales Illusion
Use when: Interpreting early sales data after launching a new SKU under an existing brand name, or setting expectations with a founder before a sub-brand launch.
- Recognize that a name related to the original ('Diet Coca-Cola', 'Alka-Seltzer Plus') produces an instant flash of understanding and an instant flash of sales.
- Separate pipeline from demand: retail buyers stocking the new SKU produce good numbers in the first months regardless of consumer pull. The source notes that booking $1 million only requires $35 of stocking per supermarket.
- Watch the reorder curve as the real signal. When reorders do not arrive, the source says things turn dark quickly.
- Expect the extended name to be forgettable because it has no independent position — the source calls such names satellites to the original, contributing only blur.
- For an online store, the analog is a variant or bundle launched under a hero product's name: check repeat purchase and direct search for the new item, not just launch-week sell-through.
Caveat: The '$35 per supermarket' figure is the source's illustrative arithmetic for physical retail distribution, not a benchmark for ecommerce.
Caveat: Anti-extension argument, not a blanket ban; the source explicitly allows extension when there is no competition and no intention to build a position.

Tags: brand, positioning, line-extension, measurement, merchandising, retention, product-launch
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Bayer and Protein 21: How Sub-Brands Undercut the Parent
Use when: Considering whether to attach your main brand name to an adjacent product line, ingredient variant, or differently-positioned version.
- Apply the contradiction test: if the brand means aspirin, 'Bayer nonaspirin' cannot logically exist in the customer's mind. Each extension — timed-release, decongestant, nonaspirin — undercuts the aspirin position.
- Track the share consequence the source reports: Bayer's total analgesic share kept falling as the line extended.
- Use Protein 21 as the cautionary arc: a shampoo/conditioner that reached a 13% share of the shampoo market, then extended into hairspray, conditioner, concentrate, and Protein 29 for men, and fell to 2%.
- Translate to a store: if your brand means one ingredient or one job, do not put that name on a product with a contradictory job. Give the new job a new name.
- Treat the share declines as the source's historical examples, and check your own category data before concluding extension caused it.
Caveat: Share figures are historical packaged-goods examples from the source, not predictive benchmarks.
Caveat: The source also warns the decline is 'bound to continue' — an opinion about trajectory, worth testing rather than assuming.

Tags: brand, positioning, line-extension, naming, category-strategy, product-launch
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Broadening the Base: Reverse Line Extension
Use when: Looking for growth from an existing hero product without launching a new SKU, or planning messaging and landing-page angles for a narrowly perceived product.
- Identify a product whose current perception limits its audience but whose actual attributes fit a bigger one. Johnson's baby shampoo was promoted for its mildness to the adult market and became a leading adult shampoo.
- Keep product, package, and label unchanged. The source states that a separate 'Johnson's adult shampoo' extension would not have been nearly as successful.
- Find the sole unclaimed usage claim, as Blue Nun did by advertising a white wine as good with meat as well as fish. The source argues these work because each is the first and only brand making that claim.
- Apply to ecommerce: rewrite PDP copy, ad creative, and use-case imagery to promote the additional occasion or audience for the same SKU, rather than creating a duplicate listing under a new name.
- Check that no competitor already owns the broadened claim before investing; the source attributes the success partly to being first with it.
Caveat: Source's examples are beverages and personal care; the mechanism (usage broadening) transfers, the results do not.
Caveat: Advertising-intensive: the source ties these wins to sustained promotion, so a silent PDP edit alone is unlikely to shift perception.

Tags: brand, positioning, product-page, creative-strategy, merchandising, retention
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## The Bartender Test for Step-Up and Step-Down Products
Use when: Deciding whether a premium tier or a cheaper entry model should carry your existing brand name, and how to name tiers in a catalog or subscription ladder.
- Run the bartender test on your own brand: order it the way a customer would. 'J&B on the rocks' reliably gets scotch; 'Chivas' reliably gets the premium version. 'Cutty' does not tell the bartender whether you mean Cutty Sark or Cutty 12.
- For step-up products, the source observes they usually produce anemic sales at the higher price — buyers do not want to pay premium prices for a low-price name. Chivas Regal outsold the earlier Johnnie Walker Black Label.
- For step-down products, the source observes near-instant success followed by a hangover: Packard's inexpensive Clipper was its best-selling car and destroyed the prestige position that kept the company alive.
- The exception: Vaseline Intensive Care works because customers simply call the product 'Intensive Care' — in the mind, Vaseline is petroleum jelly and Intensive Care is hand lotion. A separate name in the customer's mouth is what saves it.
- Store application: name premium tiers so customers can say the tier name alone, and avoid slapping the hero brand on a cheap entry SKU that pays the bills short-term but erodes pricing power.
Caveat: The step-up and step-down patterns are the source's observations from liquor and autos, stated as tendencies, not laws.
Caveat: The Vaseline case shows extension can survive when the extension name is what people actually say — verify this through customer language, not internal naming documents.

Tags: brand, positioning, pricing, line-extension, naming, merchandising, subscriptions
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## When to Use the House Name: Five Rules of the Road
Use when: Deciding naming and brand architecture for a new product launch, collection, or private-label item in a multi-product catalog.
- Expected volume: potential winners should not bear the house name; small-volume products should.
- Competition: in a vacuum a brand should not bear the house name; in a crowded field it should.
- Advertising support: big-budget brands should not bear the house name; small-budget brands should.
- Significance: breakthrough products should not bear the house name; commodity products such as chemicals should.
- Distribution: off-the-shelf items should not bear the house name; items sold by sales reps should.
- Note the underlying reasoning: products with no going-in preference in the shopper's mind will do better under any known name than an unknown one, and companies with thousands of small-volume products cannot afford a new name for each.
- Store translation: a flagship, ad-supported, differentiated hero SKU likely earns its own name; a long-tail commodity SKU can ride the house name.
Caveat: These are the source's stated heuristics, framed as 'an economics call as much as a judgment call' — apply with your own volume and margin data.
Caveat: The rules assume a world where advertising budget and distribution channel differ by product; re-derive them for your channel mix.

Tags: brand, positioning, naming, line-extension, product-launch, merchandising, private-label
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## First-Mover Position and the 100mm Cigarette
Use when: You are early into a new product category, format, or claim and choosing how to name and describe it in your store and ads.
- Identify whether you are genuinely first with a new concept, format, or benefit, as Pall Mall Gold was the first 100mm cigarette and Mazola was the first corn-oil margarine.
- Do not dilute the first-mover claim by attaching the incumbent house name to it. The source argues Pall Mall's line-extension thinking let Benson & Hedges preempt the long-cigarette position, and Fleischmann's now leads corn-oil margarine.
- Name the new concept so the name itself carries the idea. Taster's Choice beat Maxim 2-to-1 even though General Foods invented freeze-dried coffee, partly because 'Maxim' connoted nothing and 'Taster's Choice' plus 'tastes like ground roast' related the product to a standard of excellence.
- Apply the same to a store: a genuinely new format or benefit deserves a distinct, benefit-connoting name and copy, not a variant SKU of a legacy bestseller.
- Watch the share trajectory after extending: Pall Mall went from No. 1 in 1964 with 14.4% share to 3.8%, per the source, following its first extension in 1965.
Caveat: All share figures are the source's historical examples, not forecasts for your category.
Caveat: The source acknowledges that leading brands like Marlboro and Winston also extended — it frames the outcome as a tendency, not an inevitability.

Tags: brand, positioning, naming, product-launch, first-mover, creative-strategy, category-strategy
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## The Three-Act Pattern of a Positioned Brand's Decline
Use when: Reviewing an expanding product line or variant count, or planning a range extension for a category-leading product.
- Act One: a big breakthrough from finding a wide-open slot and exploiting it — Volkswagen with 'Think small' and the small-car position. The source notes the brand rose above name to become a lifestyle statement for a practical, no-nonsense buyer.
- Act Two: greed and visions of unending success lead to extending the same reliability and quality to bigger, more expensive cars, buses, and jeep-type vehicles.
- Act Three: the reversal. The source reports Volkswagen fell from first among imported cars to fourth, behind Toyota, Datsun, and Honda.
- Diagnose your own line: count the models or variants carrying the hero name. The source asks whether eight models can sell as well as one — and answers that in this case they did not.
- Use 'what's a [brand]?' on your own expanded line. If the answer is 'a big, small, cheap, expensive car' (the source's Chevrolet critique), the range has entered the everybody trap.
- If breadth is needed, the source's alternative is a new concept with a new position and a new name.
Caveat: The Volkswagen decline is the source's example, with its own era's competitive context, not a general law about range width.
Caveat: Range breadth can be right for some retailers; the source's argument targets brands attempting to own a position in the customer's mind.

Tags: brand, positioning, line-extension, assortment, category-strategy, merchandising, product-launch
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Name as Rubber Band: How Far to Stretch
Use when: Deciding how many product categories one brand name should cover, versus launching a separate brand or holding a category-specific name.
- Accept that the stretch limit is an economics question, not purely a judgment call: putting a separate name on peas, corn, and string beans would not be economical, so Del Monte using one name across canned fruits and vegetables is probably right.
- Watch for the focused attacker. Dole versus Del Monte in canned pineapple is, in the source's words, 'no contest' — the specialist wins.
- Run the teeter-totter check: if Dole makes the name mean bananas, what happens to the pineapple franchise? Extending one meaning trades against another.
- Decide consciously whether you want full-line supplier status or a category franchise. The source says Dole can become full-line only at the expense of its valuable pineapple franchise, and with the added disadvantage of being the last to extend.
- For a store: if a category has a credible specialist competitor, holding a category-specific name or sub-brand for that category protects the position better than folding it into a general brand.
Caveat: Del Monte and Dole conclusions are the source's market observations, not universal rules.
Caveat: The source explicitly tolerates broad names for commodities with no meaningful brand preference.

Tags: brand, positioning, category-strategy, naming, line-extension, merchandising, competitive-analysis
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Diagnose Star Products Whose Sales Outrun Their Position
Use when: Reviewing whether a category-leading SKU in your catalog actually owns a distinct claim, or launching an innovation that looks like a variant of an existing product.
- Separate market share from share of mind, as the source does for Scott: leading the paper-products market did not mean owning a position, and Charmin eventually displaced ScotTissue.
- Test whether the customer can name which product does which job: the source asks which brand is engineered for the nose, Scotties or ScotTissue — the answer is unclear, so the name sits in limbo.
- When you have a real innovation hidden inside a familiar name, rename it. Jergens Extra Dry was a creamlike lotion in an era of liquid lotions, but the similar name meant prospects did not recognize the difference; Chesebrough-Pond's Intensive Care named and positioned it clearly and took the lead.
- Watch for the specialist's opening: if a competitor clearly names and positions the difference you buried, the source says the marketing victory goes to them, and a late counter-brand (Direct Aid) may be too little, too late.
- Even a line-extended winner here is called by its extension name — customers say 'Intensive Care', not 'Vaseline' — so check what customers actually call your product.
Caveat: History is the source's packaged-goods examples, not a measured forecast.
Caveat: The lesson is about distinct position, not merely renaming: a rename without a real, communicated difference will not carry the position.

Tags: brand, positioning, naming, product-page, creative-strategy, competitive-analysis, measurement
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Position the Destination, Not the Vehicle (Country and Company Positioning)
Use when: Marketing a marketplace, region, or store category where demand must be grown before your own product can sell, including multi-seller marketplace or regional seller positioning.
- Find the destination-level problem before optimizing your own offer. Sabena sold food and service while only one in fifty North Atlantic passengers flew to Belgium at all; the source argues no amount of food advertising induces a flight to somewhere the traveler does not want to go.
- Do what KLM had done for Amsterdam: make the place a destination people want to spend time in, rather than a place they pass through.
- Build the position on a perception already in the prospect's mind. The 'three-star cities of beautiful Belgium' campaign anchored to Amsterdam, which travelers already knew.
- Borrow credibility from an authority already trusted. The source credits the Michelin Guide (which rated five Belgian cities three-star 'worth a special journey') with making the claim believable.
- Make the offer sufficient for a multi-day decision. The source notes nobody treats Monaco as a destination because Monte Carlo can be seen in an evening — five cities made Belgium a bona fide destination.
- For a store: if your category requires a habit or occasion the customer does not yet have, market the occasion, then your product as the best execution of it.
Caveat: The Belgium program, per the source, was not ultimately executed because new management did not commit — the source treats that as its own lesson about long-term commitment.
Caveat: This is an agency case from the source's era; the transferable mechanism is anchoring to existing perceptions, not the specific media plan.

Tags: brand, positioning, marketplace, creative-strategy, landing-page, customer-research, category-strategy
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Avoid Cliché Visuals That Blur Your Category
Use when: Planning ad creative, PDP imagery, or a landing-page hero where your category's visuals look identical to competitors'.
- Audit your category's standard imagery. The source notes the couple on the beach under palms is a visual cliché for the Caribbean, and asks whether a customer can keep Nassau, the Virgin Islands, and Barbados separate in mind. If not, the mind files them under 'islands' and tunes out.
- Break with the cliché using something already meaningful, as Jamaica's neighbor Bermuda did with motorbikes beside pink sand — an enduring, effective visual.
- Design a distinctive device that carries your position's meaning. For Belgium, Michelin stars were used as if they were church bells ringing as they were superimposed on city scenes.
- Reach for moving image when the position is visual: the source says television drives pictures of a place into the mind faster than print. Adapt for ecommerce by using video where the position is pictorial.
- Warn against the same blur in other formats: quaint European villages or smiling residents waving mugs of beer fail for the same reason as the palms.
Caveat: The Bermuda visual is cited as effective and long-running, not as a measured lift.
Caveat: The Belgium television work is described by the source as generating substantial response, but the program was not fully deployed — treat response as directional.

Tags: brand, positioning, creative-strategy, advertising-creative, landing-page, product-page, differentiation
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Positioning Must Be Sustained by Committed Management
Use when: Setting positioning or rebranding direction for a store and deciding how to protect it through team, agency, or platform changes.
- Treat positioning as a multi-year commitment. The source's Belgium program failed not because the concept was weak but because new management arrived uncommitted and headquarters changed strategy.
- Write the position down with the reasoning and the evidence (the shopping-list test result, the anchor perception, the credibility source) so a successor inherits the argument, not just the slogan.
- Note the source's own framing: whether it's a corporation, a church, an airline, or a country, a successful positioning program requires major long-term commitment by the people in charge.
- Build in a re-check rather than a rewrite: the source says you must constantly keep your position targeted to today's problems and today's markets, which is different from abandoning it for fashion.
- For an ecommerce operator with high founder or agency turnover, document the position in the brand guide and tie creative, naming, and range decisions to it.
Caveat: The source's evidence is a case study where the program was abandoned; it demonstrates the risk, not a measured effect of commitment.
Caveat: The source acknowledges this is difficult in a constantly changing political environment — the same applies to fast-changing platform and marketing environments.

Tags: brand, positioning, brand-strategy, creative-strategy, team-process, differentiation
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Third-Leg Strategy: Grow From the Position You Own
Use when: Deciding which adjacent category, product line, or feature set to enter when your store already owns one strong position and a bigger competitor owns the adjacent one.
- Map the market as legs. The office had three: telephones (AT&T), typewriters/computers (IBM), and copiers (Xerox). Ask which leg you currently own and whether new technology has changed what that leg means.
- Do not attack the leg you do not own. The source notes Xerox failed at computers, workstations, and local area networks (IBM's leg), and IBM failed at copiers (Xerox's leg).
- Find what your own leg has become. The copier leg of the future is the output leg: printers, scanners, storage devices.
- Look for a technology that has already earned credibility elsewhere to attach to that leg — the laser, in the source's account, had proven itself in communication, medicine, retail checkout, and consumer disk players.
- Name the new capability so it connects to your existing meaning: 'lasography' sounds new and different, sounds related to xerography, and uses a leading-edge technology, so it broadens the existing position instead of abandoning it.
- For a store: extend from your owned position outward (e.g., from one category you genuinely own into adjacent formats for the same job), not into a category where a dominant specialist already owns the customer's mind.
Caveat: Xerox's attempts are recounted as failures by the source; this is a strategic proposal made at the time, not a documented success.
Caveat: The leg framework reflects that era's office market; the reasoning about avoiding the leader's turf transfers, the specific technologies do not.

Tags: brand, positioning, category-strategy, differentiation, product-launch, brand-strategy, competitive-analysis
Source: Positioning_ The Battle for Your Mind_ The Battle for Your Mind (1).docx

---

## Corporate Clarity: What Does Your Company Name Stand For?
Use when: Positioning your store or company as a whole — for recruiting, supplier relationships, or investor conversations— rather than positioning one product.
- Check whether your name locks you to a past reputation. The source notes Pullman is barely a factor in railroad cars and Greyhound's buses keep it perceptually stuck despite millions telling the financial community it is 'more than a bus company'. If you want a broader meaning, the source argues you need a new name, not corporate advertising against the existing one.
- Pick a quality that cuts across all your offerings, as Ford settled on 'innovation' ('Ford has a better idea') since it builds all types and sizes.
- Avoid a people-based platform. The source argues most people assume bigger, more successful companies have better people, so claiming better people from a non-top position creates an inconsistency the prospect resolves against you.
- Avoid 'diversification' as the theme. The source states positions are built on major achievements, not broad product lines, and cites GE as known for electrical manufacturing rather than diversified goods — plus stock markets undervaluing conglomerates.
- Beware positions so broad they are meaningless: the source cites a company describing itself as 'a developer and supplier of information systems for work, education, and entertainment' — Bell & Howell.
- Apply to a store: if you cannot state what your company name means in one specific way inside your category, tighten it before recruiting or supplier campaigns.
