Caveat: Change can be trivial (1% market share shift) or profound, but the definition must be specific and real.
Caveat: A vague mission fails because it gives you a convenient excuse in moments of despair.

Tags: brand, positioning, strategy, offer-design, mission
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Use 'it's not for you' to filter and focus
Use when: When writing brand copy, choosing who to target in ads, and deciding how to handle critical reviews.
- Accept that every best-selling book on Amazon has at least a few one-star reviews; work that matters can't please everyone.
- Use the comedian's dilemma: a comic bombing in New York may simply be performing for an Italian tour group who don't understand English—maybe the work failed, or maybe it wasn't clear who it was for.
- Instead of sanding off the edges to fit the mass audience, keep your focus on the smallest viable audience you originally set out to serve.
- Apply it in practice: say 'I made this for you. Not for the other folks, but for you.'
Caveat: The source's framing is that respecting someone means not wasting their time pandering to them.
Caveat: Distinguish 'not for you' from dismissing legitimate product-quality problems.

Tags: brand, positioning, targeting, advertising, copywriting
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Substitute 'students' for 'prospects and customers'
Use when: When designing onboarding emails, content marketing, tutorials, and retention touchpoints.
- Replace 'prospects' and 'customers' with 'students' in your internal framing.
- Ask: where are your students? What will they benefit from learning? Are they open to being taught? What will they tell others?
- Adopt the student–mentor relationship of enrollment, choice, and care—not the student–teacher relationship of testing and compliance.
- Ask yourself what you'd want to be taught and what you'd teach if given the chance.
Caveat: This is a reframing device, not a claim that customers want to be lectured.
Caveat: Keep the emphasis on enrollment and mutual choice.

Tags: brand, retention, content-strategy, lifecycle, relationship
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Apply the Open Heart Project's eight-part growth system
Use when: When growing a subscription, membership, or community-driven store and deciding how to deepen customer relationships.
- Start with empathy to see a real need: not 'how can I start a business?' but 'what would matter here?'
- Focus on the smallest viable market: how few people could find this indispensable and still make it worth doing?
- Match the worldview of the people being served—tell the story they want to hear in language they're eager to understand.
- Make it easy to spread: if every member brings one more, within a few years you'll have more members than you can count.
- Earn and keep attention and trust; offer ways to go deeper; instead of looking for members for your work, do work for your members.
- At every step, create and relieve tension as people progress toward their goals; show up often with humility, focusing on the parts that work.
Caveat: The 20,000-member result and one-person staff are case-study specifics, not guarantees.
Caveat: Membership depth requires ongoing content and connection.

Tags: brand, retention, community, lifecycle, content-strategy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Build a generous beacon, not a selfish USP
Use when: When defining your brand's differentiating position and best-for claim in a crowded category.
- Accept that 'better' isn't transitive for subjective categories: an Hermès bag is more expensive than Louis Vuitton, but that doesn't make it 'better' in any absolute sense.
- Work out who remembers your brand as the best for what: someone who cares about sustainability and price has a slot for their favorite brand, and their neighbor who cares about status and luxury has a different one.
- Send up a signal flare, a generous beacon, so people looking for you can find you easily.
- Frame everything as 'we're this, not that,' rather than a self-serving USP.
Caveat: Better isn't up to you; taste matters and everyone else may disagree.
Caveat: Expense and similar measurable attributes aren't always the dimensions people care about.

Tags: brand, positioning, merchandising, advertising, differentiation
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Use storytelling, connection, and experience as core tools
Use when: When planning content, community, and post-purchase experience instead of chasing new channel tactics.
- Tell true stories made true by your actions, products, and services, so they hold up over time.
- Make connections: humans are lonely and want to be seen and known, and want to be part of something safer and more fun.
- Create experiences with intent—using a product, engaging with a service, calling customer service, going to a rally—each action is part of the story and builds connection.
- Treat marketing as all of it: what you make, how you make it, and who you make it for, and the effects and side effects, the pricing and profit, all at once.
Caveat: Stories must be true, made true by actions, not inflated claims.
Caveat: This requires organizational buy-in because marketing is everyone's job.

Tags: brand, content-strategy, community, experience-design, retention
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Recognize that marketing shifts from ads to empathy in a world of tiny whispers
Use when: When allocating budget between paid interruption ads and building a brand that earns discovery, trust, and spread.
- Recognize that TV and radio were built to hold ads, but the internet is a billion tiny whispers—selfish conversations that rarely include you.
- Stop assuming you can steal attention cheaply the way previous generations did with mass media.
- Instead of asking 'how do I get the word out?' first, do the other part first: understand the customer's worldview and desires so you can connect.
- Spend on getting discovered last, not first; see what others see, build tension, align with tribes, and create ideas that spread.
Caveat: This is Godin's directional claim about media change, not a blanket ban on all paid advertising.
Caveat: Paid acquisition can still work; it's just no longer the reliable default it once was.

Tags: brand, acquisition, advertising, strategy, positioning
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Wait for better is not selling with a rational argument
Use when: When crafting comparison claims, product superiority messaging, and promotional copy.
- Accept that when a marketer says 'this is better,' they actually mean 'this is better for someone and it might be better for you.'
- Identify which dimension matters to your segment (sustainability, price, status, taste), and claim that dimension rather than a vague superiority.
- Note the Beer Advocate case: 250 beers have earned 3,400+ ratings each, and each is someone's favorite—taste matters and everyone else can be wrong.
- Avoid linear A > B > C reasoning on subjective attributes like stylish or fashionable.
Caveat: Expense is easy to measure but more of it isn't always better.
Caveat: Claims of superiority in your segment are not universal truths.

Tags: brand, positioning, copywriting, advertising, differentiation
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Treat every purchase as a bargain the buyer thinks they're getting
Use when: When setting prices and constructing offers, and when writing nonprofit, donation, or premium pricing pages.
- Use the nonprofit fundraising example: if the fundraiser thinks a $1M ask is crazy based on her own worldview, the donation won't happen; the donation is for the donor, who gets $2M worth of joy, status, and satisfaction, and that's fine.
- Accept that everything we purchase is a bargain—we bought it because it was worth more than we paid.
- If you're unwilling to have empathy for the narrative of the person you seek to serve, you're withholding a valuable option.
- If they understand the offer and decline, that's okay—it's not for them today, not at this price, not with that structure.
Caveat: Empathy for the buyer's narrative is not the same as overpricing or manipulation.
Caveat: A declined offer is not always a failure of the seller.

Tags: brand, pricing, offer-design, customer-research, copywriting
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Market to the human condition as Godin defines it
Use when: When researching customer motivations, writing brand voice guidelines, and designing experiences for return customers.
- Internalize sonder: everyone around you has an internal life as rich and conflicted as yours.
- Assume everyone thinks they are right, has suffered affronts, is afraid, feels lucky, wants to contribute, wants things they can't have, is lonely, insecure, and a bit of a fraud—and cares about something.
- Stop trying to do marketing 'to' people by insisting they get with your program; dance with them instead.
- Use this to design offers that meet people where they actually are.
Caveat: This is Godin's characterization of the human condition, not a research finding.
Caveat: Avoid overgeneralizing from it to override specific customer research.

Tags: brand, customer-research, positioning, experience-design, copywriting
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Position on a Two-Axis Map, Not on Competitors' Claims
Use when: Choosing brand positioning, category angle, or PDP messaging when your store sells in a crowded category (snacks, supplements, apparel, tools) and you need a defensible reason to be chosen.
- List all alternatives your customer could consider (not just direct competitors): other brands, other store types, marketplaces, DIY, doing nothing.
- Pick two axes that customers actually care about, e.g. price vs. status, convenience vs. security, purity vs. indulgence, speed vs. sustainability. The source offers a long menu: performance, ingredients, maintenance cost, safety, edginess, distribution, trendiness, privacy, professionalism, elitism.
- Plot every alternative on the grid. If several cluster in the same spot, that quadrant is crowded and hard to win without a big ad budget.
- Choose a spot on an edge where few offerings sit, and make sure you can actually deliver it operationally (spec, sourcing, service, returns).
- Rewrite your homepage, product page and ad copy around that single claim. Choose your extremes and you choose your market, and vice versa.
- Re-run the exercise with different axes periodically; the same category can be re-mapped many ways.
Caveat: Positioning claims only work if they are true and consistently delivered; a claim you cannot keep becomes a commodity in the making.
Caveat: Picking a popular axis usually means a crowded quadrant where customers default to doing nothing or buying on price.

Tags: brand, positioning, merchandising, differentiation, product-page, strategy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Market to the Buyer, Not the End User
Use when: Product development, packaging or PDP copy for any store where the buyer differs from the user (pet products, kids' goods, gifts, B2B tools bought by a manager).
- Identify who clicks 'buy' and who lives with the product. They may have completely different criteria.
- List the buyer's inputs and emotions (status, generosity, care, guilt, convenience), not just the user's functional requirements.
- Check whether your feature roadmap and copy are optimized for the user's experience or the buyer's feeling. 'Performance and appeal' are usually disconnected.
- Reframe value propositions around what the buyer gets: the satisfaction of good care, the status of a luxury good, the generosity of sharing.
- Keep functional quality as table stakes; the emotional layer is what drives the choice between near-identical products.
Caveat: This is a reasoning framework from a marketing book, not a tested ecommerce benchmark; validate with your own buyer interviews or returns/refund data.

Tags: brand, customer-research, positioning, product-selection, copywriting, packaging
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Serve Early Adopters Differently From the Main Market
Use when: Deciding which SKUs or editions to launch first, how to sequence drops, and whether to write clever experimental copy or reassuring mainstream copy.
- Classify the product's current stage: is it built for discovery-seekers or for people who want the world to stay the same?
- For early adopters, emphasize novelty, insider status, and 'this might not work' tension. They forgive missteps during the thrill of discovery and brag about finds.
- For the mainstream, emphasize reliability, safety, and reduced risk. Laggards want things not to change.
- Don't expect early adopters to stay loyal once the newness fades; plan the transition to a broader audience and repeat purchase before the initial tribe loses interest.
- Write separate landing pages, ad creative and email flows for the two groups rather than one averaged message.
- The magic question before any launch or campaign: who's it for?
Caveat: Early adopters are described as forgiving early and unforgiving later; 'you can't be perfect in the eyes of an early adopter, the best you can do is be interesting' is the source's claim, not a universal law.

Tags: brand, positioning, acquisition, launch, creative-strategy, customer-research
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Position as a Service: A Generous Signal Flare, Not a USP
Use when: Writing your brand's core promise for the homepage hero, About page or ad hook when the current message is a generic superiority claim.
- Start from an audience worth serving and their needs, wants and dreams; build the offering for them rather than inventing a claim to fill a market hole.
- Choose what to stand for and what to be against — 'we're this, not that' — and repeat it across every touchpoint.
- Be generous with the claim: it should exist as a service to the customer, not as a boast about you.
- Make the claim concrete enough to act on operationally, including which customers you'll decline or which requests you won't take.
- Use the same signal in ads, packaging, PDP headline and support scripts so the promise is recognised, not just asserted.
Caveat: Old positioning practice (invent a claim to fill an empty spot) is described as not holding up in a hyper-competitive market; claims must be true and continually doubled down on in all actions.

Tags: brand, positioning, message-design, landing-page, copywriting
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Micro-Tribes: Sell Uniqueness and Belonging, Not Consensus
Use when: Community building, loyalty programs, or positioning a niche store or limited drop against a mass-market competitor.
- Identify the small, outspoken group your product already appeals to and where they gather (forums, Discords, subreddits, local scenes).
- Design the offer so belonging is visible: shared identifiers, member-only products, limited runs, insider language.
- Give members something to brag about — a discovery or knowledge that outsiders don't have.
- Prefer depth in the micro-tribe over reaching a broad indifferent audience; the source's study of conspiracy believers found stronger engagement when told only 19% shared the belief than 81%.
- Translate that into ecommerce: edition sizes, member pricing, community-only drops, and visible badges of belonging rather than mass discounting.
Caveat: The underlying study concerns conspiracy theorists, not shoppers; the transferable point is about uniqueness-seeking behaviour, and should be tested with your own customers.

Tags: brand, customer-research, community, retention, niche-marketing, loyalty
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Run the 'It's a Test' Loop Instead of Betting on a Miracle
Use when: Launching a new product, bundle or landing page when you're tempted to perfect it before exposing it to the market.
- Make an assertion about what a specific group wants and turn it into a concrete promise and offer.
- Because prototypes and limited runs are now cheap, ship a real test — a small batch, a pre-order, a single landing page — rather than a polished plan for later.
- Expose it to curious, dissatisfied or bored people, who are the only ones who will pay attention.
- Expect to be wrong most of the time; treat wrongness as the cost of finding the edge, not a reason to stop.
- Use the findings to choose new extremes and repeat, rather than concluding the idea itself was bad.
- Design being tested into normal operations: always be seeking, connecting, solving, asserting and testing.
Caveat: The source presents this as a heuristic, not a numeric success rate; 'most of the time you'll be wrong' is its opinion, not a measured benchmark.

Tags: brand, experiment-design, launch, product-selection, conversion-optimization
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Start From the Problem and the Change, Not the Product
Use when: Deciding what to build, stock or promote next; writing the top of a landing page; evaluating whether a new SKU has a real reason to exist.
- Write down the group you seek to serve, the problem they seek to solve, and the change they seek to make before listing product features.
- Frame the change emotionally, not tactically: a new emotional state is what's being purchased, not the widget.
- Check whether the claim serves the customer's interest or only makes you look clever; commodity alternatives have no claim at all.
- Ask whether the change is one the customer already wants or one you'd have to argue them into.
- Turn this into an offer statement and test it against your product page headline and first screen.
Caveat: Quality and meeting specification remain required but are no longer sufficient — this framework assumes you can already deliver reliably.

Tags: brand, offer-design, positioning, product-selection, product-page
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Sell the Emotional State, Not the Object
Use when: Writing PDP copy, choosing product photography or naming a new line, especially for non-essential goods.
- For each product, list plausible feelings the buyer is chasing (confidence, belonging, nostalgia, status, peace of mind, being seen).
- Pick the one or two feelings your audience is genuinely seeking rather than the most obvious functional benefit.
- Design the product presentation — images, copy, bundle, unboxing, return policy — to deliver that feeling.
- Example from the source: a first car for a teenager is a status shift for the teen and a change in control for the parent; the marketing must serve both emotional shifts.
- Check that the functional claims support the emotional promise rather than contradicting it.
Caveat: The source argues people are bad at inventing new ways to satisfy their wants and often default to familiar solutions; a new emotional angle needs to connect to an existing want.

Tags: brand, customer-research, product-page, copywriting, offer-design
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Find the Bear: Diagnose Non-Purchase as Fear
Use when: Diagnosing low conversion, abandoned carts, low retention or poor response to a new offer.
- For each step of the funnel, ask what the customer is afraid of: being cheated, being judged, wasting money, being seen with the wrong thing.
- Look at friction that signals risk: unclear returns, hidden shipping cost, unfamiliar payment, no social proof, aggressive urgency copy.
- Check whether your own anxiety-driven copy is amplifying their fear rather than answering it.
- Replace fear with concrete reassurance where the fear is real, and with a status or belonging cue where the fear is social.
- Test the change; do not assume that lower price is the answer to a fear-based problem.
Caveat: This is a diagnostic heuristic from the source; it doesn't replace funnel data, session recordings or customer interviews.

Tags: brand, customer-research, conversion-optimization, checkout, retention
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Design Remarkability Into the Product Itself
Use when: Designing referral mechanics, gifting options, multiplayer features, or shared/subscription plans in a store.
- Ask of any product or feature: does it work better, or feel better, if my customer's friend also has it?
- If not, consider adding a shared element: gifting, group buying, referral credit, a two-person bundle, or community-generated content.
- Model the network logic rather than assuming it: cost of adding users rises roughly linearly, while value is said to rise much faster — the source cites Metcalfe's Law and the fax machine/Ethernet examples.
- Make the spread visible and easy so that word of mouth is the growth engine rather than paid reach alone.
- Watch whether your best customers actually recruit; if not, the design isn't doing the work yet.
Caveat: Network effects do not apply to every category; the source generalizes from fax machines and Ethernet. Verify before restructuring a physical-goods store around them.

Tags: brand, retention, referral, subscription, growth, product-selection
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Build Customer Traction in the Small Before Chasing Scale
Use when: Evaluating a new product line, store concept, or marketing budget increase — deciding whether to scale or fix the core first.
- Ask how many people outside your company use the product every day, and how often they send improvement suggestions.
- Ask how many are actively insisting that friends and colleagues use it right now.
- Ask whether they'd miss it if it were gone, and whether they come back repeatedly bringing new customers.
- If you can't succeed in the small, the source argues, there is little reason to believe you'll succeed in the large.
- Fix retention and organic recommendation before raising acquisition spend; expensive failure is the usual outcome of the 'miracle' approach.
Caveat: The source is addressing startups, restaurants and market stalls; adapt the metrics (repeat purchase rate, referral rate, request volume) to your store rather than copying the wording.

Tags: brand, retention, measurement, growth, acquisition
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Use the Thousand True Fans Sizing Rule for Niche Offers
Use when: Sizing a niche subscription, membership or creator-style product line and deciding whether to chase a broad market instead.
- Define a true fan for your store: buys nearly every drop, pays full price, waits for restocks, and brings others.
- Estimate how many such customers exist and what each is worth across a year, including repeat purchases.
- Compare that against the cost of competing for low-loyalty broad-market buyers.
- Design the offer so superfans want everything you make — collectible editions, member perks, advance access, a consistent story.
- Use the source's rule of thumb (Kevin Kelly's 1000 true fans) as a sizing heuristic: 1,000 people who buy your next release the day it launches can support a modest living.
Caveat: The thousand-fan figure is a source-specific rule of thumb about living income for independent creators, not a proven ecommerce benchmark; substitute your own unit economics.

Tags: brand, retention, subscription, loyalty, niche-marketing, measurement
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Scrapbook Proven Elements Instead of Inventing From Scratch
Use when: Building a landing page, email campaign or new product listing when time is limited and you need a credible starting point.
- Collect references your audience already trusts and responds to: competitor pages, category leaders, respected non-competitor experiences.
- Break each down into indivisible elements — typefaces, pricing display, offer structure, imagery style, interface patterns.
- Identify the beacons (your chosen extremes) that matter to you and your audience, and discard the rest.
- Recombine the pieces into a new thing rather than copying a whole design.
- Test the rebuilt page against incumbents; scrapbooking is an efficiency play, not a guarantee of originality or outperformance.
Caveat: Radical originality is described as low-ROI and exhausting; this method trades distinctiveness for speed and reuse.
Caveat: Avoid drifting into a generic look by keeping the positioning extremes explicit.

Tags: brand, landing-page, conversion-optimization, creative-strategy, design
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Price With the Ten-Times Question
Use when: Pricing a premium tier, a luxury bundle, a membership, or a high-end physical product.
- Ask directly: what would make this worth ten times its current price?
- Reject 'more of the same' answers: more words, a larger portion, or a louder stereo doesn't multiply willingness to pay.
- Look for a different extreme, a different story, or a different type of scarcity — access, status, curation, exclusivity, relationship, or transformation.
- Check whether your highest-priced competitors are priced by features or by story; the source's example is an ice cream cone at a near-Ottawa resort that could rationally be priced at peak margin but is deliberately kept cheap as a beacon and engagement device.
- Decide deliberately whether this SKU is a profit centre or a beacon; don't let a spreadsheet turn a strategic loss-leader into a price increase that kills its job.
Caveat: The resort and supermarket examples are anecdotes illustrating the irrational side of pricing, not evidence that raising prices always works.

Tags: brand, pricing, offer-design, product-selection, margin
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Add an Offline or Human Element Online Retail Can't Copy
Use when: Merchandising and positioning a physical store, showroom, pop-up, or local pickup experience against Amazon and other online retailers.
- Identify what an online-only seller structurally cannot provide: a place to meet, a third place, coffee, expertise, try-on, same-day pickup, repair.
- Make that element central rather than incidental — the source describes Trident Booksellers as a coffee shop that sells books, where the books are a souvenir of a personal connection.
- Reframe merchandise in support of the experience rather than the other way round.
- Communicate the human element on the store's pages and signage; don't bury it as a footnote.
- Consider the source's suggested tagline pattern: '. . . and we serve coffee' — a concrete, non-generic promise that location-based competitors can't copy.
Caveat: This applies to retailers with a physical presence; pure online operators need an equivalent human element (expert advice, community, live events) to get the same effect.

Tags: brand, retail, positioning, merchandising, local-marketing, retention
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Perform Emotional Labor Instead of Striving for Authenticity
Use when: Customer service scripting, brand voice, founder-led content, UGC and support replies where the founder's mood and the brand's message conflict.
- Accept that marketplace 'authenticity' is a myth: the barista's smile and James Brown's stage collapse are performance, repeated every night.
- Treat emotional labor as the job: showing up with empathy for what the customer wants, even when you don't feel like it.
- Guard against the 'authentic jerk' problem — if the real you is selfish, keep it out of the storefront.
- Endorse the work with the human phrase the source offers: 'Here, I made this' — take responsibility as a person, not a faceless corporation speaking in the second person.
- Make the work personal rather than personalized: a signature, a named founder, a real reply, not automated intimacy.
Caveat: The source argues authenticity is a dangerous myth and that professionals play roles; this is a strong opinion, and some categories and creator brands legitimately benefit from raw disclosure.

Tags: brand, customer-service, brand-voice, retention, content-marketing
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Assert the Customer's Beliefs Before Choosing Channels
Use when: Writing briefs for acquisition creative, ad hooks, or a pre-launch validation plan.
- Start from the shared basket of dreams and desires the source lists: adventure, affection, belonging, community, control, creativity, delight, freedom, friendship, good looks, health, learning, luxury, nostalgia, peace of mind, power, reassurance, reliability, respect, romance, safety, security, sex, strength, sympathy, tension.
- Write assertions about what your audience wants, what is on their mind when they wake up, and what they remember at the end of the day.
- Do not expect customers to invent new solutions: ask them and you mostly get familiar answers and conservative preferences.
- Accept that wants and needs get confused — almost everything beyond air, water, health and shelter is a want, treated by the buyer as a need.
- Then build the promise and the creative, and test whether people see what you see and take action.
Caveat: The source warns customers are bad at inventing breakthrough solutions; treat survey answers as constraints, not blueprints.

Tags: brand, customer-research, creative-strategy, acquisition, message-design
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Replace 'You Can Choose Anyone, and We're Anyone' with an Edge
Use when: Rewriting an undifferentiated homepage or category page that competes only on price.
- Audit your messaging: if it could be swapped onto a competitor's site unchanged, you have a commodity problem.
- Identify the edge only you can claim authentically — the source's example is a music teacher claiming 'I'm serious, my students are serious, this is about rigor' plus 'my students win competitions', which requires expelling students who aren't serious.
- Accept the operational consequences: a sharp positioning means turning away the customers it isn't for.
- A neighbouring competitor can occupy a completely different position (whole-student, non-competitive, connection-led) — you aren't competing, you're on the same board.
- Choose the popular, broad axes or you'll land in a crowded quadrant where customers, unsure, do nothing.
Caveat: The source attributes this 'commodity suckout' risk partly to price transparency: your customers now often know more about your competitors than you do.

Tags: brand, positioning, differentiation, product-page, pricing, copywriting
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Start with the Smallest Viable Market, Not the Mass Market
Use when: Choosing a positioning and target-customer focus for a new store, product line, or niche brand before spending on broad acquisition.
- Define the smallest group of buyers you can serve exceptionally, then ask: who would miss this brand if it disappeared? If you cannot win the small group, do not assume you will win the large one.
- Follow Kevin Kelly's '1,000 true fans' framing as the source's heuristics for independent creators: roughly a thousand people who will buy nearly everything you release can sustain a living, though not a fortune—treat this as an illustration, not a store benchmark.
- Resist 'bending yourself into a pretzel' to please anonymous masses before you have 50–100 people who would miss you.
- Build roots in the specific dreams and desires of the niche you serve; a commodity store with shallow roots gets crowded out by similar competitors.
- As the niche deepens, its members organically spread the word to the next layer, growing reach without mass-media spend.
Caveat: This is the source's argument for connection over hits; it does not claim small markets always beat mass-market plays—the author explicitly says some businesses should chase being the 'hit machine.'
Caveat: The 1,000-fan figure is a creator-economy heuristic, not a validated ecommerce threshold.

Tags: brand, positioning, customer-research, niche-strategy, retention, community
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Model the Grateful Dead's Small-Market Strategy for Repeat Buyers
Use when: Designing merchandising, drops, loyalty or community programs for a niche brand that wants high repeat purchase rather than broad one-time reach.
- Appeal to a relatively small audience and focus all energy on them; skip mass broadcast in favor of fan-to-fan sharing.
- Encourage customers to share and even reproduce your content (the Dead encouraged taping) rather than blocking it—hand-to-hand spread replaces paid reach.
- Prefer many true fans who support a lot over many casual buyers who support a little.
- Own the extremes on a positioning axis (live long jams vs polished records); pick polarizing strengths rather than averaging for everyone.
- Give fans something to talk about and stand for, including insider/outsider identity, so community itself drives demand.
Caveat: Success required three things the source names explicitly: extraordinary talent, significant patience (it took over a decade; in 1972 only ~5,000 came to a typical show), and the guts to be quirky.

Tags: brand, community, retention, advocacy, super-fans, positioning
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Do Not Assume You Can Copy the Hit Machine Model
Use when: Deciding whether to pursue mass-market, hit-driven growth or a focused connection strategy for your store.
- Recognize that someone will always be the hit machine in a market; the source says it is probably not going to be most brands.
- If you genuinely find a playbook to become a hit machine, the author tells you to go for it—there is no law against it.
- For most operators, take the alternative path: connection, empathy and change within a chosen group.
- Note that even enormous hits now reach tiny fractions of the population (Hamilton seen by under 1% of the U.S.; Mad Men regularly seen by ~1%), so 'hits' are not as total as they once were.
- Design your plan around being meaningful to a defined few rather than invisible-but-widely-known.
Caveat: The Hamilton and Mad Men figures are the author's illustrative examples, not benchmarks for any store category.

Tags: brand, positioning, growth-strategy, niche-strategy, advisory
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Separate Criticism From Useful Advice You Can Act On
Use when: Reviewing negative product reviews, customer feedback, or testing a new PDP/landing-page concept with real shoppers.
- Accept that the critic who does not like your work is correct in that they do not like it—arguing is pointless.
- Reject the leap that 'no one' will like it; the same product routinely gets one-star and five-star reviews (the source cites 12% of ~21,000 Harry Potter reviews at one or two stars), showing bimodal audiences.
- Replace 'what do you think?' feedback requests with advice requests: 'I made something I like and thought you would like; how did it land, and what advice do you have to fit your worldview better?'
- Recognize universalizing language ('everyone'/'no one') as a specific sore spot triggered by one moment, not a market signal.
- Use the advice to identify fears, dreams and wants of the audience, then adjust product, messaging or merchandising accordingly.
Caveat: The Harry Potter review split is an example of bimodal distribution, not evidence your product will be polarizing.

Tags: brand, customer-research, feedback, product-page, review-mining
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Ask Why Non-Buyers Are Right (Empathy Exercise)
Use when: Diagnosing why an audience segment rejects your store, product or offer, or when repositioning after low conversion.
- List the people who do not buy from you, do not take your calls, or buy from a competitor despite knowing you exist.
- Suspend the temptation to call them ill-informed, selfish or wrongheaded.
- Fill in the sentence: 'For people who want (X) and believe (Y), choosing (your competitor/not buying) is exactly correct.'
- Example given: a cooking student asks why ground turkey can replace veal; both teacher and student are right because they care about different things (taste memory vs availability/health/morals).
- Use the completed sentence to identify whether the issue is offer, story, price, availability or worldview—then decide whether to serve them or refer them elsewhere.
- The source suggests finding freedom by telling mismatched customers: 'this isn't for you, here's my competitor.'
Caveat: This is a mindset/empathy exercise; it does not guarantee the segment is winnable—sometimes the correct conclusion is they should buy elsewhere.

Tags: brand, customer-research, positioning, offer-design, empathy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Use the 'People Like Us Do Things Like This' Frame for Behavior Change
Use when: Designing adoption campaigns, community programs or merchandising for a defined tribe (e.g., getting customers to try a new product category).
- Recognize the core question buyers ask: 'Do people like me do things like this?'—actions follow cohort perception, not pure rationality (cricket flour vs beef example).
- Define 'us' first and specifically; the tighter and more connected the group, the better for change.
- Map the worldview of the culture you seek to change, then focus all energy on it and ignore everyone else.
- Build and live a story that resonates with that culture, and let core members organically share with the next layer.
- Track how normalization creates culture, culture drives choices, and choices reinforce normalization—design your brand into that loop.
Caveat: This is a change-thy-culture framework; it will not work on a diffuse mass audience with no shared identity.
Caveat: The Blue Ribbons case (budget passed 2:1 after ribbons spread town-wide) is a civic example the source uses to illustrate the principle, not a store benchmark.

Tags: brand, community, behavioral-change, positioning, customer-research, culture
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Build an Exclusive Cohort, Not Just an Elite Brand
Use when: Designing loyalty tiers, member communities, VIP programs or a brand identity based on insider belonging.
- Distinguish two measures: elite = external respect (e.g., Rhodes Scholarship); exclusive = an insider tribe (e.g., Hell's Angels). Harvard Business School and Navy SEALs are both.
- The source argues exclusive institutions change things and can be controlled; elite status is external and can be taken away instantly.
- Build membership so that every member is 'people like us'; signing up gains status and walking away loses it.
- Engineer tension and useful connections inside the cohort rather than chasing press validation.
- Apply to ecommerce via member-only drops, early access, gated community, or a clear 'in-group' identity—but keep the group meaningfully tight.
Caveat: Exclusivity risks alienating the accidental audience; if your model depends on broad reach, weigh this tradeoff.

Tags: brand, community, loyalty, exclusivity, retention, positioning
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Engineer Peer Pressure Through 'Cash for Status' Mechanics
Use when: Designing high-value fundraising, charitable tie-ins, or VIP purchase experiences tied to social status.
- Identify the cohort where relative status is deeply felt (the source cites hypercompetitive Wall Street investors for Robin Hood).
- Build a visible, time-bound event or drop where peers and spouses are present so that status competition activates.
- Offer a simple trade: cash for status. Auctions or limited purchases let buyers raise profile and 'win' against peers.
- Spread word about early adopters' generosity over years to normalize the expectation.
- Repeat so the behavior becomes 'what we do,' not an extreme act—the tension becomes the norm over time.
Caveat: The $101M figure is a single historical outcome the source uses as illustration, not a promise.
Caveat: The source stresses this is intentional and rarely an accidental side effect; venue/format alone does not produce it.

Tags: brand, status, community, fundraising, exclusivity, offer-design
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Plan for Pattern Match vs Pattern Interrupt
Use when: Deciding go-to-market approach for a new product category, new store, or a subscription that replaces an incumbent habit.
- Classify your offer: does it slot into an existing routine (pattern match) or replace an established habit (pattern interrupt)?
- If pattern match, simply place your product into the existing cadence—cheap and low-friction.
- If pattern interrupt, accept that most recipients will say no; you must first undo the old pattern before earning forward motion.
- Prioritize people whose life context already broke their pattern: new dads, engaged couples, people who recently moved (the source calls these segments highly profitable because there is no pattern to match).
- For apps or platforms, market when the underlying platform habit is new, so you do not have to convince buyers their old choices were mistakes.
Caveat: Buying managers in organizations are trained to match patterns for job security; do not assume you can interrupt a procurement habit without strong justification.

Tags: brand, behavioral-change, positioning, acquisition, new-category, messaging
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Create Tension to Move Customers Forward (Not Fear)
Use when: Writing acquisition or cart-recovery messaging for an offer that requires breaking an existing habit or decision.
- Identify the specific tension your offer can create: what becomes less true for the buyer if they don't act?
- Examples from the source: scarcity of a going-out-of-business sale; early adopter benefit on a new social app; last three houses on your block sold below expectations; Supreme making only 250 units; TV series finale this Sunday.
- Apply the slack analogy: using the product is better when peers use it, giving existing users a selfish reason to invite others; a colleague saying 'you're missing out' is the pattern interrupt.
- Make the tension resolvable by a single, simple forward action—sign in, buy, sign up.
- Explicitly test whether the message creates tension or fear; if it coerces or scares, the source says you are probably doing it wrong.
Caveat: Tension works because of status and cohort belonging; it is not the same as fear, and should not be used manipulatively.
Caveat: The examples (Slack, Supreme, casinos) are illustrations; do not assume the same tension will move your specific audience.

Tags: brand, messaging, conversion-optimization, urgency, scarcity, status
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Decide Whether Your Store Is a Cab or an Agent of Change
Use when: Setting brand strategy and differentiating from commoditized competitors in your category.
- Ask honestly which you are: a cab driver who shows up and charges the meter, or an agent of change who creates tension and then relieves it.
- If cab: compete on execution and price; not much else changes no matter how hard you work.
- If agent of change: identify what your launch makes no longer true (e.g., the second hotel makes the first no longer 'the only one'; the telephone makes the telegraph no longer fastest).
- Aim for extremes (most efficient, least expensive, most convenient) so whatever you exceed is no longer the extreme its fans sought.
- When a new network or brand gains the cool kids, existing alternatives lose traction—use this to plan entry timing and partnerships.
Caveat: This is the source's framing of two marketing postures, not a claim that one is always correct; some markets simply need another good option.

Tags: brand, positioning, differentiation, category-design, messaging
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Remember the Status Quo Resists You Because of Status, Not Truth
Use when: Planning competitive entry against a market leader or legacy category belief.
- Accept that the market leader, policies and procedures exist for a reason and are good at resisting insurgents.
- Stop assuming a better idea, simpler solution or more efficient procedure is enough—the source says if that were true we would have changed long ago.
- Focus on shifting the culture of a specific group rather than winning an argument on merits.
- Remember the engine of culture is status; frame your offer in terms of how the buyer's status shifts (up, or safely down).
- Use the 'us vs them' exclusive group to accumulate cultural momentum rather than fighting the incumbent head-on.
Caveat: This is a strategic posture; it does not mean product quality is irrelevant, only that correctness alone is insufficient.

Tags: brand, positioning, competitive-strategy, status, culture
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## View Every Purchase Through Status Roles
Use when: Designing product tiers, brand imagery, influencer partnerships, or loyalty perks where buyers signal who they are.
- Use the six properties from the source: status is relative (no absolute scale); it is in the eye of the beholder; only status attended to matters; it has inertia (people defend their role); it is learned; shame is the status killer.
- Map your customer's external status (how their community sees them) and internal status (how they see themselves), then infer behavior patterns: belittling others, seeking approval, selfless help, striving for more.
- Note that not everyone wants higher status—some fight to stay or even move lower (coal miner example); designing for upward movement only can miss segments.
- Consider that status is not wealth: a local yogi may outrank the richest man in town with certain peers.
- Apply to product tiers, premium imagery, or memberships as signals buyers can use to reinforce or shift their standing.
Caveat: The source warns that using status in marketing is walking on thin ice; you cannot know whether an outwardly high-status customer actually believes it or wants more.

Tags: brand, status, customer-research, positioning, premium-tier
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Adapt Ritual and Status Instead of Fighting Cultural Beliefs
Use when: Adapting a brand, product or category to sensitive cultural or identity-driven customer behaviors.
- Identify the underlying human desires your audience's behavior serves (binding community, empowerment, bravery, patience, rite of passage) rather than attacking the surface behavior.
- Recognize that killing a lion was historically tied to status for Maasai youth—rational arguments alone could not shift the belief.
- Work within the cultural system to introduce a new behavior that satisfies the same desires and confers equivalent status.
- For ecommerce: when a behavior (e.g., ritual consumption, gifting) serves identity, design products and messaging that honor the identity rather than asking customers to abandon it.
- Anchor new behaviors to existing rituals and social proof so adoption feels like belonging, not breaking from the group.
Caveat: This case involves community-level conservation work by Leela Hazzah's team; do not transpose numeric wildlife estimates to product strategy.
Caveat: Cultural sensitivity is required; outsider framing that ignores underlying desires will fail.

Tags: brand, behavioral-change, community, status, positioning, culture
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Segment by Affiliation vs Dominion Worldview
Use when: Writing acquisition creative, influencer briefs or loyalty messaging for distinct buyer segments.
- Diagnose which worldview your segment holds. Affiliation questions: who knows you, who trusts you, have you made things better, what's your circle, where do you stand within the tribe?
- Dominion questions: this is mine not yours, who has more power, I did this myself, my family needs more, my side dominating yours.
- Match language: affiliation audiences respond to community, sync, and mutual support (e.g., jazz band keeping in sync); dominion audiences respond to competitive wins and dominance.
- Recognize that a person can hold different frames in different contexts (the same 12-year-old cares about winning on the field but who sits next to them on the bus).
- Use the distinction to segment creative and offers rather than averaging messaging to total blandness.
Caveat: Affiliation and dominion are worldviews the source presents as analytical lenses; they are not strictly binary and can shift with context.

Tags: brand, segmentation, messaging, audience-research, creative-strategy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Engineer a Standing Ovation: Seed a Small Number of Status Leaders
Use when: Planning product launches, review/social-proof seeding, or community engagement campaigns.
- Identify how many respected insiders are needed to trigger cascading adoption in your venue; the source's illustration claims as few as three at TED and roughly fifteen strangers spread through a Broadway theater.
- Recognize that in rooms with trust and recognition among attendees, few strangers are needed because people want to align with respected leaders.
- In venues of strangers or with strong anti-standing-ovation norms (e.g., Mezzrow jazz club), cascades may be nearly impossible regardless of effort.
- Apply to launches: seed a small, visible group of respected customers whose behavior others will mirror, especially in high-trust communities.
- Use the venue's existing bias as a constraint—if the culture rejects the behavior, plan to change the venue or segment instead.
Caveat: The number thresholds (three, fifteen) are illustrative of the mechanism, not universal rules; audience composition matters more than headcount.

Tags: brand, community, social-proof, launch-strategy, influence
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Define the 'Us' Explicitly in Marketing Statements
Use when: Writing brand manifestos, community guidelines, or acquisition copy that invokes an in-group.
- Replace universal audiences ('Americans,' 'everyone') with a tightly defined 'us' that actually exists.
- Avoid the backwards formulation 'the people who give are people like us'; instead specify the identity, values and behaviors that define the group.
- Map the worldview of that culture and focus all energy there, ignoring everyone else as the source directs.
- Use the group's shared behaviors as proof points ('people like us own a car; we don't take the bus') to make the offer feel like belonging.
- Apply the same logic internally—pitch ideas to a defined 'us' in meetings, not a universal crowd.
Caveat: The tighter the us, the more effective for change, but the smaller the addressable market; balance reach against relevance deliberately.

Tags: brand, positioning, messaging, community, copywriting
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Use the Roots and Shoots Metaphor to Plan Brand Growth
Use when: Planning long-term brand differentiation and category dominance instead of rapid commodity launches.
- Plant roots in the soil of the dreams and desires of the specific people you seek to serve—not everyone.
- Avoid shallow 'quick response to obvious demand' positioning, which produces a commodity that gets crowded out.
- Grow the tree: early adopters engage, climb, use shade, eat fruit, and attract others.
- Exploit the open canopy—if few similar trees exist, grow taller faster and become the dominant choice in the neighborhood.
- Remember the market likes a winner; once dominant, the tree's height blocks futile efforts of similar competitors.
- Do not expect a crowd from an acorn—work that matters for people who care is the shortest route to impact.
Caveat: Metaphor is strategic framing (the author's), not an operational model with timelines or metrics.

Tags: brand, positioning, differentiation, growth-strategy, category-design
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Position Just Ahead of Today—But Not Too Far
Use when: Designing product aesthetics, campaign concepts or brand refresh that must read as fresh but still familiar.
- Study the hierarchy of yesterday, today and tomorrow in your category; you cannot jump straight to tomorrow.
- Imitate yesterday's style is technically easy but offers no differentiation; aim to establish the next phase with something 'a bit better and a bit unexpected.'
- Use the JetBlue/Virgin America illustration: JetBlue only had to be hipper than American and Delta; Virgin America had to be hipper than JetBlue—each launch faced a raised bar.
- Calibrate the leap by asking whether the tribe will follow; if too far, you lose them.
- Apply to ecommerce brand design, packaging and creative—being one step ahead is differentiating; being ten steps ahead is alienating.
Caveat: The JetBlue/Virgin comparison is the source's illustration; the 'how far is too far' threshold is contextual.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, positioning, design, innovation
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Build a Peer-Powered Ratchet Like Slack
Use when: Designing referral mechanics, collaboration features, subscription plans for teams, or buyer-side network effects.
- Identify whether your product is better when peers or teammates use it. If yes, existing users have a powerful selfish reason to recruit.
- Reduce the friction for the peer-to-peer pattern interrupt: a colleague's simple statement 'we're going to try this new tool' should be enough.
- Provide that message as part of the product itself (sharing invites, collaborative workspaces, joint accounts) so the horizontal transmission is built in.
- Sequence go-to-market: first pattern match early adopters who love new tools; then let them trigger the pattern interrupt for their colleagues.
- Give existing users a felt cost of delay ('you're missing out; people at work are talking without you') to accelerate the ratchet.
Caveat: Slack is the source's example; network-effect design does not automatically produce virality, and the mechanics depend on the product category.

Tags: brand, acquisition, network-effects, referral, replenishment, retention
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Interpret Famous Cases Without Copying Tactics Blindly
Use when: Evaluating whether to replicate a well-known competitor's marketing format or campaign structure.
- When a tactic looks like the cause of a hit, ask what underlying mechanism actually drove results.
- Use Robin Hood Foundation as the example: many concluded the gala format raised $101M; the source argues it was peer pressure, status trading and decades of normalization.
- Look for the cohort dynamics, historical context and status economics that a copycat would also have to recreate.
- Recognize that intentional, long-cultivated processes (spreading word about early adopters, building competitive tension) usually underpin outcomes, not a single night's format.
- Before copying a famous tactic, inventory whether you have the audience, history and status dynamics it depends on.
Caveat: The $101M figure and Robin Hood context are examples of the source's analytical argument; do not treat them as reproducible benchmarks.

Tags: brand, strategy, case-analysis, community, positioning
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Write a five-part business plan for a store launch or reposition
Use when: Planning a new product line, store launch, brand repositioning or annual plan and you need an honest internal document rather than an investor compliance exercise.
- Write the Truth section first: the market you're entering, the needs that exist, competitors in your space, technology standards, and how others have succeeded and failed. Footnote if useful, but keep it non-partisan—no positions, just how things are. Include market share analysis, spreadsheets and visceral customer stories. The goal is that you and your team agree on the assumptions.
- Write Assertions: 'We will do X, and then Y will happen.' Name the specific market you serve and the change you expect on arrival. This is the heart of the plan and it will be partly wrong—budgets, deadlines and sales will miss.
- Write Alternatives so a missed assertion isn't fatal: how much flexibility does your product, supply chain or team have? If the assertion doesn't pan out, what's next? This is what lets you test rather than bet the store.
- Write People: not resumes but attitudes, abilities and track record in shipping. Include who you serve, who your champions are, what they believe about status, and what worldview they hold.
- Write Money last: capital needed, how you'll spend it, cash flow, P&L, balance sheet, margins, exit.
- Treat your 'why' or mission statement as not the deliverable. The source argues most purposes reduce to 'sell more of what I already decided to sell.' The plan's job is to make the universal need specific: who it's for, what tension you create, what status roles you engage, and the story that makes change happen.
Caveat: The source concedes this format may not satisfy a local VC; it's framed as a thinking tool for your team, not an investor deck.
Caveat: A failed assertion doesn't mean the business lacks purpose—it rules out one route and frees you to find another.

Tags: brand, business-planning, positioning, strategy, merchandising-planning
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Choose dominion or affiliation status signals for your customers
Use when: Deciding brand voice, creative direction and community mechanics for a store—especially when you must pick between aggressive competitive positioning and belonging-based positioning.
- Determine which status worldview your customers already hold. Dominion is vertical—above or below, winning and losing, 'who eats first.' Affiliation is horizontal—'who's standing next to me,' insiders and outsiders, being in sync with the group.
- If you market to dominion: accept that some customers, partners and employees respond to a narrative of winning and losing. The source points to Uber's early years—contentious relationships with governments, competitors and drivers—as dominance signaling that let aligned investors, employees and users double down on the story.
- If you market to affiliation: send signals of who else is here. Trade-show booth traffic, who attends the launch, who blurbed the book—all shorthand for 'are people like us doing something like this?' Prime the pump with the right signals to the right people to start a cascade.
- Adapt affiliation tactics by business type as the source suggests: B2B sellers build referrals; a local craftsperson hunkers down in a single neighborhood until reputation is assured; investment banks run tombstone ads listing the 'right' firms.
- Note that affiliation tolerates abundance (more affiliation helps everyone) while dominion leans on scarcity. Fashion, per the source, is usually affiliation: the leader's role is providing a signal that everyone else will be in sync—the goal isn't winning, it's being part of the group.
- Test which frame your audience actually uses before committing spend; the source warns that people aligned with one worldview often can't imagine choosing the other, so internal consensus is not evidence.
Caveat: The source states the customer's worldview is always stronger than the story you choose to tell—this is positioning guidance, not a claim that dominance messaging fails.
Caveat: Uber is given as an example of aligned signaling, not a validated long-term brand outcome.

Tags: brand, positioning, customer-research, messaging, community, status-signaling
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Use semiotics so your store reminds buyers of the right thing
Use when: Designing or auditing a storefront, PDP photography, packaging, email design or ad creative where first-impression trust is at stake.
- Accept the scanning reality: with too many choices and everything 'good enough,' busy people don't study your work, don't know your competitive drama, and don't care how hard you worked. They ask what it reminds them of.
- Audit your signals for unwanted associations. The source's examples: a site that feels like GeoCities with flashing GIFs, or a call with telltale clicks and pauses that reads as robocall and gets hung up before a word is spoken. If you remind someone of a scam, undoing that first impression takes a long time.
- Choose signals that fit your category's trust code. Big companies' logos often look alike not from laziness but because designers are trying to remind you of a solid company. A summer blockbuster gives itself away in four frames—it's clearly not a teen makeup guru's YouTube video.
- Hire a professional when the stakes justify it, because the professional designs what other people will like, not what you like. The source allows that an amateur occasionally stumbles onto the right vernacular, but says the rest of the time it's best done with intent.
- Remember the symbol lives in the viewer's mind, not yours: a hoodie signals status in Silicon Valley ('too busy to shop') but may put someone in East London on alert. There is no single right answer—the symbol that works for one group won't for another.
- Be aware that flags and flares get copied until they stop being scarce and become mere signals of changing taste, so what reads as premium today can drift.
Caveat: Semiotic readings can encode bias—the Witchsy.com case is cited, where a fictitious male partner 'Keith' got faster, more helpful vendor and developer replies than the real female founders. The source says marketers should acknowledge the culture they seek to change rather than deny it.
Caveat: Semiotics is a design and trust heuristic; the source offers no measurement method.

Tags: brand, semiotics, packaging, product-page, creative-strategy, design, trust
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Fly a specific flag instead of claiming no brand identity
Use when: Running brand positioning work, naming a store, writing taglines or choosing the aesthetic that determines who self-selects into your customer base.
- Define a brand as the customer's expectation: what promise do they think you're making when they buy, meet you or hire you? That promise—not your logo—is the brand. The source contrasts a true brand with a commodity (wheat by the ton, coffee by the pound, bandwidth by the GB), where no expectation exists beyond spec: same as yesterday, faster and cheaper.
- Accept that you cannot build a brand for everyone. Be specific, be very specific, then overdo your brand marketing on that narrow target. Every slice of every interaction should reflect the whole: from any one touchpoint, a customer should be able to make a smart guess about all of you.
- Choose a flag with intent, because not flying one is as intentional as flying one. Customers are trying to figure out who you are; make it easy for them to know who you are and where you stand. The source calls the lazy move insisting you don't need a flag and the foolish move pretending your features are so good nothing else matters.
- Send a signal that feels like a sign customers already trust, then change it enough to show it's new and yours—this is the same-and-different principle. Apple's 1984 ad only needed a million people to care, targeting the media-savvy and the nerds first, ignoring everyone else; the source notes the idea took about thirty years to spread outward.
- Don't overinvest in the logo itself: logos matter less than designers want but more than committees realize. Make it work at different sizes in different media, avoid offending or distracting people, then pick one, don't spend a lot of money or hold many meetings about it, and keep it as long as your first name.
- Expect tension between going first (innovation reminds some people of a past failure) and reaching the market that will actually give a new thing a chance—start with the audience that tolerates that association.
Caveat: The Apple 1984 example is presented as a semiotic case, not a replicable playbook, and its results are described as a 30-year compounding outcome.
Caveat: The source asserts logos of mediocre brands can still work and great brands can lack memorable logos, so logo spend is often overrated.

Tags: brand, positioning, logo-design, target-market, messaging, creative-strategy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Aim new products at neophiliacs first, not the whole market
Use when: Deciding who to seed a new product, launch, bundle or category with first, and how to allocate launch marketing budget.
- Use the distribution as a rough mental model for any group: about 68% cluster near the average, 27% sit significantly further out, and a few are extreme outliers. For style, technology and innovations, most people like what they have and want to do what others are doing.
- Identify the early adopters—those who will queue for opening night, upgrade their operating system immediately, and read Vogue for the ads. The source frames this as the left side of the curve and says not to waste a minute of your time or theirs on anyone who isn't there.
- Skip the direct-conversion attempt on satisfied customers. The source's reasoning is arithmetic, not moral: if someone is content, you likely don't have the time or money to make them dissatisfied enough, interested enough and open enough to switch—not right now.
- Reach the holdouts horizontally, person-to-person through earned media, with persistence and smarts. That's an eventual path, not a launch tactic.
- Design the offer for what neophiliacs actually want: to go first, hope and possibility and magic, the thrill of it working and the risk it might not, the pleasure of showing innovation to the crew, and the satisfaction of doing better work faster.
- Segment by desire rather than demographics. The source lists different wants among the same market: the corporate buyer wants to avoid trouble and have an alibi; the social crusader wants a glimmer of hope and a chance to make things right; the dominance-seeker wants to win; the affiliation-seeker wants to fit in without the risk of being picked to lead; some want a bargain and a few eagerly overpay to prove they can; almost no one wants to feel stupid.
Caveat: The 68/27/4 percentages describe standard deviation in a measured population; the source uses them as a behavioral heuristic, not a store-specific conversion model.
Caveat: The instruction not to market to the middle is a marketing-resource argument, not a claim that satisfied customers never buy.

Tags: brand, target-market, early-adopters, product-launch, customer-research, acquisition
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Earn enrollment instead of buying attention
Use when: Designing launch content, email opt-ins, community or loyalty programs, and deciding what you offer in exchange for a shopper's attention.
- Treat mandatory education as impossible; there's no teaching people against their will. Aim instead for enrollment: hands raised, eyes on the board, notes being taken.
- Make the exchange explicit: they lend attention because they'll get the insight or forward motion they want. Enrollment leads to change and to permission to keep engaging.
- Avoid buying enrollment with flashy ads; the source calls that the lazy route. The best marketers earn it by seeking people who want the change on offer, and by connecting those people to others who want the change as well.
- Use enrollment as the first step in a two-way journey: you learn from the customer and she learns from you. This distinguishes it from a one-shot acquisition push.
- Sequence it in the marketing chain the source describes: an ad exists to get a click; the click exists to make a sale or earn permission; the sale exists to lead to another sale or word of mouth; permission exists to lead to education and then a sale. If you offer education content instead of a hard sell, know which rung of that chain it sits on.
- Target people who want what the education delivers—not everyone wants responsibility, some want recognition, and many want convenience so they don't have to pay attention or exercise judgment. The source's instruction is to always be wondering, testing and willing to treat different people differently, because if you don't, they'll find someone who will.
Caveat: The source gives no conversion benchmarks for enrollment, so treat the mutual-journey claim as a framing principle rather than a measured funnel.
Caveat: It warns against bribing attention with flashy ads, which is a resource-allocation opinion, not proof that paid acquisition fails.

Tags: brand, content-marketing, retention, email-marketing, community, acquisition
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Identify and multiply overinvest in your superusers
Use when: Designing reviewer, UGC, refer-a-friend, ambassador or loyalty programs and deciding where to concentrate service and perks.
- Reject the flat 'everyone is a critic now, so treat everyone better' logic. The source argues the math doesn't hold: treating everyone better with fixed resources is a bit like treating everyone worse—you can't treat everyone better than you already are.
- Watch behavior instead of stated intent. Everyone could be a neophiliac, a sneezer or a power user; only some take the opportunity. Look at who is actually adopting your cause, talking about you, or trying to lead.
- Adopt them back. When someone is eager to talk about what you do, give them something to talk about. When someone itches to be a generous leader, give her the resources to lead. The source notes the technical levers exist to treat different people differently if you choose.
- Ground the prioritization in contribution economics: your fixed costs—suits, storefront, software, inventory, ads, publicity and a hundred other things—spread across the whole customer base. The source's graph shows gross margin earned per customer against the per-person marketing spend line, with only eight customers on the graph actually contributing profit.
- Extend that to service. A valuable customer in a support queue may be identifiable in the record: years of tenure, deposits, tweeting about you, no returns, on-time payment, high-margin purchases, producing far more revenue than the average. For that customer, the source endorses extraordinary acts—drive across town, get the shipment into the last FedEx pickup, have the CEO call the person you triple-charged—because the cost of being human is covered by the upside of delighting an extraordinary customer.
- Ask what the interaction is for before defaulting to scripts. If the goal is to get it over with, deny responsibility and cite policy, the source says keep doing that and watch it all fall apart.
- Accept that you can't do this for every customer, but you can learn to see and act accordingly. Serve many; profit from a few—the whales pay for the minnows.
Caveat: The 'eight of the customers on this graph' figure is a hypothetical illustration of the fixed-cost math, not a benchmark for any store.
Caveat: The source explicitly permits treating different customers differently rather than trying to treat everyone equally well; that conflicts with absolute service-equality policies some operators hold.

Tags: brand, retention, customer-service, loyalty-program, customer-research, advocacy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Separate goal, strategy and tactics before running campaigns
Use when: Setting annual marketing direction, deciding whether to drop a channel, or aligning a team on what counts as failure.
- Write the goal as the change in the world or in those you serve, not the self-focused goal of earning money. It's the shining light and unwavering destination.
- Write strategy as the long-lasting way you invest toward that goal. The source offers examples: earn trust and attention; be seen as the best and maybe only alternative; build alliances and partnerships that reach exactly the right people. Using stories, status and connection to create tension and forward motion is itself a strategy.
- List tactics as the dozens or hundreds of steps supporting the strategy. If a tactic fails, another can take its place—tactics can be changed the moment they stop helping.
- Test strategy changes rarely, not reactively. If a strategy fails you may need to change it, but you don't want to do so often.
- Learn from the contrast: Coca-Cola's goal was more people drinking more Coke, its strategy an enormous volume of mass-market ads tying Coke to happiness and everyone else drinking it, while each individual ad's content was a replaceable tactic. Patagonia's goal keeps a small group of outdoor enthusiasts caring for the environment and expressing it through apparel; the strategy has been stable for more than thirty years—redefining how some people think about environmental impact and clothing quality, giving a small group a label and a tool to evangelize, creating insiders and outsiders—while tactics range from clothes recycling to brick buildings to material, selection and price choices.
- Use the test of disclosure: if you tell a competitor your tactics, they'll steal them and it costs you; telling them your strategy doesn't matter, because they lack the guts or persistence to convert it into their own.
Caveat: The source is explicit that strategy talk is a guardrail against tactic churn; it does not supply metrics for when a strategy should be retired.
Caveat: Coca-Cola and Patagonia are illustrative cases, not templates to copy.

Tags: brand, strategy, marketing-planning, positioning, channel-strategy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Decide whether to buy ads at all—and which kind
Use when: Allocating budget between paid acquisition and brand-building, or judging whether a paid channel should be scaled or killed.
- Use the three genuine advantages of online ads: precision (psychographics—what people believe and want—not just demographics), instantaneity (decide at 10 a.m., reach people at 10:01 a.m.), and measurement.
- Weigh the cost side the source stresses: it is not unusual to run an ad in front of a hundred thousand people and get not a single click, or to start, run and finish an entire campaign without moving the culture. Advertising is unearned media and recipients know it—they're suspicious, inundated and exhausted, so you're ignored.
- Understand the price-attention equation before buying. The source contrasts a fancy magazine at about $80 CPM (roughly a dime per reader) with an $80 placement in front of a million people on a second-rate site where readers are scanning and clicking past—those people won't remember the ad or act.
- If buying direct, measure everything: compute the cost to earn attention, the cost per click, and the cost to turn that attention into an order. The chain is: ad exists to get a click; click exists to make a sale or earn permission; sale exists to lead to another sale or word of mouth; permission exists to lead to education and to a sale. Assign values to each step, and if you can't, the source says don't run any direct-response ads until you can.
- If buying brand, be patient and refuse to measure, engaging with the culture instead. Brand marketing is culturally oriented and can't be measured; direct is action-oriented and measured. The source warns the danger is confusing the two.
- Fund brand work only from what you can sustain: you're already spending on brand marketing through phone manner, packaging, location, hold music, executive behavior and packing peanuts. Be consistent and patient, and if you can't afford to be consistent and patient, don't pay for brand marketing ads.
- Note how the economics differ by size: Google's and Facebook's revenue growth comes from ads that pay for themselves—$100 of online advertising generating $125 in profit means buying more until ads stop paying—while many large brand advertisers (Ford, Absolut, Palmolive; P&G's Tide and Crest on TV) can't construct direct campaigns that work, and local businesses hooked on Yellow Pages struggle to move to review platforms that feel uncontrolled and unproven.
Caveat: The $80 CPM and $100-to-$125 examples are illustrations of the math, not benchmarks for any product category.
Caveat: Google, Facebook and Meta ad interfaces and measurement options change frequently; verify current capabilities and attribution in the ad platform before relying on any specific setup.
Caveat: The instruction to 'refuse to measure' brand marketing is the source's opinion and conflicts with common practice—hold brand and direct to different standards rather than applying the same scorecard.

Tags: brand, paid-acquisition, measurement, media-planning, conversion-optimization, creative-strategy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Build frequency before judging a message dead
Use when: Deciding whether to refresh ad creative, retire a tagline, change a packaging story or abandon a channel that hasn't produced results yet.
