- Accept the memory reality: people don't remember what they read, hear or even see, and aren't good at remembering what they do. We remember what we rehearse—what we see again and again. The familiar is normal and the normal is trusted.
- Expect the dip: there's a very real gap between when the marketer gets bored and when the audience gets the message. The source's pattern is people giving a talk a few times and moving on, launching a freelance practice and quitting when it sputters, or raising money and spending fast, hitting the wall just before the good stuff happens.
- Apply the Jay Levinson standard the source quotes: don't change your ads when you're tired of them, not when your employees are tired of them, not even when your friends are tired of them—change them when your accountant is tired of them.
- Extend frequency beyond ads. All storytelling requires it: a new statement, a new market exploration—when it doesn't work right away the instinct is to walk away, but frequency is what earns the chance.
- Differentiate deliberate consistency from blind stubbornness by using the goal/strategy/tactics split: frequency applies to the strategy and story, while individual tactics can be swapped the moment they stop supporting it.
- Budget the repetition honestly before you launch, since frequency means paying for the same message multiple times rather than producing one round of creative.
Caveat: The source gives no frequency threshold (such as a number of impressions or weeks) and no measurement guidance; treat frequency as a persistence principle, not a calculable target.
Caveat: The Levinson line is quoted as an opinion about creative refresh timing, not a data-backed rule.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, advertising, campaign-planning, messaging, retention
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Make your store the thing people search for by name
Use when: Deciding whether to invest in generic-term SEO, brand-name search, or marketplace and paid placement for a store with limited budget.
- Reject the myth that grooming your site for generic terms means searchers will find you. There are a thousand pages of results for 'tire store,' 'restaurant' or 'freelance copy editor,' and the source argues the math can't support every business imagining it will be the first match.
- Recognize the unequal outcome: the locksmith, hotel or doctor who wins a generic search earns a huge profit, while everyone else spends on consultants and trickery to rank higher—a pyramid the source says the math can't support.
- Reframe the objective: the path is to have someone care enough about you and what you create that they type your name, looking for you rather than a generic alternative.
- Do step one—build a product or service worth searching for specifically, not for the generic term. Once that exists, Google is on your side: platforms want you to be found when someone searches for you.
- Do step two—be the one they want to find when they go looking. That means owning the branded result for your name and product names, not just the category term.
- Translate this into store decisions: distinctive products, ownable product names and a brand people remember are the SEO assets; generic category keywords are a bidding war, not a moat.
Caveat: This is the source's argument, framed as a mathematical objection to generic SEO; it does not deny that category search can drive sales for some businesses—the source acknowledges the locksmith, hotel or doctor gains a huge profit from a generic win.
Caveat: No keyword volumes, ranking factors or measurement methods are given.

Tags: brand, seo, search, product-selection, acquisition, differentiation
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Price as positioning signal, not just revenue math
Use when: Setting price points, launching a premium or value line, or deciding whether to compete on cheapest price.
- Start from the two-way rule: marketing changes your pricing, and pricing changes your marketing. People form assumptions and associations from the price, and the price shapes what they believe about the service.
- Check alignment: your price should match the extremes you claimed in positioning. Note that asking who buys the cheapest or the most expensive wine says nothing about taste or value—only price, which is itself the signal. A Porsche Cayenne has no utility proportionate to its expense; it's a flag flown in the driveway and in self-esteem.
- Run the margin arithmetic before choosing the position. The source's baker: costs of $1.95 per loaf at reasonable quantity yield a nickel profit at $2.00, fifty-five cents at $2.50 (an eleven-fold increase, over 1,000% more profit per loaf), and over a dollar at $3.00—more than twenty times the first example. The $2.00 baker must sell twenty-one loaves for every loaf the luxury baker sells.
- Decide what the higher price is actually buying the customer, using concrete trade-offs: a sparkling clean shop, well-paid and helpful staff, a new window sign, a local baseball team in jerseys with your logo, a handsome shopping bag with every loaf, free butter-cookie samples, and the social pleasure of telling friends they're eating the same bread as the fancy restaurant down the street. The source's framing: better to apologize for the price once than to excuse a hundred small slights again and again.
- Recognize that racing to be cheapest means you're not investing sufficiently in change—you're promising the same, but cheaper. The source notes the race to the bottom is tempting because nothing is easier to sell than cheaper, but that ease is the trap. (This is the source's opinion, offered with the caveat 'unless you've found an extraordinary new way to deliver your product or service.')
- Remember that margin is the money available for outbound marketing—the price position and the marketing budget are one decision, not two.
Caveat: The baker's cost and profit figures are an illustrative example, not a benchmark for any category.
Caveat: The claim that low price signals insufficient investment in change is the source's opinion with a stated exception for genuinely novel delivery; it is not proof that low-price strategies fail.

Tags: brand, pricing, positioning, margin, product-page, offer-design
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Handle price discrimination with story, not mechanics
Use when: Planning discounts, member pricing, dynamic pricing, flash sales, Kickstarter-style tiers or loyalty pricing—and anticipating customer reaction.
- Take the historical frame: Quakers originated the fixed price tag because charging different people different prices felt immoral; Macy's and Wanamaker's scaled it because they needed huge stores with low-paid staff and couldn't train or trust that many people to haggle. Industrialists liked the efficiency.
- Recognize what the internet changed: on one hand, a store can tell the story that the price is the price—the source cites Tesla, whose fixed pricing luxury buyers reportedly greeted with relief. On the other, matching price to demand cost Uber billions in brand trust, per the source.
- For most organizations, especially small ones, the difficulty isn't the mechanics of charging different amounts—it's the storytelling.
- Anticipate the emotional questions customers ask: how do you feel when you discover you got a discount nobody else got—and does it matter if you deserved it? How do you feel when others got the discount and you didn't?
- Use scarcity mechanics deliberately where they fit, as in Kickstarter tiers, where the fear of missing out on a level that's almost full pushes action—but recognize this is a story you're telling, not a neutral feature.
- Decide the policy that matches your positioning before publishing: a fixed-price story supports trust and predictability, while tiered or dynamic pricing trades some of that trust for yield, and the source indicates the trade should be made consciously.
Caveat: The Tesla and Uber outcomes are presented as anecdotes about brand perception, not controlled measurements.
Caveat: The source is explicit that this is largely a storytelling problem for small organizations, not a recommendation for or against dynamic pricing.

Tags: brand, pricing, discount-strategy, checkout, customer-psychology, positioning
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Storytelling Your Price (and Tiers) Instead of Racing to Cheapest
Use when: Setting or defending product pricing, launching a discount or dynamic-pricing policy, or building a tiered offer on a product page.
- Treat price as a story you tell, not just a number. Decide the narrative for every price point on the PDP: what change you promise, who the price is for, and why it is fair.
- Run the source's reaction test on your pricing and promotions: how do buyers feel when they get an exclusive discount? When someone else gets it and they don't? Use the answers to choose whether to offer a discount quietly, publicly, or not at all.
- Use scarcity and tiered pricing (as on Kickstarter-style campaigns) to build tension that pushes a buyer to act, but ensure the scarcity is real and the tiers have a defined reason for existing.
- Avoid defaulting to cheapest. Attributing to the source: racing to be the cheapest usually means you're not investing in change, and 'cheap is another way to say scared' — you promise the same thing, only cheaper, and low price is the last refuge of a marketer out of generous ideas.
- Watch the demand-matching failure mode: Tesla's fixed-price story reassured luxury buyers, while Uber's demand-matched pricing cost it brand trust. For your own store, test demand-based pricing only where your customers can articulate the fairness story.
- If you charge a premium, make generosity of change, empathy and respect the axis of competition — the source argues customers will pay a lot if you genuinely change them.
Caveat: The Tesla and Uber outcomes are the source's illustrative examples, not benchmarks for your store.
Caveat: Scarcity and FOMO tactics depend on real limits; fake scarcity damages the trust you're trying to build.

Tags: brand, pricing, offer-design, product-page, promotion, positioning, discounts
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Free Ideas, Paid Souvenirs: Structuring Your Giveaway-to-Purchase Ladder
Use when: Deciding what to give away free (content, guides, tools, samples) versus what to charge for in your product line and funnel.
- Split your offering into two married categories: free ideas that spread easily, and expensive expressions of those ideas that are worth paying for.
- Make the free layer genuinely free and easy to find, engage with frequently, and share — the source's examples are a chef giving recipes or appearing on podcasts while charging $24 for the plated dish, and free radio songs with $84 concert tickets.
- Treat the paid item as a 'souvenir' of the free idea: it should feel worth paying for because it delivers the idea in an elevated, tangible form (china and a tablecloth, a live show).
- In an ecommerce context, the free layer might be education, calculators, guides or community content, and the paid layer a physical or service expression of it. The source's demonstrated cases are chef/restaurant and musician/concert, not ecommerce, so treat the mapping as an application, not a proven result.
- Do not go fully free: the source gives two reasons — (1) a free transaction loses scarcity, tension and enrollment, and (2) without cash flow you can't invest in product, team or marketing.
- Remember free is a different category of transaction, not a penny less: a free idea can spread at effectively infinite scale, but you still need the paid expression to make a living.
Caveat: The source's free-plus-paid examples come from restaurants and music; the ecommerce version is an adaptation, not a documented ecommerce result.

Tags: brand, offer-design, content-strategy, monetization, acquisition, funnel
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## High Price as a Trust-Building Device (Not a Trust Killer)
Use when: Deciding whether to discount to overcome buyer hesitation on a high-consideration item, membership or premium service.
- Recognize the source's pattern: because a large purchase is risky, cognitive dissonance makes buyers invent trust to justify the commitment ('I'm smart, so I must trust this seller').
- Use commitment as an alignment tool. The source points to boot camp, Outward Bound and high-end restaurants that survive bad reviews: heavy investment of cash, reputation or effort makes people align with the choice they made.
- Therefore, do not assume a discount buys trust. The source explicitly says lowering your price doesn't make you more trusted — it does the opposite.
- For ecommerce, this matters most for high-ticket items and subscriptions: invest in the pre-purchase story, proof and onboarding that support the buyer's justification rather than cutting the price to reduce risk.
- Be honest about which risk you are asking buyers to take (money, time, effort, reputation) and design the offer around earning the justification, not around removing all cost.
- Balance the two readings: trust is generally required before risky transactions, but the same risk can also generate trust after commitment. Use the second effect to support premium positioning, not to excuse bad products.
Caveat: This is the author's explanation of a psychological effect, illustrated with examples; it is not a recommendation to raise prices without supporting quality and service.

Tags: brand, pricing, trust, positioning, retention, high-ticket
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Who Is This Change For: Segmenting by Status, Not Just Demographics
Use when: Rolling out a pricing, packaging, service-model or policy change that will affect existing customers, and choosing which customers you are willing to lose.
- State the change plainly: what is changing, and who is it for. Accept the source's position that a change like this can't be for everyone — some customers will have to be told 'it's not for you, sorry.'
- Map how each segment's status is affected. USHG eliminated tipping and raised prices 20 percent to fund parental leave, fair wages and professional treatment; diners who enjoyed status from leaving a big tip lost that thrill, while diners whose worldview is fairness and dignity gained a reason to prefer the restaurant.
- Give customers a story they can tell themselves and others about choosing you — a small purchase that signals something larger, as the source describes virtue signaling for hosts choosing USHG.
- Answer the source's three questions for your own change: Who is it for? What is it for? How is status changed?
- Answer the fourth: 'What will I tell the others?' — if the change gives buyers a shareable reason, it can serve as marketing; if not, expect friction.
- For ecommerce, apply this to subscription price increases, bundling shifts, shipping-fee changes and loyalty-tier redesigns: write the customer-facing story for each affected segment before launching.
Caveat: The 20 percent increase and no-tipping model are USHG's specific case, not a template figure.
Caveat: Existing customers who value the old status dynamic may leave; budget for that rather than assuming everyone stays.

Tags: brand, pricing, positioning, customer-research, retention, loyalty, subscrip
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Earn Permission Before You Buy Attention: The Owned-List Rule
Use when: Deciding where to invest acquisition effort and how to treat email/SMS lists, subscriptions and follower bases.
- Define real permission the source's way: if you stopped showing up, people would be concerned and ask where you went. Presumed, legalistic or fine-print permission doesn't count.
- Earn it incrementally like dating: don't ask for the sale at first contact; make a promise ('I will send X'), keep it, and don't expand the usage without agreement.
- Make a specific, kept promise the core of your email or SMS program — the source notes Woot's daily sales pitch as a valid permission model because it was the promised deal.
- Treat the list as a physical asset: the author says to act as if someone spamming the list to hit a metric had walked out with your laptops.
- Prefer owned channels over rented ones. Social platforms are sharecropping: you don't hold permission, the platform does, and it monetizes by charging you to reach people you thought you already had.
- Measure and grow the metric the source highlights: how many people would reach out and complain if you didn't send the next message?
- Let subscribers choose what they hear and in what format; the internet allows differentiated, opted-in communication.
Caveat: The source's 70 percent open rate and 33 percent response rate come from Yoyodyne in the 1990s and are not modern benchmarks.

Tags: brand, retention, email, owned-media, acquisition, permission, customer-research
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Generosity at the Top of the Funnel: Launch to Neophiliacs Without Selling
Use when: Planning a product launch, community seeding or influencer campaign for a brand-new offer with no existing audience.
- Identify the neophiliacs in your category — the people who seek out new voices, ideas and options — and note the source's warning that there aren't many of them, but there may be enough.
- Go where those people already gather and have opted in, rather than starting with broad ads. The source's example is Marvel launching at San Diego Comic-Con a year before the film, giving a coming attraction, a director interview and actual news instead of ticket selling.
- Be generous rather than promotional at this stage: give attention-worthy content and signal 'this is the sort of thing people like us will be talking about.'
- Treat the early phase as earning permission and attention over time, not immediate conversion.
- Accept that the size of the early audience doesn't matter; the quality of the story, empathy and generosity does, and word of mouth is the intended output.
- Build tension and status into the early experience so early users become connected ambassadors (see the closing lesson of the HugDug case, below).
Caveat: This is a launch-strategy argument illustrated with film and technology examples; it does not guarantee organic spread.
Caveat: Early adopters want new and risky; the mass market wants proven and safe, so the same message will not carry you across (see the chasm card).

Tags: brand, acquisition, launch, content-strategy, community, early-adopters
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Remarkability Is Decided by Users, So Build Talkability Into the Product
Use when: Designing product, packaging, service or post-purchase moments intended to generate word-of-mouth.
- Build remarkability into the product or service with intent — the source argues effective marketers are therefore in charge of the customer experience, not just the message.
- Accept the asymmetry: whether something is remarkable is the user's decision, not yours; if they remark on it, it's remarkable.
- Optimize for self-interested sharing: people spread word because it boosts their taste, standing, or desire for novelty and change — 'look at how good my taste is.'
- Design the post-purchase moment so the buyer gets a reason to talk: something notable to show, tell or gift.
- Avoid stunts. The source explicitly distinguishes stunts (offensive, juvenile, urgent or selfish attention grabs) from genuine remarkability, warning that people reciprocate by shunning and censuring those who cross lines.
- Test the rule that even an anti-sharing rule can backfire into sharing — Fight Club's first rule became an invitation to talk — but use that dynamic knowingly, not as a cheap stunt.
- For ecommerce specifically: consider the unboxing, the surprise insert, the genuinely novel product feature, or a shareable result the buyer can post; and check whether your own team would bring it up unprompted.
Caveat: The source gives qualitative reasoning and cultural examples, not measured lift from remarkability in stores.

Tags: brand, word-of-mouth, product-page, creative-strategy, packaging, customer-experience, differentiation
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Designing Evangelism Into the Offer: Giving Customers a Reason to Tell Others
Use when: Building referral mechanics, shareable content, community features or product designs meant to generate peer-to-peer spread.
- Start from the source's premise: ideas travel horizontally, person to person, not from organization to customer, so you must begin with the smallest possible core and give them something to talk about and a reason to do so.
- Give people a 'why' to share, not an instruction. The source says it is never the case that people tell friends because you want them to, ask them to, or worked hard — give them a why, and that usually means changing what you offer.
- Design the sharer's benefit into the exchange: it furthers their goals and lets them tell a story about themselves they're proud of.
- Borrow the AA pattern of generosity: members talk about it as an act of service and a lifeline, not as a promotion — the source calls it a shame killer and a fellowship.
- Answer the two bridge questions for any shared item: 'What will I tell my friends?' and 'Why will I tell them?'
- In practice for a store: add referral or group-buy mechanics where the sharer's own life improves when a friend joins, and write the customer-facing script so the sharer can tell the story without awkwardness.
Caveat: The awkwardness of pushing friends to a shopping site is real; the HugDug case shows that even low-effort sharing can fail without tension and a status story.

Tags: brand, referral, word-of-mouth, community, offer-design, acquisition, retention
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Fixing a Leaky Funnel: Six Levers for Store Conversion
Use when: Diagnosing where a store or landing-page funnel loses buyers and deciding what to change next.
- Attract the right people at the top rather than maximizing raw traffic volume.
- Align the promise that brought the visitor in with the destination you want them to reach — mismatch between what you say and what they hear is a named leak.
- Remove steps so fewer decisions are required; note the source's explicit tension here: each extra click makes the funnel more expensive, but remove too many and buyers won't trust you enough to purchase.
- Support people as they move through: reinforce their dreams and ameliorate their fears along the way.
- Use tension to create forward motion, and recognize that saying yes is often more stressful than walking away — the source says people generally become more distracted, fearful and eager to run away as they progress.
- Hand successfully engaged customers a megaphone, a tool to tell the others, completing the 'people like us do things like this' cycle.
- Recognize that trust is not static through the funnel; it shifts as people move from stranger to friend to customer to loyal customer.
Caveat: The source presents this as a framework, not a tested sequence with measured lift per lever.

Tags: brand, conversion-optimization, checkout, landing-page, funnel, customer-experience, measurement
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Funnel Math with LTV: When Ads Pay and When to Fix the Funnel First
Use when: Deciding ad budgets and channels, diagnosing an unprofitable paid campaign, or setting the target CAC for a store.
- Lay out the funnel as explicit stages. The source's illustration: spend $1,000 on online ads reaching a million people, get twenty clicks ($50 per click), one in ten of those becomes an order ($500 per order). Recreate this with your own store's numbers before scaling.
- Establish the lifetime value of a customer, which the source calls the most important figure to determine. Use the supermarket example as the model: ignore the $1–2 profit on a single trip; a shopper spending $100 twice a week for five years is $50,000+ in sales, about $1,000 profit at 2 percent margin.
- Add referral value if regular customers bring in other regulars; the source says this makes each customer more valuable as an engine of growth.
- The buy-rule from the source's direct-mail framing: if stamps cost 50 cents and you need 1,000 letters per order, each order costs $500. If LTV is $700, buy as many as you can; if LTV is $400, you have no business buying them — get a better letter or a better business.
- Apply the source's two hard rules: if you can't see the funnel, don't buy the ads; if you can measure it and it still costs too much, don't buy the ads — fix the funnel first.
- Optimize for step shifting and elimination and for lifetime value rather than only shaving cost per click. Lowering CPC with a ridiculous ad promise backfires: trust drops once buyers are inside and yield plummets.
Caveat: The source notes google/facebook take roughly half of online ad spend and skim much of the advertiser's profit; margins here are illustrative.
Caveat: Numbers are the author's examples (and a rough 2018 estimate that about 60 percent of advertisers measure results); use your own data.

Tags: brand, measurement, acquisition, customer-acquisition-cost, lifetime-value, funnel, paid-ads
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Why Magical Self-Running Funnels Are Rare — Build the Post-Click Cohort
Use when: Evaluating the promise of an automated or passive acquisition system, and deciding how much effort to put after the first click.
- Reject the passive-funnel fantasy. The source says these magical funnels are rare because the lifetime value of a new customer rarely exceeds the cost of ads to acquire them, given distrust and web clutter.
- Adopt the source's operating model: prime the pump with ads aimed at neophiliacs, then build trust with frequency, gain trial, generate word of mouth, and make it pay with a cohort that needs your work to be part of who they are.
- Put disproportionate effort into what happens after the first click, which the source calls the easy thing to skip and the expensive part to neglect.
- Accept that most thriving brands are primed by advertising but built by good marketing — users evangelizing to friends, and communities coalescing around cultural change.
- Avoid pushing too hard and too fast to force perpetual motion; the source says that ends in bitterness because it can't last.
- For a store: budget for retention, repeat purchase and community alongside paid acquisition, and judge new customer cohorts by whether they return, not just by first-order ROAS.
Caveat: The source's claims about funnel economics are opinions and examples, and include an aside that he is unsure why funnel math confuses people; verify against your own P&L.

Tags: brand, retention, measurement, paid-ads, lifetime-value, funnel, cohorts
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Trust Through Action: What You Do Outweighs What You Say
Use when: Handling refunds, defective products, data problems, fulfillment errors or any moment when the store's behavior is visible to a customer.
- Choose your path deliberately. The source says marketers land in one of three places: ignored, sneaking around, or trusted — and only trust pays for the investment.
- Commit to the third path: make a promise and keep it, earning enrollment, then attention, then the ability to tell a story, then more trust and word of mouth.
- Favor doing over talking. The source asks the concrete test: when a customer asked for a refund on a defective product, what did you do? When you lost data, what did you do?
- Focus on what you do when no one is watching, one person at a time, day by day — rather than a press conference for the masses.
- Recognize that benefit of the doubt is scarce and is only granted when people are already heading where they think you're going and their identity and status are on the line.
- Apply it to fulfillment and support policy: the source's supermarket reasoning implies it is hardly worth a thousand dollars in lost lifetime sales to argue over a four-dollar unripe melon. Set refund and return thresholds accordingly.
Caveat: The supermarket example is the source's illustration of LTV logic, not a universal refund policy; set your own threshold from your LTV data.

Tags: brand, trust, retention, customer-service, returns, fulfillment, lifetime-value
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Famous to the Smallest Viable Audience, Not the Biggest Number
Use when: Deciding where to concentrate brand and PR effort, and how to measure community reach versus raw audience size.
- Adopt the source's framing: everyone is famous to about fifteen hundred people, some to three thousand, and for a consultant, designer or inventor, being famous to the right three thousand is plenty.
- Define your smallest viable audience — the number and type of people who make the business work — and aim your store's marketing at them rather than at everyone.
- Use fame functionally: it breeds trust partly because people your buyers already trust have heard of you. Prioritize credibility within the tribe over broad reach.
- Choose public relations over publicity. The source distinguishes public relations (telling your story to the right people in the right way) from a publicist chasing clips, and warns against ink at all costs.
- Treat the peer-to-peer trust transferred through trusted voices as the goal, and be aware the author expects the value of broad fame to fade over time as more people become famous.
Caveat: The 1,500–3,000 figures are the author's heuristics, not computed audience benchmarks.

Tags: brand, positioning, audience, publicity, community, trust
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## The Chasm: Why Early Buyers Won't Carry You to the Mass Market
Use when: Planning category entry, product-market expansion or launch sequencing when early-adopter traction has not converted into mainstream sales.
- Recognize the gap Geoff Moore named in the Rogers adoption curve: early adopters buy new, interesting, slightly risky things because that is what they like, and there aren't enough of them to build a mass business.
- Understand why the crossing so often fails: satisfying early adopters may require annoying the masses, because the very thing your product did (break the pattern) is what the mass market does not want. The source's illustration is watching the help desk at an Apple store — the middle of the curve is barely adapting.
- Build the bridge on network effects. The source's rule: people won't talk about your new chocolate because friends eating it doesn't improve their life, but they will talk about a communication app because their life improves when friends join.
- Answer the two questions the bridge rests on: what will I tell my friends, and why will I tell them? Then change what you offer so the answer is a real incentive, not a request.
- Expect the Gartner hype trough — neophiliacs get bored and the mass market disdains you — and treat that moment as when you most need a bridge story matched to a more conservative worldview.
- For a store, ask whether your product creates ratchet value for the buyer when neighbors or coworkers adopt it; if not, plan a different bridge (proof, safety, pattern-matching) rather than relying on viral spread.
Caveat: The source frames this as Moore's chasm applied to marketing generally; specifics about which stores can bridge it are your application, not documented results.

Tags: brand, positioning, adoption, network-effects, launch, word-of-mouth, strategy
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Crossing the Local Chasm: Yo-Yos, Facebook and Village Water
Use when: Seeding a product in a specific community, campus, niche or region before attempting broader rollout.
- Seed with the right early adopter, not the first volunteer. In the source's schoolyard example, the first kid with the yo-yo was the wrong kid on the wrong day; weeks later a charismatic fifth-grader founded the Yo-yo Union, was skilled but not intimidating, and brought three more yo-yos for friends who were each leaders in their own right.
- Keep entry cost low and the payoff quick, so a core forms fast — the source describes thirty kids within a week and the whole school soon after.
- Then add stickiness. The source notes yo-yos cooled off as fast as they spread, while Uggs, black backpacks and penny skateboards persisted because of identity and persistence.
- Select local tribes where insecure, status-seeking, well-connected members already gather; the source's Facebook reading is that it started at Harvard with students who had urgent status needs, fast connections, spare time and a desire to be seen.
- Make the early product a badge and a conversation starter. In the Water Health International case, the brightly colored jerry cans signaled adoption and invited conversation.
- Use vivid local demonstration to shift habit, as WHI does by bringing a microscope projector into classrooms so children see the germs and parasites in their own household water samples — the source says changing a multigenerational habit takes time even with obvious benefits.
Caveat: Facebook's and WHI's outcomes are case illustrations; the author also warns that the final leap to a huge mass market is unlikely for most marketers.

Tags: brand, launch, community, adoption, positioning, local-marketing, network-effects
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Divide the Market into Short Heads: Escaping the Long-Tail Trap
Use when: Choosing product selection strategy, niche assortment breadth, or deciding whether to compete as a tiny seller on a giant marketplace.
- Understand the shape from the source: hits on the left each sell enormously, while the long tail doesn't sell much per item but collectively equals the short head — half of Amazon's book sales are titles outside the top five thousand.
- Accept the economics honestly. The source's stark warning is that these marketplaces depend on the hopes of individuals far out on the long tail: the internet can live on tiny slices, but you can't.
- Recognize that aggregating the tail can work. The April Fools'/Passover/Easter/birthday T-shirt would sell about four units alone, but companies making tens of thousands of print-on-demand designs together sell thousands per month.
- If you are not aggregating, take the way out: build the definitive, most essential, extraordinary contribution to your field, and connect the market so they see you belong in the short head.
- Name your niche market explicitly (the source lists teen literary fiction, chip carving, GH5 filmmaking, improv) and make the hit that serves as glue: the DeWalt hammer drill and Rocky Horror are cited as things that unite 'people like us.'
- For product selection, ask both questions: does this item create or dominate a short head for a defined audience, and does it help that audience recognize each other?
- Treat outlier success stories as wishes, not strategies — the source says becoming an outlier isn't a strategy.
Caveat: The Amazon halves and T-shirt volume are the source's illustrative numbers, not audited data.

Tags: brand, product-selection, niche, positioning, assortment, differentiation, marketplaces
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Learning From HugDug's Failure: Trial Without Whales and Tension Dies
Use when: Designing a user-generated, community, referral or affiliate program and setting realistic time-to-traction expectations.
- Review what HugDug did: users could build a page profiling an Amazon product in about four minutes, with a royalty split to charity; the bet was that authors would promote their own books and Pinterest-style users would build pages at scale.
- Look at the failure pattern honestly: they got trial (thousands of pages) but fewer than six people built more than a dozen pages or promoted them much; tension dissipated after a single visit with no reason to return, and many pages sold zero books in a month.
- Diagnose the three named causes and check your own program against them: insufficient incentives, no tension dynamic turning early users into connected ambassadors, and no status story.
- Also note the stated difficulty: it remains emotionally hard to push friends to a shopping site even when it is technically easy.
- Apply the fix to your program design: be specific about who your first customers are and what they might want, believe and say; create a status story; and build a reason to return repeatedly.
- Set realistic timelines — the source's team concluded they were naive to think four months was enough to create an overnight success, and calls a Kickstarter-like success harder than it looks.
Caveat: This is the author's own failed venture, presented as a lesson; the causes are his diagnosis, not an independently verified post-mortem.

Tags: brand, referral, community, retention, customer-research, status, program-design
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Casey Neistat's Funnel Numbers: Reading Stage Conversions Realistically
Use when: Setting realistic expectations for a content-to-conversion funnel and deciding which stage to improve.
- Use the source's chain as a mental model for your own staged funnel: about one in fifty viewers clicked from YouTube to the Twitch stream (10M+ views, 1M seen at the time, 18,000 on the stream).
- Then about one in eighteen of those stream viewers posted a comment (roughly 1,000 comments), and of those, perhaps five took a further action such as signing up.
- Apply this in a store: measure each transition separately (content view → product page click → add to cart → purchase → repeat) rather than only the endpoints, and expect steep attrition between each.
- Note the source's own diagnosis of what makes this work: the reason isn't funnel optimization, it's that the top is regularly and effortlessly filled with people already enrolled in his journey.
- Prioritize strengthening the top-of-funnel enrollment and the trust behind it over micro-optimizing deep stages, since the source says everything gets better once you earn that trust.
- Use exact numbers for your own funnel, treating these as an illustrative shape rather than a benchmark.
Caveat: These are the author's approximate, single-observation numbers from one creator at one time, explicitly 'your mileage may vary,' and are not benchmarks for stores.

Tags: brand, funnel, measurement, content-strategy, conversion-optimization, audience, metrics
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Make early product adoption visible and easy to discuss
Use when: Launching an unfamiliar product or replenishment habit where mainstream shoppers need peer reassurance.
- Identify the early adopters who actively try new products and can describe their experience to the intended customer community.
- Give the product a recognizable, useful artifact: packaging, a refill vessel or a visible usage result that naturally invites questions. Apply the source principle of the distinctive water container; this is an ecommerce adaptation, not an ecommerce result from that case.
- Help customers demonstrate the practical benefit in their normal setting rather than relying only on brand claims. Make product education and sharing easy.
- Observe whether adoption extends beyond novelty-seekers before scaling the same acquisition message to everyone.
Caveat: The source case concerns water adoption in a village, not a measured online-store conversion experiment.
Caveat: Visibility and conversation do not establish incrementality; price, access and product usefulness also affect adoption.

Tags: brand, product-launch, word-of-mouth, packaging, customer-education
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Write a founder-to-customer narrative using self, us and now
Use when: Writing an About page, launch email or founder-led product video.
- Tell the story of self: explain the real experience that led the founder to understand the product problem and what changed.
- Tell the story of us: show why that experience matters to the specific shoppers being served, using their shared situation rather than a generic mission statement.
- Tell the story of now: invite a relevant next step, such as exploring the product, using a guide or joining a real launch event. Explain why the timing matters if there is a genuine reason.
- Check that the product and customer experience deliver the promise made by the narrative.
Caveat: This applies the source narrative framework to store content; the source does not establish a conversion uplift.
Caveat: Do not manufacture urgency, vulnerability or customer outcomes.

Tags: brand, founder-story, brand-story, email, product-launch
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Build customer community around shared practices
Use when: Designing a brand community for products connected to a shared hobby, practice or identity.
- Define the interest, goal and language that members already share, independently of the brand.
- Create participation around that interest: product-use demonstrations, member stories, shared challenges or helpful exchanges. These are merchant applications of the source community principle.
- Use recognizable artifacts and rituals to reinforce belonging when they genuinely help members participate.
- Listen to what members need and invest in their progress; do not assume the brand owns the community or can dictate its goals.
Caveat: Community participation is not automatically profitable retention; evaluate the store outcome separately.
Caveat: Avoid hostile in-group messaging or manipulation to manufacture belonging.

Tags: brand, community, customer-experience, retention, brand-identity
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Maintain the community after launch excitement fades
Use when: Planning ownership and cadence for a store community, loyalty program or customer education series.
- Assign an owner and a sustainable recurring cadence for useful customer interaction after launch.
- Keep helping existing customers use and enjoy the product instead of constantly replacing them with the next audience.
- Provide fresh reasons to participate while maintaining the recognizable brand promise and customer experience.
- Monitor whether participation and repeat engagement are fading and use customer feedback to adjust the program.
Caveat: The source describes activity decay metaphorically as a half-life; it does not provide a measurable universal decay rate.
Caveat: Do not assume engagement proves repeat-purchase impact.

Tags: brand, community, retention, loyalty, customer-education
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Give cautious shoppers repeated consistent evidence
Use when: A product attracts early adopters but struggles to reach more cautious shoppers.
- Distinguish buyers seeking novelty from those seeking reassurance and evidence that the brand will remain dependable.
- Provide consistent demonstrations, reviews, usage guidance and customer support across the consideration journey rather than changing the promise after each campaign.
- Plan follow-up content around unresolved product questions, not repeated pressure to buy.
- Judge the approach over the actual purchase cycle; avoid imposing a fixed number of touchpoints from an unrelated example.
Caveat: Adapted from the source account of repeated local cookware demonstrations; no fixed online conversion frequency is established.

Tags: brand, purchase-consideration, customer-education, trust, product-launch
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Position an upgrade around a meaningful change in identity or outcome
Use when: Launching a premium replacement or upgrade in a mature product category.
- Identify what the target buyer values about the product they already own and why replacing it is not currently urgent.
- Find a credible improvement that changes the story of ownership: capability, craftsmanship, convenience or identity supported by the actual product.
- Make that difference visible in product comparisons, demonstrations and merchandising rather than relying on a higher price alone.
- Validate that the intended segment values the difference before generalizing the proposition to the whole market.
Caveat: The source uses early Tesla positioning as a historical illustration; its claims are not current specifications or ecommerce performance evidence.
Caveat: Avoid unsupported superiority claims or shaming customers for owning an alternative.

Tags: brand, premium-positioning, product-launch, differentiation, merchandising
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Ship a credible brand experience and improve from customer response
Use when: Deciding whether a product page or brand launch is ready for a limited initial release.
- Define the customer promise and ensure the product information, checkout and fulfillment experience can deliver it.
- Release the usable version to the intended audience and collect specific questions, confusion and purchase feedback.
- Use engagement to understand where the promise or experience needs improvement, then make targeted revisions.
- Distinguish a message or audience mismatch from evidence that the underlying product does not satisfy a real need.
Caveat: This applies the source good-enough-then-better principle; it is not permission to skip product safety, accurate claims or working transactions.
Caveat: A weak response may reflect demand, audience or offer problems, not merely design polish.

Tags: brand, product-launch, brand-experience, customer-feedback, iteration
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Audit brand promises for value the customer actually receives
Use when: Reviewing a campaign or product-page promise that could raise short-term conversion while harming trust.
- Write down what a reasonable shopper will believe they are buying after reading the message.
- Compare that expectation with actual product capability, delivery, usage effort and ongoing charges.
- Remove misleading implications and explain material limits where the purchasing decision is made.
- Use customer complaints, returns and repeat-purchase feedback to identify gaps between the story and the delivered experience.
Caveat: This is an ecommerce application of the source argument about marketer responsibility, not a quantified growth tactic.

Tags: brand, brand-trust, product-page, customer-experience, claims
Source: This Is Marketing_ You Can’t Be Seen Until You Learn to Se (1).docx

---

## Run the Comfort Trap check on product and name candidates
Use when: Choosing which new product, bundle concept, or brand name to take forward from a shortlist of candidates.
- Before finalizing a product or name shortlist, collect team and customer reactions and note the spread, not just the average score.
- Treat an idea that roughly half the reviewers hate and half love as a live candidate: the source says polarizing ideas contain energy and distinctiveness, whereas ideas everyone mildly approves of usually sit in the 'invisible zone,' safe but unremarkable.
- Do not use consensus as the tiebreaker. If every idea on the shortlist reads as agreeable and forgettable, the process has probably filtered out the differentiated ones, so go back and generate more polarizing candidates.
- Keep the trap in mind at the point of decision: the comfortable, unanimous option is the one most likely to be forgotten by shoppers, while the one that provokes strong reaction is the one with a story worth telling on the PDP and in creative.
- Pair this with actual demand evidence, not just reaction spread — a polarizing name still has to clear legal, sourcing and margin checks before launch.
Caveat: Polarization is a signal of distinctiveness, not proof of commercial viability; a name or product can be polarizing for the wrong reason (confusing, offensive, hard to say).
Caveat: This is a decision heuristic from the source's creative-naming experience, not a measured conversion benchmark.

Tags: brand, product-selection, naming, positioning, creative-strategy, team-process
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Suspend evaluation to keep ideas alive during ideation
Use when: Running a brainstorm for a product launch, seasonal campaign, bundle concept or PDP angle where early criticism keeps killing options.
- Separate generation from evaluation: in the idea phase, forbid verdicts like 'that won't work' or 'too expensive' and collect ideas first.
- When a concern does surface, reframe it as a problem to solve rather than a rejection. The source's example phrasings: replace 'That's too expensive' with 'I wish we could make that so it wasn't expensive'; replace 'That will never pass legal' with 'How do we modify that so it's legally available?'
- Apply the same reframing in ecommerce terms: instead of 'we can't ship that,' ask how the offer, packaging or fulfillment model could change to make it shippable.
- Capture the reframed constraints as follow-up tasks so the idea is not lost, then evaluate once the group has exhausted its output.
- The goal is psychological safety and courage: teams that fear instant rejection stop proposing the distinctive ideas that later become your differentiators.
Caveat: Suspending evaluation is an ideation-phase rule, not a license to skip feasibility, margin or compliance review before launch.
Caveat: Someone still has to run the evaluation pass; deferring judgment indefinitely stalls decisions.

Tags: brand, team-process, creative-strategy, product-selection, innovation
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Use approximate thinking to collect half-formed ideas
Use when: Designing a team or customer ideation session for new products, offers, campaigns or merchandising concepts.
- Introduce a spectrum with 'bizarre/absurd' at one end and 'safe/workable' at the other.
- Ask participants to deliberately target the middle: ideas that are neither so wild they are unusable nor so safe they are generic.
- Explicitly give permission to present unpolished, partially formed ideas, and instruct people to stop at the approximate stage rather than polishing before sharing.
- Collect those approximate ideas as raw material for a later evaluation and refinement pass, since the source's point is to evaluate ideas before they are fully baked or prematurely thrown out.
- In an ecommerce setting, this works for new bundle concepts, subscription mechanics, landing-page angles or product line extensions: gather the rough versions from merchandising, marketing, ops and support, then filter afterward.
Caveat: Approximate ideas still need a real evaluation and feasibility pass; the framework delays judgement, it does not replace it.
Caveat: If the team only ever produces safe ideas, the spectrum instruction is not working and the framing may need to be re-set.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, team-process, innovation, product-selection, ideation
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Name as Highest-Frequency Brand Lever
Use when: Deciding how much effort and budget to put into naming an ecommerce brand, product line or sub-brand before launch instead of accepting a Friday-afternoon placeholder name.
- Reframe the decision: the name is used more often and for longer than any other brand element — every ad, pack, PDP title, email subject line and customer mention repeats it, so an okay name versus the right name compounds over years.
- Evaluate candidates against three jobs: (1) get attention in a saturated feed and shelf, (2) hold attention by being processing-fluent — pronounceable and partially understandable, (3) be surprising rather than comfortable or popular.
- Ask how the name creates asymmetric advantage for your specific category: does it claim a benefit competitors cannot credibly claim on their own label?
- Use the Swiffer versus Ready Mop comparison as the source's illustration, not a benchmark: same floor-cleaning job, Swiffer named to put fun and efficiency into a chore people dislike, Ready Mop described the product they already understood. The source attributes the early gap largely to the name and believes it matters most in the first roughly 12 months of interest, retail listings and press.
- Treat that as a presenter's opinion about launch momentum, not a measured causal result.
Caveat: Name advantage is stated as most influential in the launch window; after that, marketplace dynamics take over.
Caveat: Source is a naming-agency creative director speaking from his portfolio; no independent attribution data is presented.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, product-selection, packaging, differentiation, creative-strategy
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Run a Category Audit Before Naming Anything
Use when: Naming a new product, sub-brand or storefront when the category is full of descriptive names (fiber, mop, natural, fresh) and you need a defensible direction rather than a list of words.
- Audit the landscape: list the incumbent names and labels in the category. Expect patterns like 'Fiber One', 'Fibre Plus', 'Ready Mop' — descriptive and comfortable. The source's first working hypothesis is usually 'we do not know where we are going, but we know we are not going there.'
- Audit your own product: build the list of what is actually different — ingredients, process, format, materials, usage.
- Audit the buyer: why do they buy in this category, what do they expect, and what is the ultimate benefit up the ladder from the functional one? For a cleaning tool the ultimate benefit included less drudgery; the source found people happily polish furniture or wash windows but nobody wants to mop.
- Ask the four sequence questions: How do you define winning here? What do you have to win (real product facts)? What do you need to win (the breakthrough claim)? What do we need to say? Different stakeholders answer 'winning' differently — surface that before creating.
- Only then articulate a naming strategy, and accept that a name cannot carry everything: packaging, copy and go-to-market carry the rest.
- Ecommerce application: this audit doubles as your PDP positioning brief — the ultimate benefit you uncover becomes the hero claim, not the ingredient list.
Caveat: You may return to a rejected direction once the product truth is clearer; the first hypothesis is a starting filter, not a verdict.
Caveat: For ordinary commodity products without a breakthrough feature, the differentiation often has to come from the benefit framing rather than a unique ingredient.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, customer-research, product-page, offer-design
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Name the Ultimate Benefit, Not the Product Category
Use when: Writing PDP hero copy, naming a new SKU, or repositioning a supplement, food or personal-care product where every competitor's label says the same category word.
- Observe that most category labels assume buyers already know the category is good for them — the source's example: every fiber package says 'fiber' and stops there.
- Climb the benefit ladder with real customer language: gut health, regularity, metabolism, digestion, feeling lighter.
- Watch physical reactions as much as words — in the source's exercise, a posture shift and an upward hand motion produced 'you feel lighter' as the real territory, and the naming director latched onto it.
- Build the naming territory from that endpoint rather than the ingredient. From 'light' the source's hunting grounds included Greek and Latin roots for light, expressions about feeling light, aerodynamics and how a wing generates lift.
- Check the resulting name against shelf reality: the example was a new fiber brand called 'Feather' sitting between 'Fiber Plus' and Metamucil, plus a supporting line such as a lightweight fiber, with 'fiber' still present on the label so shoppers are not confused.
- Ecommerce translation: put the category word in metadata, filters and secondary copy for search, and put the benefit word in the brand name and hero headline.
- Keep the claim honest — the name can lead the benefit, but the product has to deliver it.
Caveat: The Feather example is an in-conversation thought exercise, not a launched brand with results.
Caveat: Forcing an abstract benefit name onto a highly search-driven commodity category can hurt discoverability unless the category term stays visible in titles and tags.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, product-page, copywriting, customer-research, offer-design
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Quantity Leads to Quality: Generate Roughly 2,000 Names
Use when: Running any divergent naming, campaign or creative sprint for a store, SKU or ad concept and tempted to converge after the first hundred ideas.
- Set the volume expectation explicitly: the source said clients often come back stuck with 50-100 names, while his team works toward roughly 2,000 candidate words written down. These are not 2,000 good names — most are raw material he internally calls 'trash'.
- Separate generation from evaluation in time: run a divergent cycle, then a second cycle, then start cutting. The source's cycle is roughly two rounds over about two to two and a half weeks, with team members also working on other projects.
- After the divergence, return to the creative framework and objectives and ask whether each angle produced anything usable before shipping a shortlist to legal.
- Apply to ecommerce creative broadly: the same volume logic holds for ad hooks, PDP headline variants and email subject lines, where the good ones are usually found by writing many bad ones first.
- Budget volume against your clock — the source can pursue this because names are his core product; a small operator should scale the number down but keep the ratio of generation to selection.
Caveat: 2,000 is the source agency's working number for a paid naming engagement, not a required target for every creative task.
Caveat: Volume without an explicit selection framework just produces a longer unusable list.

Tags: brand, naming, creative-strategy, ideation, experiment-design, process
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Replace Brainstorming With Small Teams and Shifting Briefs
Use when: Structuring an internal or agency naming/creative sprint for a new brand, SKU or campaign and deciding how many people should work on it and with what information.
- Drop the large brainstorm: the source cites peer pressure in groups and the difficulty of stopping the group's evaluation cascade with four, six or ten people, plus a slow grind. He ran an internal study across roughly 18 months and concluded his best names came from individuals or two-person teams, not freelancers or brainstorm sessions.
- Split into small teams with different information diets. In the source's fiber example: Team 1 knows everything about the product; Team 2 knows it is fiber plus an added energy or mineral ingredient; Team 3 is not briefed on fiber at all and works on athletic performance. The third brief produces names the first two would never reach.
- Expect three distinct lists rather than one blended list, and keep them separate so you can see which framing generates the energy.
- Give teams explicit permission to fail — the source states that if the team is not failing, it is not doing the job. Treat the raw output as a treasure hunt rather than a deliverable.
- Ecommerce adaptation: in a small store, simulate this by running the same naming or PDP-copy brief through three different lenses — product specs, buyer outcome, and an unrelated domain (aviation, sport, hometown street names) — rather than three people.
Caveat: The 18-month internal study is the source's private research; methodology is described as 'a little sloppy and crude' by his own account.
Caveat: Two-person teams assume availability; solo operators should substitute sequential briefs, not skip the diversity of framing.

Tags: brand, naming, creative-strategy, ideation, process, team-management
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Systematic Treasure-Hunt Databases for Naming
Use when: Filling the candidate pool for a new brand or product name when the obvious descriptive options are taken or legally blocked.
- Work from commonly available sources first: Latin roots, Greek roots, Roman and Greek mythology. The source cites Intel Pentium as an output of periodic-table work.
- Enrich the source rather than using it raw: the agency loads the periodic table with additional articles and vocabulary so the word pool around each element is much larger than the bare table — that extra layer is where the good candidates appear.
- Build a cross-category archive: the source files names by theme, not by product. A small car for Nissan and a small Intel processor both go into a database called 'small things', so unrelated assignments feed each other.
- Assign short, bounded side hunts inside a naming project — for the 'light' territory the source had someone spend about 15 minutes on what makes an airplane lift.
- Embrace synchronicity: the source describes connecting seemingly irrelevant things as the fundamental layer of creativity, illustrated by Impossible Burger refusing to sound like a natural-foods product that would blend into Whole Foods.
- Ecommerce application: when naming a supplement, subscription box or private-label SKU, run 20-30 minute side hunts into an unrelated domain (aviation, geology, street names, sports) and look for transferable words rather than direct category synonyms.
Caveat: Databases are a starting point, not a source of finished names; raw output needs heavy filtering.
Caveat: Watch for trademark collisions — the source routes shortlists through paralegals and trademark attorneys before presentation.

Tags: brand, naming, creative-strategy, research, ideation, differentiation
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Run a 20-Minute Personal Naming Session
Use when: Sitting down to generate names or headline options yourself for a product, bundle or campaign and needing to get into flow quickly.
- Start with a pad and pen and free-associate about the direction before touching a screen; the source sometimes draws a treasure-map grid and places ideas in it.
- Do not ask AI tools for names. Ask them questions about the subject matter — for a fiber product, questions about fiber, digestion, lightness — and use the answers to seed directions. Verify any tool's current interface and capability, since these change.
- Pick a promising territory, e.g. Greek words for light, aviation and lift, other words for light leading to sunshine and soil, and then start crafting actual candidate names.
- Keep the whole session short: the source describes this as roughly a 20-minute activity, not an arduous process — the point is starting to move, not completing the job in one sitting.
- Treat the paper notes as the raw dump and the crafted names as the output; expect a long session-free interval is not required for solo generation.
- Ecommerce application: run this before writing PDP titles, bundle names or campaign hooks, and keep the resulting direction notes as a tagged file you can revisit for future SKUs.
Caveat: This routine reflects one practitioner's working method, not a validated protocol.
Caveat: Using AI for context rather than name generation is the source's stated approach; his competitive claim now rests on judging names from proprietary data.

Tags: brand, naming, creative-strategy, ideation, ai-tools, process
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Separate Divergence From Evaluation and Reframe Rejections
Use when: Reviewing creative submissions from staff or agencies — naming options, ad concepts, PDP concepts — and catching yourself about to kill an idea in the room.
- Adopt the source's two stock phrases when an idea triggers a 'no': replace 'that's too expensive' with 'I wish we could make that so it wasn't expensive', and replace 'that'll never pass legal' with 'how do we modify that so it's legally available?' Both convert rejection into a solvable problem, which the source says humans are wired to want to solve.
- Use softer openers first: 'that's interesting' or the source's preferred 'I wouldn't have thought of that' — framed as honest credit-giving rather than faint praise.
- Model the behavior visibly: the source, as creative director, says his team knows he will speculate on their list rather than evaluate it harshly. If you are the founder reviewing your own staff's work, that visible restraint is what gives them courage — the source roots 'encouragement' in 'courage'.
- Delay the cutover: give ideas a period where they simply live in the system, then start pairing down. In the agency process trademark review also automatically prunes candidates.
- When you do switch to converging, do it deliberately after about two cycles, revisit the objectives and creative framework, and treat the framework as a window for creation rather than a set of five conformity boxes.
- Ecommerce application: apply this to ad-hook and landing-page headline review so early concepts are not killed by the person with the loudest veto before any traffic test.
Caveat: Permission to speculate does not remove eventual client or business evaluation; the source explicitly says people still have to do the right thing for clients.
Caveat: Soft language only works if the underlying idea later gets a genuine decision — constant deferral stalls the project.

Tags: creative-strategy, ideation, team-management, naming, experiment-design, process
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## The Comfort Trap: Polarizing Names Carry Energy
Use when: Deciding between a safe, easily-approved brand name, campaign concept or offer angle and a polarizing one that some of your team dislikes.
- Draw the two zones: the tension zone, where roughly half the team resists the idea, energy is high and the choice is polarizing; and the invisible zone, where consensus is high, the choice feels safe, and distinctiveness is near zero.
- Expect comfortable names to test well in research precisely because they merely confirm the category — the source's example is Clorox's Ready Mop reading as a mop that respondents understood immediately.
- When an idea polarizes internally, do not discard it automatically. The source's version of this insight came from Andy Grove reviewing Intel's Pentium, who argued a good name is so polarizing that it carries energy — and the source's advice is to examine what inside the word is creating that energy even if you ultimately do not use it.
- Apply the test to ecommerce inputs: a product name, a bundle framing, a founder-led ad concept or a bold hero claim. Ask whether it produces an internal reaction or glides by.
- Pair this with believability: before presenting polarizing candidates, the source's team applies one rule internally — is that believable? The first sub-second reaction must lean toward 'I believe this could exist'.
- Use a context mock-up (see separate card on proof of concept) to test believability before showing the client or publishing.
Caveat: Polarization is a signal of energy, not proof of quality — the source says you may not decide to use it.
Caveat: Believability at first glance is a stated agency rule, not a published benchmark.

Tags: brand, naming, differentiation, positioning, creative-strategy, conversion-optimization
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Present Names in Context, Not as a Spreadsheet
Use when: Presenting name or brand options to founders, retailers or internal stakeholders who rarely make creative decisions and are choosing between unfamiliar candidates.
- Build a 'proof of concept' for two or three finalists before anyone outside the team sees them. Internally the source's team applies one test: does this look believable in context?
- Place the name in realistic settings: a Wall Street Journal-style article layout, an endorsement ad with a famous actor or tennis player in a black-and-white photograph and a line like companies that run well run on [name], or on merchandise and transit creative.
- Use the mock-up to test whether the candidate survives first-glance processing — in under a second the viewer should lean toward believing the thing could actually exist. The example given is Microsoft Azure: believable as a cloud brand even though Microsoft's portfolio is typically descriptive.
- Recognize the psychological reason: most decision-makers have little practice making creative decisions, so context supplies the comparison that a plain list cannot.
- For a store, the equivalent artifacts are a mocked PDP header, an ad in a familiar placement, a packaging visual, or a search results listing — show the name where customers will actually meet it.
- Keep the set small: the source presents roughly 10, sometimes 15 names in a list, but tests believability internally on a narrower finalist set first.
Caveat: Context presentation influences perception and can sway stakeholders — it is a decision aid, not consumer evidence.
Caveat: Mock-ups must not imply real coverage or real endorsements that do not exist.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, landing-page, creative-strategy, merchandising, stakeholder-management
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Score Nameless Knowledge: Judging Names You Did Not Create
Use when: Choosing between shortlisted names for a brand, collection or product line and needing a consistent scoring rationale rather than gut preference.
- Use the source's rule for scoring: no sevens. Force a strong call rather than a lukewarm middle score, because middling marks hide the specific quality that makes a name work.
- Ask whether the name is unexpected for this particular company: the source rated Microsoft Azure highly because Azure is an outlier in a portfolio of descriptive names, and noted the same name would be less surprising from Google.
- Check processing fluency: can a person pronounce it, and is there something understandable inside it? The source describes the target as surprisingly familiar, because brains are lazy but still need an interesting hook.
- Consider sound symbolism: consonant-vowel-consonant-vowel structure is what children first learn, and letters have perceived speed — in the source's framework P, K, B sounds read as reliable and fast, with D, P and Z reading fast, and the letter X signaling innovation in tech and cars.
- Check multi-market legibility, since names are often shipped across several markets; the source tested Blackberry in roughly six countries and languages, but note that testing was expensive.
- Apply to ecommerce: score brand and collection names by how predictable they are for your category (Fiber One, Natural Farms), whether the word is easy to say aloud in a video ad, and whether it survives being heard once rather than seen.
Caveat: The 1-10 scores are the source's subjective creative judgment, made without knowing the companies.
Caveat: Sound-symbolism claims are presented as the agency's accumulated research; they are directional, not guarantees of consumer response.
Caveat: The naming mechanics of specific sound symbolism belong to brand-creative work rather than store operations.

Tags: brand, naming, differentiation, creative-strategy, ad-creative, measurement
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## When to Rename and How to Launch It
Use when: Deciding whether to change a store, product line or brand name that creates friction in search, spelling or customer recall, and planning the relaunch.
- Start from the compounding premise: the longer a name sits in the market the more advantage it accumulates through familiarity, which is the source's argument for getting naming right at the very beginning instead of a Friday-afternoon session.
- Identify friction as the trigger: the source says bad names simply create friction. His cited example was Kodium, which no one knew or could spell and which had poor search performance, renamed to Windsurf over about a six-week period — the source attributes the brand's takeoff partly to the name. Treat that as the source's example, not a guaranteed outcome.
- Check for the simple cases first: being sued is a straightforward reason to rename. Otherwise, work through pluses and minuses and decline the project if the owner is determined to keep the old name at all costs.
- Target young companies: the source says for a Series A or B company a rename is not traumatic, whereas for a Coca-Cola-scale business you need a compelling reason.
- Counter the standard objection with the source's claim: the biggest reason people refuse to rename is fear of losing equity and momentum, and he states he has never seen that loss materialize, provided the launch is done with enthusiasm and a story — 'we were here, now we're going this way, and here is the benefit to you'. Customers know the software and technology have not changed.
- Use the removal of friction as the payoff over the following six to nine months of extra invested resources.
- For a store: migration means new domain or brand assets, redirects, updated marketplace listings, and a customer-facing announcement that leads with what stays the same (products, team, quality) and what improves.
Caveat: The 'never seen equity loss' claim is the source's agency experience, not independent research.
Caveat: Consumer-goods giants with decades of brand equity are explicitly excluded from the easy-rename advice.
Caveat: Renaming carries real SEO, marketplace-listing and repeat-purchase risk that the source does not quantify.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, retention, search, acquisition, process
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Apply a Creative Curiosity Process
Use when: Setting up the brief for naming, positioning or a major PDP rewrite, or diagnosing why your creative output keeps landing in the invisible zone.
- Step 1 — landscape: list what exists in the category and form a first hypothesis about what you are not going to be.
- Step 2 — product: build the factual list of what your product actually has and does.
- Step 3 — consumer: establish why they buy, what they expect, and the ultimate benefit up the ladder.
- Step 4 — strategy questions, which the source says pay off greatly with almost no exceptions: How do you define winning? What do you have to win? What do you need to win? What do we need to say? Expect six stakeholders to give different answers to the winning question.
- Step 5 — generate in small teams or solo, working across several unusual directions, with permission to fail and a target of large volume.
- Step 6 — converge after roughly two cycles against the creative framework, prune legally, and narrow to about 10-15 candidates.
- Step 7 — test believability and the sound-fluency properties, then present in context.
- For ecommerce, steps 2 and 3 come from your own product specs plus reviews, support tickets and search queries rather than agency interviews — the inputs differ, the sequence does not.
Caveat: The full agency process takes weeks and multiple people; small operators must compress it without skipping the sequencing.
Caveat: Timing bands such as two cycles in two and a half weeks assume team members working several projects at once.

Tags: brand, naming, positioning, process, customer-research, creative-strategy, product-page
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Discipline, Not Talent: Managing Creative Contributors
Use when: Founders or operators trying to scale creative output beyond themselves for naming, ad creative, PDP copy or content.
- Reframe the belief: the source, whose process is highly systematized, describes creativity as process-driven and therefore teachable, not as raw talent — Leonardo da Vinci's method is cited as persistence and tenacity rather than a single burst.
- Change how you respond to submissions: the source's rule as creative director is to speculate on a list rather than evaluate it, and his team knows that. If people get smacked down on their first bad idea, they stop producing useful ideas.
- Notice what encouragement means literally — the source points out its root is courage, and asks whether you are actually giving people room to flow rather than leading every move.
- Watch the difference between leading and managing, and between evaluating and encouraging, as the source's diagnosis of the habitual management mistake.
- Protect the generation phase from judgment by structure, not willpower: separate the dreaming, planning and judging stages the way Disney's three-room practice is described, so judgment happens at a scheduled point rather than in the moment of ideation.
