- Invite the person who produced the rejected idea to solve the constraint (cost, legal availability) rather than dropping the idea.
Caveat: These are the source's management prescriptions and personal practice, not measured team-performance results.
Caveat: Small teams may not be able to isolate phases in separate people; they still need to isolate them in time.

Tags: team-management, creative-strategy, ideation, process, leadership, content
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Use an Inconvenient Outside Source to Force New Connections
Use when: A headline, PDP narrative, campaign angle or product story feels like generic category copy and you need a genuinely different perspective.
- Choose genuinely unfamiliar material for the category — a couple of magazines you never read, or a book on a subject unrelated to your product such as Thomas Edison.
- Spend about 30 minutes reading only for connections that could move into the piece, suspending logic and evaluation during that window.
- The source's claim, from his own practice, is that roughly 30% of the time this yields a new perspective, spin or insight for the work — treat that as his experience, not a measured rate.
- This supports the source's broader principle of synchronicity: creativity comes from connecting seemingly irrelevant things. Impossible Burger is the cited case — deliberately not a natural-foods-style name that would blend into the Whole Foods shelf.
- Extend the same move to the natural world, which the source sometimes uses as a direct source: for 'lightness' he looked at what makes an airplane lift, and at Greek roots and expressions for feeling light.
- Ecommerce application: before finalizing a product narrative or ad hook, read one industry-adjacent and one completely unrelated publication or community, and mine them for a metaphor or angle you can justify with your real product facts.
- Make the borrowed angle serve the product truth, not replace it — a clever reference that overstates the product breaks believability.
Caveat: The 30% hit rate is the source's own estimate from practice.
Caveat: Borrowed metaphors must be checked against what your product actually does before use in claims.

Tags: creative-strategy, copywriting, content, product-page, ad-creative, research
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Judging names is the scarce skill, not generating them
Use when: When naming a new product, brand line, or store, or when you have a longlist of candidates and need to pick the one to launch with.
- Generate a wide longlist of candidate names with AI or a group brainstorm; the naming expert behind BlackBerry, Febreze and Swiffer notes that anyone can now generate 200-300 names with AI tools, so generation is no longer the bottleneck.
- Instead of spending your time generating more, invest it in building a judgment framework: define what 'right' means for this product before you evaluate, so the criteria are not reverse-engineered from whichever names you happen to like.
- Score each candidate against that framework rather than arguing pairwise. The source's point is that separating the right names from the good and the okay names is where the value now sits.
- Apply the same judgment to each candidate: does it fit the product category, the positioning line you intend, and the customer's vocabulary?
- Keep the rejected-but-good names in a file; they may suit a future SKU or sub-brand.
Caveat: The source runs a naming firm whose competitive advantage is proprietary data and software integrated with AI; a small brand will not replicate that dataset, so allocate more weight to your own category and customer knowledge.

Tags: brand, naming, product-selection, positioning, ai-tools, decision-framework
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Budget and scope for professional naming work
Use when: When planning budget, scope, or an in-house vs agency decision for a new brand, product line, or flagship SKU.
- Treat these numbers as a directional benchmark from one firm, not a market rate. The source describes most projects landing between a low of about $75k and a high of about $150k, with big corporate work up to roughly $200k.
- Expect the deliverable to extend beyond the name: the source describes helping with nomenclature (how the product is defined), a positioning line, and a short brand story.
- Ask about tracking research: the firm runs small quantitative studies monitoring how the name lands over roughly 90-120 days after launch.
- If your budget cannot support this tier, decide explicitly which parts you can do yourself (name generation, positioning line) and where you will buy expertise (qualitative validation, tracking).
- Convert the fee into a per-year cost if the name will be used for years, and compare it against the cost of a later rename: packaging, listings, domain, ad creative, and accumulated search equity.
Caveat: These are one firm's 2026-era numbers for consulting projects; costs vary widely by region, scope and firm size.
Caveat: Small ecommerce brands rarely need this spend for every SKU; reserve it for the flagship or umbrella brand.

Tags: brand, naming, budgeting, agency-management, positioning
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Name launches usually include a post-launch tracking study
Use when: When you have just launched or rebranded and want to know whether the new name is gaining traction before locking in packaging, listings and ad spend.
- Define a small set of measurable signals before launch: unaided and aided recall of the name, correct association with the product category, and how easily it is repeated or spelled when someone hears it.
- Run a lightweight survey at launch and again at roughly 90-120 days; the source describes exactly this cadence as typical tracking research.
- Include a practical commerce signal: can people find the store by searching the name, and does the paid-search auction for the name show rising volume?
- Watch for name-specific failure modes: misspellings in search queries, voice-assistant mishearing, and people confusing the name with an existing brand.
- Decide in advance what result triggers action: if recall and category association do not improve materially by the second wave, weigh the cost of a repositioning line versus a full rename.
- Feed findings back into the positioning line and short brand story rather than changing the name reflexively.
Caveat: The source describes the practice but gives no numeric thresholds; choose your own meaningful improvement level.
Caveat: Small samples can be noisy, so treat the second wave as directional.

Tags: brand, naming, measurement, post-launch, customer-research, positioning
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Use 'approximate thinking' to unlock team creativity
Use when: When briefing your team or an agency on ad creative, product naming, landing-page concepts, or any ideation session where output has become predictable.
- Draw the spectrum: on one end bizarre, absurd or even illegal ideas; on the other, safe workable ideas. Write 'approximate thinking' in the middle and show it to your team.
- Explain the rule: you may move from bizarre through approximate, then stop there. Do not force the jump to finished, safe ideas.
- Present the approximate set deliberately unfinished: not baked out, not full, possibly bad. The source states that giving people permission to work at this stage makes creativity spike.
- As the operator, resist killing an idea because it is not yet a campaign. Ask instead: what would have to be true for this to work?
- Select a small number of approximate ideas to develop further into ads, PDP copy, or bundles, and keep the bizarre end visible so the safe end does not become the whole range.
- Repeat the exercise each time the output gets stale rather than only once.
Caveat: This is the source's own coaching device for creative teams, not a documented experiment; expect to sift a lot of unusable material.
Caveat: Not every category can absorb a bizarre concept, and in regulated categories legal review still applies.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, ideation, acquisition, team-process, ad-creative
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## Give AI the generation work, keep the judgment in-house
Use when: When deciding how far to automate naming, product-copy generation, or creative concepts, and what capability to keep human.
- Use AI to widen the funnel cheaply: the source notes anyone can now generate 200-300 names with assistants such as ChatGPT or Claude. Treat that output as raw material, not decisions.
- Build the judgment layer yourself: define the criteria, the category conventions, and the customer vocabulary that a name or concept must satisfy.
- Invest in the data that makes judgment repeatable: the source firm spent about 16 months integrating AI into a database it had already invested in. For a retailer, that may be search-query logs, review language and returns reasons rather than a naming database.
- Note the source's observation that six of roughly twelve or thirteen active projects were AI-driven, i.e. even an established naming practice is restructuring around the technology.
- Assign a single owner to final judgment so AI output does not get adopted by whoever generated the most options.
Caveat: The 16-month integration and the project mix are one firm's experience, not a productivity benchmark.
Caveat: AI output may reproduce existing or trademarked names; run clearance checks before committing to anything.

Tags: brand, naming, ai-tools, decision-framework, positioning, workflow
Source: We named a billion dollar “startup” with the guy that named BlackBerry, Febreeze and Swiffer.docx

---

## The Daily 3: Cut to Three Needle-Movers
Use when: Weekly planning for a lean ecommerce team or solo operator overwhelmed by merchandising, marketing, and ops tasks.
- Each morning (or the night before), list everything you could do across your store — product pages, ads, inventory, email, support.
- Force-rank and select only 3 tasks that most move the needle (e.g. fix the top PDP, launch the restock email, resolve a checkout drop-off).
- Work top to bottom through those 3 before touching anything else.
- Say no to the other 97% deliberately; defer, delegate, or delete them.
- Review the pattern over weeks so recurring 'yes' items reflect your real priorities, not noise.
Caveat: Source is the presenter's personal operating rule, not a proven system for all teams.
Caveat: Some urgent ops issues (stockouts, payment failures) may need same-day handling outside the 3.

Tags: brand, simplicity, focus, operations, productivity, prioritization
Source: the psychology of building a brand everyone loves.docx

---

## "Audience Comes Last": Build to Your Taste, Not Market Guessing
Use when: Designing brand identity, packaging, creative direction, or new products where you're tempted to poll customers first.
- Separate the creation phase from the validation phase: block the audience out while conceiving the brand, product, or creative.
- Lean on your own taste, intuition, and judgment as the core input rather than surveys or focus groups (the Rick Rubin / Steve Jobs principle the presenter cites).
- Trust the signal from your inner voice to distill to one simple idea.
- After creating, take it to market where real behavior (clicks, add-to-cart, repeat orders) is the test.
- Note the source's framing: the audience only knows what has come before, so customers tend to ask for a faster horse rather than something genuinely new.
Caveat: This concerns the creative act; it is not a license to skip customer research, pricing tests, or post-launch data.
Caveat: The presenter attributes this to Rubin and Jobs — treat as their philosophy, not a validated universal method.

Tags: brand, creative-strategy, positioning, customer-research, product-development, authenticity
Source: the psychology of building a brand everyone loves.docx

---

## Let Weirdness Become the Brand's Authentic Edge
Use when: Positioning a store in a crowded category where every brand sounds and looks the same.
- Identify the beliefs, niche interests, or approaches you hold that others in your category call odd or dismiss.
- Ignore critics who say it's too niche or nobody cares; the source kept his 'soulful' angle despite pushback.
- Lean that unfair advantage into brand voice, product curation, and creative instead of flattening to category norms.
- Use the search through your own weirdness to find what is genuinely you — the presenter says authenticity comes from that exploration.
- Express it consistently across PDP copy, packaging, and social so it reads as a coherent brand, not a gimmick.
Caveat: Distinctiveness can narrow appeal; ensure the core offer still solves a clear customer problem.
Caveat: Authenticity claims are the presenter's philosophy, not a measured conversion driver.

Tags: brand, positioning, authenticity, differentiation, creative-strategy, voice
Source: the psychology of building a brand everyone loves.docx

---

## Obsess Over the Craft, Not the Metrics
Use when: Allocating effort between chasing metrics (views, follower counts) versus improving the actual product, photos, or content quality.
- Redirect effort from metric-chasing to mastering the craft: cinematography, lighting, copy, product quality.
- Go all-in on each project rather than spreading thin — the source cites Mr. Beast 'betting the farm' each video.
- Aim to solve the problem three times deeper than competitors; different solutions follow from deeper work.
- Pick a single format on a single platform and try to make it best-in-world rather than dabbling across all of them (the presenter says dabbling gets lost in the noise).
- Example cited: hand-drawing concept images on LinkedIn out of genuine obsession led to ~110,000 followers in a month — treat this as an anecdote, not a target.
Caveat: Obsession compounding is the presenter's analogy, not a linear formula with verified numbers.
Caveat: The Mr. Beast and LinkedIn follower results are case examples, not promises.

Tags: brand, craft, obsession, content-strategy, organic-content, differentiation
Source: the psychology of building a brand everyone loves.docx

---

## Insanely Simple: Strip to One Idea
Use when: Simplifying a cluttered PDP, navigation, product line, or ad concept that has too many competing messages.
- Iterate toward fewer elements: the source recounts iPhone prototypes going from three buttons to one to a buttonless interface under Jobs.
- Apply the same to messaging — Apple's ads made a simple, memorable comparison rather than an exhaustive feature list.
- Distill your product or store down to one simple thing customers can remember.
- Cut feature creep and competing CTAs; each extra element dilutes the signal.
- Note the Ford framing: customers didn't ask for a car, they asked for a faster horse — so simplicity often means leading, not polling.
Caveat: Simplification can strip genuinely useful features; test whether customers still complete the task.
Caveat: The Apple/Jobs story is illustrative, not a documented playbook you can copy directly.

Tags: brand, simplicity, ux, product-design, messaging, product-page
Source: the psychology of building a brand everyone loves.docx

---

## Choose One Format on One Platform
Use when: Deciding which organic channel to build for a store instead of posting thinly across X, LinkedIn, YouTube, and Instagram.
- Pick one platform and one format (e.g. a weekly founder video, a daily concept image, one recurring email).
- Commit to making that single format the best in the world rather than spreading effort.
- Refuse to treat it as a side hobby or something to dabble in — the presenter says that path gets lost in the noise.
- Obsess over the format's craft, not the follower count.
- Let the format compound: the source's hand-drawn concept images shared thousands of times and grew LinkedIn by ~110k in a month (an anecdote, not a benchmark).
Caveat: Single-platform focus trades reach for depth; revisit the choice only after sufficient reps.
Caveat: The follower-growth example is a case result, not a repeatable promise.

Tags: brand, content-strategy, organic-content, platform-focus, creative-strategy, distribution
Source: the psychology of building a brand everyone loves.docx

---

## Go Deep, Not Wide: Shape the Company's Character
Use when: Deciding whether to broaden a product line or deepen an existing category and internal standard of quality.
- Bias toward depth: improve the core product, sourcing, or experience rather than adding categories.
- Adopt the source's framing of using 'pain and suffering' deliberately — i.e. embrace hard refinements that train the character of the company.
- Treat the goal as building an icon over decades, not chasing the next product launch.
- Apply the principle to team standards: obsess over craft quality so the brand's depth shows in every touchpoint.
- Remember the closing line: icons aren't born, they're built.
Caveat: Depth can mean slower diversification; weigh against the realities of your category and cash flow.
Caveat: The 'pain and suffering' language is the presenter's rhetorical framing, not an operating metric.

Tags: brand, depth, product-strategy, quality, company-culture, long-term
Source: the psychology of building a brand everyone loves.docx
